# Research Reference — Human Practice
269 practices from ten lenses, each with the mechanism that makes it work. The mechanism matters more than the label: a rule whose mechanism you cannot state is a rule you cannot safely modify for a different kind of writer.

## Voice, POV, psychic distance, and keeping characters distinct

**The five-level psychic distance ladder, used as a gear system** · <sub>load-bearing · John Gardner, The Art of Fiction (1983/84); the numbered examples are his verbatim.</sub>

Gardner's five graded openings, quoted in The Art of Fiction: (1) "It was winter of the year 1853. A
large man stepped out of a doorway." (2) "Henry J. Warburton had never much cared for snowstorms."
(3) "Henry hated snowstorms." (4) "God how he hated these damn snowstorms." (5) "Snow. Under your
collar, down inside your shoes, freezing and plugging up your miserable soul..." Operationally: tag
every paragraph of a scene 1-5 in the margin. A conventional scene shape enters at 2-3, descends to
4-5 at the point of maximum pressure, and withdraws to 2-3 to exit. Level 1 is for chapter openings,
time jumps, and any information the character does not possess. Level 5 cannot be sustained for more
than a page or two without the reader losing spatial orientation.

*Mechanism:* The reader is continuously maintaining a model of where the observing consciousness is standing
relative to the character. That model is cheap to maintain while it is stable and expensive to
rebuild when it changes. Distance is also the writer's only instrument for compression: level 1-2
can cover a year in a sentence, level 5 covers four seconds. A writer who cannot move the gear is
condemned to render everything at the same rate, which is why 'always stay deep' produces books that
are simultaneously claustrophobic and slow.

**Gardner's actual rule is 'no inexplicable shift,' not 'no shift'** · <sub>load-bearing · John Gardner, The Art of Fiction; widely misreported in workshop culture as 'stay in deep POV.'</sub>

Gardner's warning is that "a piece of fiction containing sudden and inexplicable shifts in psychic
distance looks amateur and tends to drive the reader away," and that careless shifts are
distracting. He demonstrates the fault with a passage that lurches between close and distant within
one paragraph. The operational rule is not 'never change level' but: change on a boundary
(paragraph, white space, scene break) or on a motivated trigger (a shock, a memory, a sensory
intrusion), and prefer moving one level at a time. A two-level jump needs a break or a reason. A
three-level jump almost always needs both.

*Mechanism:* An unmotivated shift makes the narrator visible as a mechanism — the reader briefly perceives an
author choosing rather than a world existing, which is precisely the puncture in the 'vivid and
continuous dream.' A motivated shift does the opposite: the reader attributes the movement to the
story's pressure rather than the writer's hand, so the same technical event reads as intensity
instead of clumsiness.

**Free indirect discourse as a mechanical transformation** · <sub>load-bearing · Flaubert (Madame Bovary) and Austen are the canonical practitioners; Roy Pascal named the 'dual voice' (The Dual Voice, 1977); Dorrit Cohn calls it 'narrated monologue'; James Wood built How Fiction Works around it and credits Flaubert as founder.</sub>

Take the character's direct thought, then: delete the quotation marks, delete the 'he thought' tag,
shift the pronoun to third person, back-shift the tense one step, and keep everything else — the
diction, the evaluative adjectives, the exclamations, the sentence fragments, the character's own
deictics. "'God, what an idiot I am,' she thought" becomes "God, what an idiot she was." The
decision points are the deictics: keeping 'tomorrow' and 'here' pulls the reader further inside;
converting to 'the next day' and 'there' restores narratorial distance. That single choice is a
fine-grained volume knob on irony.

*Mechanism:* Removing the reporting frame removes a processing step and, more importantly, removes the guarantee
of separation between narrator and character. Two consciousnesses now occupy one sentence, so the
reader can simultaneously inhabit the character's judgment and appraise it. This is why FID is the
only technique that gives sympathy and irony at the same time — an omniscient narrator judging a
character forces the reader to take a side; FID lets the reader hold both.

**The Uncle Charles Principle: infect the action sentences, not just the thoughts** · <sub>load-bearing · Hugh Kenner, Joyce's Voices (1978), naming the technique after A Portrait of the Artist as a Young Man.</sub>

Kenner's observation on Joyce's "Every morning, therefore, Uncle Charles repaired to his outhouse":
the narration of external action, not just reported thought, takes the character's idiom. 'Repaired'
is not Joyce's word, it is Uncle Charles's. Operationally: in any close-third section, plant one or
two register-marked words per page in sentences that are purely narrative report — verbs of motion,
the way objects and other people are named, the choice of 'lady friend' over 'girlfriend,'
'commenced' over 'started.' Most writers restrict FID to interiority and leave the action lines in
house style.

*Mechanism:* Readers infer speaker identity from register far faster and more reliably than from content — a
single marked lexical item retroactively re-tags the surrounding paragraph. And because the infected
word appears in ostensibly neutral report, the reader experiences the character's worldview as the
texture of reality rather than as an opinion being expressed, which is far harder to resist.

**Choose consciously among Cohn's three modes of rendering mind** · <sub>strong · Dorrit Cohn, Transparent Minds: Narrative Modes for Presenting Consciousness in Fiction (1978).</sub>

Psycho-narration (the narrator summarizes and analyzes the character's mental state in the
narrator's own idiom), quoted monologue (direct thought, verbatim, the character's idiom), and
narrated monologue/FID (the character's idiom in third-person past). Operationally: for each
interior passage decide which of the three you are in, and note that psycho-narration is the only
one that can be more articulate than the character. For a character with poor self-knowledge or low
verbal facility, run psycho-narration for the analysis and FID for the felt experience; do not force
everything through the character's vocabulary.

*Mechanism:* Each mode carries a different truth warrant. Quoted monologue caps the prose at the character's
articulacy and grants no external knowledge; psycho-narration grants the narrator knowledge the
character lacks (the unconscious, the pattern, the self-deception); FID splits the difference.
Writers who believe 'deep POV' means only the character's words end up unable to write inarticulate
characters at all, or write inarticulate characters who implausibly produce lyric prose.

**Separate who sees from who speaks** · <sub>strong · Gérard Genette, Narrative Discourse (Fr. 1972, Eng. 1980); his three types are zero, internal, and external focalization.</sub>

Genette replaced 'point of view' with two independent questions: focalization (whose perception
organizes this passage) and voice (who is narrating). Diagnostic use: when POV feels muddy, ask them
separately. If the perceptions are the character's but the diction is the house style, you have a
voice failure disguised as a POV success — the classic symptom of interchangeable POVs. If the
diction is the character's but the perceptions are the author's (the character notices exactly what
the plot requires), you have a focalization failure, which reads as characters being weirdly
convenient.

*Mechanism:* 'POV' collapses two orthogonal controls into one slider, so writers who feel something is wrong
reach for the wrong lever. Splitting them gives a two-dimensional control surface and makes the two
most common failures distinguishable, and therefore fixable by different means.

**Build idiolect on six independent axes, not one** · <sub>load-bearing · Composite of practitioner method; the salience axis is explicitly Gardner's and Robert Olen Butler's; the syntax axis is central to Le Guin's Steering the Craft.</sub>

For each POV, specify: (1) SYNTAX — mean sentence length and its variance, parataxis vs. hypotaxis,
where the subordinate clause sits, whether they front or trail their qualifiers; (2) LEXICON —
register, etymological register, domain jargon; (3) SALIENCE — what they notice first entering a
room, and in what order; (4) THE NEGATIVE — what they will not say, name, or look at; (5)
CHARACTERISTIC ERROR — the systematic wrongness; (6) CADENCE — rhythm, where the stresses fall, how
sentences end. A voice differentiated on fewer than four of these will collapse.

*Mechanism:* Every axis is independently variable, so n axes give combinatorial distinctness, whereas the common
approach (one axis, usually lexicon: 'she swears, he's formal') gives a difference the writer must
consciously remember to apply — and conscious application fails around chapter fourteen, and fails
hardest in high-emotion scenes where the writer is attending to plot. Multi-axis voices survive
because syntax and salience are structural: they change what sentences are possible, not what words
get sprinkled on.

**Assign each POV a metaphor source domain and enforce it** · <sub>load-bearing · Practitioner consensus; the underlying tenor/vehicle terminology is I. A. Richards, The Philosophy of Rhetoric (1936).</sub>

No character may reach for a comparison outside their own stored experience. Write the domain down:
the farmer compares weather to livestock and debt; the surgeon compares a marriage to a graft that
won't take; the ex-soldier compares a silence to a cleared field. Then audit every simile and
metaphor in that POV's sections against the list. Violations are usually the author's own metaphor
bank leaking.

*Mechanism:* Metaphor is a direct readout of what is in a mind's long-term memory — the tenor is given by the
scene, but the vehicle can only come from the perceiver. So a metaphor identifies the perceiver more
precisely than any amount of stated characterization, and does it inside a sentence that is
ostensibly about something else. This is the single highest-yield lever for separating close-third
POVs, and the most commonly violated, because writers experience their own figurative habits as
neutral.

**The negative lexicon — what the character will not say** · <sub>strong · Practitioner technique; consistent with Bakhtin's account of double-voiced discourse and with Hemingway's iceberg principle (Death in the Afternoon, 1932).</sub>

For each POV, a written list of forbidden words and unnameable subjects: the word for the dead
child, the ex-husband's first name, any word for their own body, all profanity, all abstraction, the
word 'love.' Enforce it absolutely, including in interior monologue. Where the concept is
unavoidable, the character must circle it — pronoun it, euphemize it, change the subject mid-
sentence.

*Mechanism:* Presence-features are copyable across characters (anyone can be given slang), but a consistent,
systematic gap is nearly impossible to give to two characters without it reading as the same
character. More importantly the reader detects the shape of an absence and reads it as psychology
rather than style — the avoidance itself becomes the characterization, and it generates subtext
automatically without the writer having to invent any.

**The characteristic error** · <sub>strong · Practitioner technique with deep roots — Dickens, Austen (Miss Bates, Mr. Collins), Joyce; Bakhtin theorizes it as the character's discourse resisting the author's.</sub>

Give each voice exactly one systematic wrongness, applied consistently: a misused idiom ('for all
intensive purposes'), a hypercorrection ('between you and I'), false precision ('about eleven-forty,
maybe eleven-forty-two'), a chronic overqualification, a category confusion, or a factual belief the
book quietly demonstrates is untrue. It must be systematic, not random, and it must never be
commented on by the narration.

*Mechanism:* Errors are low-frequency, high-information events: one occurrence carries more identifying signal
than a page of neutral prose, because the reader must model a specific mind to explain it. They are
also nearly impossible to duplicate across characters by accident. Third and most important: an
error signals a mind operating independently of the author's control — the strongest available cue
that this character is not the author's puppet.

**Bakhtin's polyphony test: does the author lose an argument on the page** · <sub>load-bearing · Mikhail Bakhtin, Problems of Dostoevsky's Poetics (1929/1963).</sub>

Bakhtin's criterion is that Dostoevsky's heroes are "not only objects of authorial discourse but
also subjects of their own directly signifying discourse," and the author's worldview is present as
one participant in the great dialogue with "no more capacity for direct signification than any other
voice." Working test: does any POV character hold a position the book cannot refute? Is the most
persuasive paragraph in the novel spoken by someone the author disagrees with? Can you state what
each character would say about the book's ending, in their own terms, without any of them being
obviously wrong?

*Mechanism:* Readers detect the authorial thumb on the scale below conscious awareness and it produces the
specific flatness of the straw-man antagonist. Polyphony is also the practical source of plot
pressure: if every character's position is genuinely defensible, conflict cannot be resolved by
anyone being right, so it must be resolved by events and cost — which is what makes an ending feel
earned rather than adjudicated.

**Heteroglossia: import a real social language per POV** · <sub>strong · Mikhail Bakhtin, 'Discourse in the Novel' (in The Dialogic Imagination, 1934-35).</sub>

Bakhtin's point is that the novel's raw material is social languages — professional jargon,
generational speech, class dialects, the languages of institutions — not individual quirks. So
differentiate by giving each POV an actual institutional language they think inside: actuarial risk,
liturgy, competitive swimming, criminal procedure, restaurant kitchens, nursing triage. Then have
them apply it to non-domain material: the character who assesses a lover the way she assesses a
claim.

*Mechanism:* An institution supplies a complete, self-consistent package — lexicon, characteristic syntax, value
hierarchy, and a way of segmenting the world — for free. This means the voice generates correct new
sentences under pressure without the writer having to remember rules, which is exactly where tic-
based voices fail. It also gives the character a value system that is not the author's, which is the
practical route to polyphony.

**The tag-strip test (the primary editorial diagnostic)** · <sub>load-bearing · Standard developmental-editing practice; widely taught in editorial training and in multi-POV craft discussion.</sub>

Produce a version of the manuscript with all chapter headings, POV labels, character names, and
dialogue attributions removed or replaced with X. Give it to a reader who knows the book and ask
them to identify who is narrating each section and who is speaking each line. Then do the harder
version on yourself: extract twenty 150-word blocks of interior narration from different POVs,
shuffle them, and sort them cold a week later. Under 80% correct means the voices are not distinct.

*Mechanism:* The author reads with the label attached and cannot suppress it; the sensation of distinctness is
being supplied by the author's own knowledge of who is speaking, not by the text. Removing the label
removes the confound. This is why writers are systematically overconfident about voice
differentiation and simultaneously accurate about it in other people's books.

**The same-scene stress test** · <sub>strong · Common workshop exercise; structurally identical in intent to Le Guin's 'The Old Woman' exercise in Steering the Craft, which holds content constant while varying person and tense.</sub>

Take one event — a specific argument, a funeral, a car breaking down — and write it at 300-500 words
from each POV in turn, same room, same three minutes, same referents. Compare. If the versions
differ mainly in pronouns and stated opinions, the voices are identical and the apparent
distinctness in the draft is an artifact of the characters being in different places doing different
things.

*Mechanism:* In a normal draft, subject matter does the differentiating work: a chapter about a hospital reads
different from a chapter about a boat regardless of voice. Holding the referents constant strips
that confound out and exposes the actual idiolect. It also forces the salience axis into view — two
people in the same room noticing different things is the purest demonstration of distinct
consciousness available.

**The register floor: differentiate more under stress, not less** · <sub>strong · Practitioner technique; the underlying observation about regression and defensive style is clinical rather than literary.</sub>

Specify for each character both a ceiling and a floor of register, and what happens to their
language when frightened, drunk, grieving, or enraged. One character loses syntax and goes to
fragments; one becomes more formal and more precise as a defense; one goes silent and the narration
goes to pure sensory data; one starts telling jokes; one reverts to a first language or a childhood
idiom. Write the rule down before you write the crisis scenes.

*Mechanism:* High-emotion scenes are where voices homogenize, because the writer's attention is consumed by plot
and by their own arousal, and the default output is generic panicked prose — short sentences,
hammering verbs, heartbeat. A pre-specified stress behavior converts the writer's cognitive load
problem into a lookup. And psychologically it is true: linguistic defense mechanisms are individual,
so characters really should diverge under pressure, not converge.

**Second-order voice: each POV's theory of other people** · <sub>strong · Implicit in Bakhtin; explicit in the practice of Henry James (the 'lucid reflector' of his Prefaces) and in Ishiguro.</sub>

Specify what each POV assumes about other people's motives by default — that they are frightened,
that they want something, that they are performing, that they are stupid, that they are kind. Then
let it govern how that POV reports gesture and dialogue. The same line of dialogue gets narrated as
'she offered' by one POV and 'she countered' by another; the same pause is read as thinking or as
lying.

*Mechanism:* This changes the content of scenes, not just their diction, which makes it the deepest and least
evadable form of differentiation — a reader can be told two characters are different, but a reader
who is shown that two characters saw a different event has no choice. It is also the operational
core of what Bakhtin means by polyphony: the characters are not merely speaking differently, they
are interpreting differently, and the book does not tell you which interpretation is correct.

**Dialogue subtext: nobody answers the question they were asked** · <sub>load-bearing · Harold Pinter; Chekhov; Charles Baxter's Burning Down the House discusses the mechanics of what dialogue conceals; Robert McKee's Story formalizes text/subtext for screen.</sub>

Write the scene once with everyone saying exactly what they mean and want. Then rewrite so that no
line is a direct answer: every line is a deflection, a bid, a counter-offer, a change of subject, or
an attack on a different flank. Keep the original as a hidden document — it is the scene's spine and
you must know it, but it does not appear. A rough working target: fewer than one in three direct
questions receives a direct answer.

*Mechanism:* Readers infer for pleasure and resent having inference done for them; supplying the intent alongside
the statement removes the reader's job. Second mechanism: in real speech the ratio of stated intent
to actual intent is very low, so on-the-nose dialogue registers as false even to readers who cannot
articulate why. Third: an evasion tells the reader what the character wants AND what they are afraid
of, which is twice the information of a direct statement.

**The eloquent silence requires an established expected response** · <sub>strong · Hemingway's iceberg theory (Death in the Afternoon, 1932); Pinter's pauses; Raymond Carver.</sub>

The strongest reply is often no reply — a change of subject, a physical action, a look out the
window. But the silence only works if the reader can compute the missing line. So the technique has
two steps: first establish, elsewhere in the book or earlier in the scene, what the expected answer
is; then withhold it. A silence in a vacuum reads as the writer skipping a beat.

*Mechanism:* Absence carries information only when it is a marked absence against a known expectation — the same
reason a rest in music is only a rest inside an established meter. The reader supplies the withheld
line themselves, and material the reader generates is held more strongly and felt more personally
than material they are given, which is the entire economic argument for the iceberg.

**Attribution mechanics: 'said' is invisible, which is both its use and its limit** · <sub>strong · Elmore Leonard, '10 Rules of Writing' (rules on 'said' and on adverbs); the underlying frequency/invisibility account is standard psycholinguistics.</sub>

'Said' is bleached and processed near-punctuationally, so it is the correct default. But the
received rule stops too early. Three refinements: (1) tag POSITION is a rhythm control — 'he said'
placed mid-sentence inserts a beat that changes how the second half of the line lands, and Elmore
Leonard used this constantly; (2) in a well-differentiated two-hander you can drop tags entirely for
a page, and the fact that you can is itself the proof that the voices work — treat tag-free runs as
a test, not just a style; (3) action beats are over-prescribed as a tag substitute — a beat is a
camera cut and costs the reader more attention than 'said,' so use beats when you want the pause,
not merely to avoid repetition.

*Mechanism:* Frequency drives invisibility: high-frequency function-like words are processed without conscious
attention. Said-bookisms ('he ejaculated,' 'she opined') are low-frequency content words and
therefore surface, pulling attention off the line to the mechanism reporting it. Adverbs in tags
fail for a different reason: they instruct the reader how to read a line they have already read,
forcing a silent re-read and simultaneously advertising that the line failed to carry its own tone.

**Render dialect by syntax, idiom and rhythm; ration respelling** · <sub>contested · John Dufresne, The Lie That Tells a Truth; the Columbia Guide to Standard American English. Contested by the counter-case of Zora Neale Hurston, whose respellings in Their Eyes Were Watching God do encode genuine phonological features of Southern American English and AAVE, and who was writing from inside the speech community as a trained folklorist.</sub>

Carry dialect in word order, aspect and tense marking, idiom, figures of speech, and vocabulary
indigenous to the locale, rather than in apostrophes and phonetic respelling. John Dufresne, citing
the Columbia Guide to Standard American English, argues that dialect belongs in 'rhythm of the
prose, by the syntax, the diction, idioms and figures of speech.' Two hard rules: never respell only
the low-status speaker while the high-status speaker gets standard orthography; and never use eye
dialect proper — spellings like 'sez,' 'wuz,' 'enuff' that encode no pronunciation difference at
all.

*Mechanism:* Eye dialect is by definition phonetically empty — 'sez' and 'says' are pronounced identically — so
its entire information content is social marking, and readers correctly decode it as the narrator's
condescension toward the speaker. Heavy respelling also forces subvocalization and decoding, which
slows reading and breaks the fictional dream at exactly the moment the character is trying to be
heard. Syntax and idiom, by contrast, are processed at normal speed and carry real linguistic
difference.

**Typography as characterization: the italics decision** · <sub>strong · Junot Díaz, 'The Italic War' (StoryWorlds); Drown and The Brief Wondrous Life of Oscar Wao.</sub>

Whether to italicize non-English words is a characterization decision, not a copyediting one. Junot
Díaz refuses italics for Spanish in the Yunior books on the grounds that his narrators do not think
in italics; Spanish is not foreign to them, so marking it foreign would falsify the narrating
consciousness. Same logic applies to interior thought: italicizing every thought marks it as an
insert into a narration that is not the character's, which fights against close third.

*Mechanism:* Typographic marking encodes a point of view — it says 'this is other' and implicitly locates the
norm elsewhere, usually with a presumed monolingual reader rather than with the narrator. So the
typography contradicts the voice, and readers register the contradiction as a slight loss of
conviction without being able to name it.

**Build unreliability on Phelan's axes, and fail on only one or two** · <sub>strong · James Phelan, Living to Tell about It (2005); building on Wayne C. Booth, The Rhetoric of Fiction (1961), which coined 'unreliable narrator' and defined it against the implied author's norms.</sub>

Phelan's six kinds sort into two families across three axes. Axes: reporting (facts and events),
reading (knowledge and perception), regarding (ethics and evaluation). Families: 'mis-' (the
narrator is wrong) and 'under-' (the narrator is insufficient). So: misreporting, misreading,
misregarding; underreporting, underreading, underregarding. Decide explicitly which one or two your
narrator commits and hold the others reliable. Underreporting — the narrator tells the truth but not
enough of it — is the most usable and the least used; misregarding (accurate facts, deformed ethics)
is the engine of Nabokov's Humbert and Ishiguro's Stevens.

*Mechanism:* The reader reconstructs the real story by triangulating from a channel they can partly trust. If the
narrator fails on all three axes simultaneously there is no fixed point, and the reader stops
detecting and starts disengaging — the book becomes noise rather than dramatic irony. Restricting
the failure to one axis gives the reader a stable instrument with a known bias, which is what makes
the game playable.

**Leak the truth through the observed layer, not the interpreted layer** · <sub>strong · Wayne C. Booth on the implied author; the technique is Ishiguro's central method in The Remains of the Day and Ford Madox Ford's in The Good Soldier.</sub>

An unreliable narrator controls interpretation but not observation. So put the counter-evidence in
what they report without understanding: dialogue quoted verbatim that the narrator misreads;
physical detail noticed and not explained; the narrator's own metaphors betraying what they deny; a
topic they evade at a consistent point; other characters' reactions that make sense only under the
true reading. Booth's corollary: the book must establish the implied author's norms independently —
through pattern, through what the plot rewards and punishes, through other characters — or the
reader has nothing to measure the narrator against.

*Mechanism:* Readers hold two representations at once — what the narrator says happened, and what they infer
happened — and the pleasure of unreliable narration is entirely in the gap between them. Verbatim
quotation is the cleanest leak because it is unmediated evidence smuggled through a mediating
consciousness: the narrator's frame is visible around it, so the reader can subtract the frame.

**Name the occasion of narration for every first-person voice** · <sub>strong · Practitioner method; the theoretical frame is Genette's narrating situation and Booth's implied audience.</sub>

For each first-person POV write one line: spoken or written or thought; to whom; when, relative to
the events; and why now. A deposition, a letter to a dead brother, a confession to a stranger on a
train, an unspoken address to a child not yet born, a diary, a story being told at a bar for the
fortieth time. Then honor it — a spoken narration has repetitions and self-corrections a written one
does not; a narration to a specific addressee omits what that addressee already knows.

*Mechanism:* Unspecified, the writer defaults to an unmarked present-tense-of-composition voice — nobody,
addressing nobody, for no reason — and that default is identical for every narrator, which is a
leading cause of first-person multi-POV sameness. Specifying the occasion also determines omission,
and omission is where voice lives most powerfully; a narrator addressing someone who already knows
the house will not describe the house.

**Know what each grammatical person costs before choosing it** · <sub>strong · Le Guin's Steering the Craft chapter 7 and the 'The Old Woman' exercise systematically works through these; the plural-first examples are the standard modern canon of the mode.</sub>

First person caps prose at the narrator's articulacy and raises the question 'why is this being
told'; a retrospective older narrator (Dickens, Ishiguro, Nabokov) buys back articulacy at the cost
of immediacy and adds the pressure of hindsight. Close third buys the ability to be more articulate
than the character while remaining inside them, via psycho-narration — which is why it is the modern
default and also why it homogenizes so easily. Plural first (Eugenides, The Virgin Suicides; Otsuka,
The Buddha in the Attic; Ferris, Then We Came to the End) makes the group the protagonist and
forbids individual interiority; it works when the subject is genuinely collective and fails the
moment the writer wants one person's inner life. Second person forces the reader into complicity and
cannot be sustained without a motivating occasion.

*Mechanism:* Each person implies a different unanswered question the reader carries: first — why are you telling
me this and to whom; third — who is this narrator and how do they know; we — who is inside the we
and who is excluded; you — who is doing this to me. A book that never answers its implied question
accumulates low-grade unease. A book that answers it early converts the question into an engine.

**Audition the voice before designing it** · <sub>useful · Robert Olen Butler, From Where You Dream (2005), ed. Janet Burroway; the character free-write is standard practitioner method.</sub>

Before drafting a POV, free-write 500-1000 words in that character's first person on material that
will never appear in the book — an argument about a parking space, a complaint about their sister,
an account of a meal. Do this in one sitting without revising. Then reread and extract the rules you
accidentally followed: sentence length, what got noticed, what got avoided. Butler's related
practice is 'dreamstorming' — generating from the unconscious, the 'white-hot center,' rather than
from an outline, and treating fiction as driven by the character's yearning rather than by the
writer's intention.

*Mechanism:* Voice is a language-production habit more than a design specification. Rules written in advance must
be consciously applied and conscious application degrades over a long draft; a groove established by
production runs automatically. The extraction step matters because it converts the accidental habit
into something you can check against later — you are documenting a voice you already have rather
than legislating one you don't.

**Read aloud to find the writer's own cadence and defeat it** · <sub>strong · Robert Frost, letters on 'the sound of sense' / 'sentence sounds' (1913-14); reading aloud is Le Guin's standing instruction throughout Steering the Craft.</sub>

Read every POV's sections aloud, ideally recorded. The homogenizing agent is the writer's own
prosody — a preferred sentence length, a habitual three-beat close, a favorite rhythm of clause-
clause-short-clause. Mark every place where two different characters produce the same music and
change one of them structurally (split a sentence, invert a clause, remove or add a conjunction),
not lexically.

*Mechanism:* Prosody is generated by the writer's own speech motor habits and is almost entirely invisible on the
page to the person who produced it — it becomes audible only when converted back to sound. This is
why the sameness that survives every lexical fix is nearly always rhythmic. Frost's 'sentence
sounds' is the same observation from the other side: the meaning-bearing tune of a sentence is heard
before its words are parsed.

**Distance-appropriate filtering, against the 'no filter words' orthodoxy** · <sub>contested · The orthodoxy is contemporary online craft culture and the 'deep POV' teaching tradition; the corrective is Gardner's ladder.</sub>

Deep-POV orthodoxy says delete every 'she saw,' 'he felt,' 'she noticed,' 'he realized.' The correct
rule is that filters are a distance control: they are wrong at level 5, correct at level 3, and
required at level 2 where the point is that we are watching a mind from slightly outside it. Audit
filters per scene against your intended distance curve rather than deleting globally. A useful proxy
metric: filter verbs per thousand words should fall as a scene descends and rise as it withdraws.

*Mechanism:* A filter verb inserts the act of perceiving between the reader and the percept, adding exactly one
degree of separation — that is a feature when you want separation. Eliminating all filters pins the
narration at maximum closeness for the whole book, which destroys the writer's ability to summarize,
to compress time, and to modulate intensity: with no distant baseline, the close passages have
nothing to be close relative to.

**Controlled omniscience is legitimate; 'never head-hop' is a scaffold, not a law** · <sub>contested · George Eliot, Tolstoy, Woolf as practice; the modern prohibition is a workshop simplification. Damon Galgut's The Promise (2021) is the most-cited recent demonstration that the mode still works.</sub>

Nineteenth-century and much twentieth-century practice moves between minds within a scene — Eliot in
Middlemarch, Tolstoy, Woolf in Mrs Dalloway and To the Lighthouse, and among moderns Franzen and
Galgut in The Promise. The technique requires a narrator with an audible identity of its own (a
diction, a set of opinions, a relationship to the reader) so that the transitions are performed by
someone rather than merely happening, plus a consistent transition device: a shared object, a sound,
a physical movement across the room. What is actually forbidden is the unsignaled and purposeless
slide.

*Mechanism:* The reader's discomfort with head-hopping is not caused by knowing two minds — it is caused by not
knowing who is presenting them. An identifiable narrator gives the movement an agent, so the reader
attributes the shift to a consciousness rather than to authorial carelessness. Modern omniscience
feels harder than it was because the FID default has trained readers to expect a self-effacing
narrator, which removes the very agent that licenses the move.

### Non-obvious findings — Voice, POV, psychic distance, and keeping characte

- Voice lives disproportionately in function words — pronouns, prepositions, auxiliaries, determiners
  — which is exactly the layer writers do not consciously manipulate. Burrows's Delta and the whole
  stylometry tradition are built on this. The practical inversion: the 'voice work' most writers do
  (slang, catchphrases, verbal tics) operates on content words, which is not the layer readers use
  to identify a speaker. This also explains why voices that feel distinct in a draft measure as
  identical.

- Gardner's rule is not 'stay in deep POV.' His words are that 'sudden and inexplicable shifts in
  psychic distance' look amateur — the operative word is inexplicable, not shift. The contemporary
  teaching of 'always deep' is a misreading that removes the writer's only compression instrument
  and produces books that are simultaneously airless and slow.

- Eye dialect carries zero phonetic information by definition. 'Sez' and 'says,' 'wuz' and 'was,' are
  pronounced identically; the respelling encodes nothing about how the character speaks. Its entire
  function is social marking, which is why readers decode it as contempt even when the writer
  intended affection.

- The Uncle Charles Principle applies to action sentences, not just thought. Kenner's example — 'Uncle
  Charles repaired to his outhouse' — is a sentence of pure external report carrying the character's
  idiom. Most writers confine free indirect discourse to interiority and leave narrative action in
  house style, which is precisely why their close third feels half-committed and why all their close
  thirds sound alike between the thought-passages.

- Under-reporting is the most usable form of unreliability and almost nobody uses it. Phelan's
  taxonomy has a whole 'under-' family — the narrator is accurate but insufficient — yet virtually
  all popular discussion of unreliable narration concerns narrators who lie. Ishiguro's Stevens
  mostly does not lie; he under-regards.

- Making two POV characters narrate the same three minutes is a stronger diagnostic than any character
  worksheet, because in a normal draft the differentiation is being done by subject matter, not by
  voice. Holding referents constant removes the confound that makes writers overconfident.

- The author cannot self-diagnose POV sameness by rereading, because they read with the label attached
  and cannot suppress their own knowledge of who is speaking. The sensation of distinctness is
  generated internally. Any real diagnostic must mechanically remove the labels.

- Italicizing foreign words is a point-of-view decision, not a copyediting one — Junot Díaz's argument
  is that his narrators do not think in italics, so italics would falsify the narrating
  consciousness. The same logic quietly indicts italicized interior thought inside close third.

- Absence differentiates better than presence. A list of words a character will never say is harder to
  accidentally duplicate across two characters than any list of words they do say, and it generates
  subtext for free. Most voice work is additive; the subtractive version is stronger and rarer.

- Bakhtin's polyphony has a brutally concrete working test: the author has to lose an argument on the
  page. If the most persuasive paragraph in the book belongs to someone the author agrees with, the
  book is monologic regardless of how many POVs it has. Multi-POV is not polyphony; it is often
  monologue distributed across bodies.

- Roy Pascal's 'dual voice' account of free indirect discourse arrived in English criticism in the
  1960s-70s, decades after Bakhtin had independently theorized double-voiced discourse in Russian in
  the late 1920s. Anglophone craft teaching inherited the weaker of the two accounts first, which is
  part of why FID is usually taught as a punctuation trick rather than as a way of putting two value
  systems in one sentence.

- Characters should diverge linguistically under stress, not converge. Draft crisis scenes homogenize
  because the writer's attention is on plot; but linguistic defense mechanisms are individual, so
  the high-emotion scenes are where differentiation should be most visible. Pre-specifying each
  character's stress behavior turns the hardest passages into a lookup.

- Action beats are over-prescribed as a substitute for 'said.' A beat is a camera cut and costs the
  reader more attention than the bleached verb it replaces — which is fine when you want the pause
  and wasteful when you were only avoiding repetition.

## Story architecture & structure

**The scene as a value-change with a locatable turning point** · <sub>load-bearing · Robert McKee, Story (1997) — the scene defined as value-change and turning points as action-or-revelation. Shawn Coyne's Story Grid formalizes it as a per-scene value spreadsheet.</sub>

A unit of story is only a scene if a value at stake for the POV character shifts polarity within it
(life/death, trust/betrayal, hope/despair, belonging/exile). McKee's operational test: name the
value and its charge on entry (+ or −) and on exit. If the charge is identical, you have written an
event, not a scene. Inside the scene, locate the single moment where the charge flips — the turning
point — and make it either an ACTION (someone does something irrevocable) or a REVELATION
(information lands that cannot be un-known). McKee's own procedure: strip the scene to its beats,
find the beat where the major gap opens, and check that everything before it is approach and
everything after is consequence.

*Mechanism:* Readers do not track 'what happened,' they track 'what changed for someone I am tracking.' A
constant-charge scene gives the reader's predictive model nothing to update, so it costs attention
and returns none — this is what 'nothing happens' actually means when readers say it. The polarity
flip also does double duty as a memory hook: valence change is one of the few things that reliably
survives into long-term recall of a narrative, which is why readers remember reversals and forget
beautifully written stasis.

**The gap between expectation and result** · <sub>load-bearing · Robert McKee, Story: 'the substance of story is the gap that splits open between what a human being expects to happen when he takes an action and what really does happen.'</sub>

McKee's deeper mechanism beneath the value change: a character takes a minimum conservative action
expecting the world to respond a certain way, and the world responds otherwise. The gap that opens
is the story. Operationally, for each scene write two lines — 'She acts expecting X' / 'What she
gets is Y' — and then a third: 'So her second action, now that she knows the world is not what she
thought, is Z.' The second action must cost more than the first. A scene where expectation and
result match is a scene of exposition wearing a costume.

*Mechanism:* The gap forces the character to reach deeper into themselves for a second action, which is the only
reliable way character is revealed under pressure rather than asserted. On the reader's side the gap
is a prediction error — the reader was running the same model as the character, so the gap updates
the reader's world-model at the same instant it updates the character's. That simultaneity is what
produces identification. It also generates the next scene automatically: the corrected expectation
becomes the next scene's premise, which is why gap-driven drafting rarely sags.

**Causal chaining: 'therefore' and 'but,' never 'and then'** · <sub>load-bearing · Aristotle, Poetics ch. 9; E. M. Forster, Aspects of the Novel (1927); Trey Parker & Matt Stone, NYU Tisch, 2011; Trabasso & van den Broek, Journal of Memory and Language (1985).</sub>

List your beats and try to insert a connective between each pair. If only 'and then' fits, the joint
is structurally dead. Parker and Stone's rule from their 2011 NYU visit: every beat must be joined
by 'therefore' or 'but.' Aristotle says the same thing at higher altitude — episodes must follow by
necessity or probability, and an episodic plot (one where episodes succeed each other without
probability or necessity) is the worst kind. Forster's version: 'The king died and then the queen
died' is story; 'the queen died of grief' is plot. Story asks 'and then?'; plot asks 'why?'

*Mechanism:* There is hard experimental backing for this. Trabasso and van den Broek's causal network model
showed that readers build a causal graph of a narrative, and that an event's number of causal
connections to other events predicts both how often it is recalled and how important readers judge
it — more strongly than its position or intensity. An 'and then' beat has causal in-degree and out-
degree of zero: it is literally not in the reader's representation of the book. This gives you an
objective cut criterion — for each scene, count how many later scenes would break if you deleted it.
Scenes with in-degree zero are candidates regardless of how good the prose is.

**Peripeteia plus anagnorisis — surprise that is retroactively necessary** · <sub>load-bearing · Aristotle, Poetics chs. 9–11 (peripeteia, anagnorisis, the 'contrary to expectation yet on account of one another' formulation).</sub>

Aristotle's 'complex' plot contains a reversal of fortune (peripeteia) and/or a movement from
ignorance to knowledge (anagnorisis); the best plots fuse them into a single stroke, as in Oedipus,
where the messenger arrives to reassure and instead destroys. The load-bearing qualifier is easy to
miss: events should occur para tēn doxan di' allēla — contrary to expectation, yet on account of one
another. Operationally: plant the evidence in plain sight, cover it with a wrong-but-sufficient
explanation the reader will accept, and let the reversal remove the cover rather than introduce new
facts.

*Mechanism:* A twist works in two passes. The first pass violates the reader's predictive model, which produces
arousal. The second pass — which happens in the same half-second, as the reader silently re-reads
the preceding two hundred pages — repairs the model using material already in memory, which produces
the specific pleasure of mastery. A twist with no planted evidence gets the first pass only, which
is why shocking endings can feel simultaneously surprising and worthless. This also explains why the
best reversals are delivered by a character trying to help.

**Ask a question, delay the answer — and know the five ways to delay** · <sub>load-bearing · Lee Child, 'A Simple Way to Create Suspense,' New York Times, 8 Dec 2012; Roland Barthes, S/Z (1970) — the hermeneutic code and the morphemes of delay.</sub>

Lee Child's formulation is the cleanest statement of the engine: imply a question at the start, then
withhold the answer; the technique nests, working at book, chapter, paragraph, and sentence level.
Child's analogy is that suspense is not a recipe with ingredients and proportions — it is making
people hungry, then delaying dinner. What Child leaves out, Barthes supplies: a taxonomy of the
delays available inside the 'dilatory space' between question and answer — the SNARE (a deliberate
evasion), EQUIVOCATION (truth mixed with snare), PARTIAL ANSWER (one true feature of a multi-feature
truth), SUSPENDED ANSWER (begun, then interrupted), and JAMMING (openly acknowledging the question
may be unanswerable).

*Mechanism:* An unanswered question is an open loop in working memory that the reader cannot voluntarily close,
which is why 'one more chapter' happens against the reader's intention. But raw withholding is
detectable and readers punish it as coyness. Barthes's five moves matter because each one advances
the reader's belief state slightly while resolving nothing, so the loop stays open while the reader
registers motion. A writer with one delay move can withhold for thirty pages; a writer with five can
withhold for three hundred without the reader feeling withheld from.

**Sternberg's three interests: suspense, curiosity, surprise** · <sub>load-bearing · Meir Sternberg, Expositional Modes and Temporal Ordering in Fiction (Johns Hopkins, 1978; Indiana UP, 1993), and his later work on narrative universals.</sub>

Meir Sternberg's claim that all narrative interest reduces to three master forces defined by where
the reader's gap sits in time. SUSPENSE is a gap about the future (prospection): the reader knows
the situation, not the outcome. CURIOSITY is a gap about the past (retrospection): the reader knows
the outcome exists, not what produced it. SURPRISE is a gap the reader did not know was there
(recognition), sprung retroactively. Sternberg also distinguishes temporary gaps (filled during
reading) from permanent gaps (never filled) — a deliberate choice, not an accident. Operationally:
label every open question in the manuscript with which of the three it runs on, and check that no
long stretch runs on only one.

*Mechanism:* The three engines have different fuel and different failure modes, which is why generic 'raise the
tension' advice misfires. Suspense fails by stalling — the reader can see the future is not
arriving. Curiosity fails by answering too early, or by revealing the answer was not worth the wait.
Surprise cannot sustain anything, because it is instantaneous by definition; it can only punctuate.
A book carried entirely on curiosity feels static; carried entirely on suspense it feels shallow,
because nothing accrues. This is the single most useful distinction available for a braided novel,
and almost nobody in the craft-book world uses it.

**Braided structure: run the strands on different engines and pace them by different rules** · <sub>load-bearing · Application of Sternberg's suspense/curiosity distinction to dual-timeline craft; reverse-chronology example from Evie Wyld, All the Birds, Singing (2013).</sub>

In a two-timeline novel, assign the present strand to suspense and the past strand to curiosity —
then pace them accordingly. The present strand needs visible forward motion and a clock; it dies of
stalling. The past strand needs its answers metered and its final revelation held; it dies of
premature disclosure. Practical consequences: the past strand's chapters can be shorter and further
apart early, then longer and closer as it converges; the present strand cannot survive the same
treatment. A powerful variant is running the past strand in reverse chronology so it moves backward
toward the origin as the present moves forward toward the crisis — Evie Wyld does this in All the
Birds, Singing, which converts the past strand's curiosity into something structurally closer to
suspense.

*Mechanism:* Because the two strands are answering different kinds of gap, a single pacing rule applied to both
will overfeed one and starve the other. The commonest braided-novel failure — 'the historical
chapters drag' — is almost never a prose problem; it is a curiosity strand paced like a suspense
strand, giving progress where it should give withholding. Conversely, when readers say 'I skipped
ahead to the present-day chapters,' the past strand has usually answered its central question at 60%
and is coasting on texture.

**Braided structure: the cost of a strand switch, and the re-indexing sentence** · <sub>load-bearing · Zwaan, Langston & Graesser, 'The Construction of Situation Models in Narrative Comprehension: An Event-Indexing Model,' Psychological Science (1995); Zwaan & Radvansky (1998).</sub>

Every strand switch imposes a measurable comprehension cost, and the first sentence after the break
must pay it down. The event-indexing model says readers track five dimensions — time, space,
protagonist, causality, intentionality — and that integration is fast when the new event shares
indices with the old. A timeline switch breaks four at once. Operationally: the first sentence after
a switch should re-specify WHO and WHEN before it does anything else; place can wait a beat; and the
sentence should ideally also carry a causal or intentional hook back to that strand's last open
question, not to the strand you just left.

*Mechanism:* Zwaan, Langston and Graesser's experiments found reading times rise significantly at protagonist
shifts and temporal shifts, and only weakly at spatial shifts. That asymmetry is directly
actionable: the expensive indices are person and time, so opening a returning chapter with a
paragraph of landscape is spending the reader's scarce reorientation budget on the cheap index. The
date-stamp header (a 'when/where stamp') is a crude but genuinely effective subsidy — it offloads
temporal re-indexing to a device outside the prose, freeing the prose to re-establish person and
intention. Literary snobbery about headers costs more than it saves in books with more than two
strands.

**Braided structure: the convergence contract** · <sub>load-bearing · Synthesis; the rhyming level is Charles Baxter's 'Rhyming Action' (Burning Down the House, 1997); the revelatory level is Sternberg's curiosity-into-recognition.</sub>

Two strands in one book constitute an unstated promise that they are relevant to each other, and
that promise must be paid at one of three levels: CAUSAL (the past strand produced the present
crisis — strongest, and the most prone to feeling mechanical), REVELATORY (the past strand supplies
the information that reframes the present — Sternberg's curiosity resolving into recognition; this
is the dominant literary mode), or RHYMING/THEMATIC (Baxter's rhyming action — the strands never
touch causally but interpret each other). Decide which one you are paying, and place at least one
small convergence beat in each act so the reader has evidence the contract is live. Also fix the
strand ratio deliberately — words and scenes per strand per act — and watch for unintentional drift.

*Mechanism:* Readers extend enormous patience to a second timeline, but they are extending it on credit against
an anticipated relevance. If the payment never comes, they do not merely feel unsatisfied — they
retroactively reclassify every past-strand chapter as padding, which damages the parts of the book
that were working. Early small convergences are interest payments on the loan: they don't resolve
anything, but they prove the debt is real. The three levels differ in feel because causal
convergence satisfies the plot-tracking system while rhyming convergence satisfies the pattern-
recognition system, and the second is much harder to over-egg.

**Promise / progress / payoff** · <sub>strong · Brandon Sanderson, BYU lecture series (Lecture 2, 'Plot: Promise, Progress, Payoff'). Kin to Chekhov's gun, but generalized from objects to reader expectation.</sub>

Sanderson's three-part account of plot: the opening makes promises (about the central conflict, the
kind of world, the tone, the type of ending); the middle must deliver visible PROGRESS on those
specific promises; the ending must pay them off with the expected thing delivered in an unexpectedly
better way. The diagnostic move is the useful part: when the middle sags, don't ask 'is enough
happening?' — ask 'what promise is the reader most invested in, and when did they last see movement
on it?' The framework nests: a scene, an act, and a book each make and keep promises, at different
scales.

*Mechanism:* It reframes pace as a function of the reader's ledger rather than of event density. A middle can be
crammed with incident and still sag if none of the incident touches the promise the reader is
holding — which is exactly what happens when a writer patches a sagging middle by adding subplot.
Conversely a quiet middle reads as fast when each quiet scene visibly moves the promised thing. The
payoff rule ('expected thing, better than expected') is a precise statement of why subverted
expectations often fail: subversion pays a different promise than the one the reader bought.

**The scene/sequel unit, and refusing to delete the sequel** · <sub>strong · Dwight V. Swain, Techniques of the Selling Writer (University of Oklahoma Press, 1965); popularized by Jack Bickham and later Randy Ingermanson.</sub>

Dwight Swain's alternating unit. SCENE = goal, conflict, disaster (the POV character wants something
specific, is opposed, and ends worse off or facing a new problem). SEQUEL = reaction, dilemma,
decision (emotional reaction to the disaster; weighing of bad options; a decision that becomes the
next scene's goal). Swain also gives a sentence-level version, the Motivation-Reaction Unit:
external stimulus in its own paragraph, then the character's response in fixed order — visceral
feeling, involuntary reflex, then rational speech or action.

*Mechanism:* The sequel is where a new GOAL is manufactured out of the last disaster, and the goal is what makes
the next scene legible as caused rather than arbitrary. Modern advice to 'cut the reaction and keep
it moving' produces prose that feels fast at the sentence and a plot that feels random at the book,
because the reader can see events but not the reasoning that connects them — a causal chain with the
causes deleted. Swain's fixed MRU order works because it matches the actual latency of human
response (limbic before cortical); reversing it produces the characteristic uncanniness of
characters who articulate before they feel. This is deeply unfashionable and remains the most
reliable fix for 'things happen but I don't care.'

**The midpoint as irreversible turn from reactive to active** · <sub>load-bearing · John Yorke, Into the Woods (2013); the reactive-to-active formulation is common to McKee, Yorke and the commercial structure tradition.</sub>

Around the middle, something must happen that changes not what the protagonist FACES but what they
WANT or what they KNOW — after which return to the prior state is impossible. The standard shapes
are the false victory (they get the thing and it is not the thing) and the false defeat (they lose
everything and discover what they were actually after). Yorke's structural point: the midpoint is
where knowledge is gained and the return journey begins; it is the point of no return in the literal
sense. Practical test: write the protagonist's stated want at 25% and at 60%. If it is phrased the
same way, you have no midpoint.

*Mechanism:* The sagging middle is a GOAL problem masquerading as an EVENT problem. If the want has not changed
shape since act one, then every new obstacle in the middle is structurally the same obstacle, and
the reader — who is pattern-matching, not counting incidents — perceives repetition regardless of
how varied the surface is. This is why adding events to a sagging middle makes it worse: it adds
instances of the pattern the reader is already bored by. Changing the want re-partitions a long
undifferentiated middle into two shorter stretches, each with its own destination, which is also a
cognitive relief for the writer.

**Freytag's pyramid is misremembered, and the correction is useful** · <sub>useful · Gustav Freytag, Die Technik des Dramas (1863). The misattribution is documented in most careful accounts of the pyramid.</sub>

Gustav Freytag's Die Technik des Dramas (1863) places the Höhepunkt — climax — at the CENTRE of the
five-act play, followed by the 'return or fall' and the catastrophe. The school-diagram version
taught for a century moved the climax to roughly 80% and renamed the catastrophe 'resolution.'
Freytag was describing classical and Shakespearean five-act tragedy, not the novel, and his second
half is CONSEQUENCE, not escalation: after the peak of the protagonist's fortune, the acts unspool
what that peak set in motion.

*Mechanism:* The correction matters operationally because Freytag's climax is what modern writers call the
midpoint. Recognizing this gives you a legitimate structural option the modern diagram hides: a book
whose highest point of the protagonist's fortune sits at 50% and whose second half is the working-
out of that success into ruin. That is the shape of most tragedy and of a large fraction of serious
literary fiction, and writers trying to force it into a climax-at-85% template mangle it. It also
explains why 'the second half feels like falling' is sometimes correct rather than a flaw.

**The five-act fractal — and its limits** · <sub>contested · John Yorke, Into the Woods (2013); Shawn Coyne, The Story Grid (2015), 'the Five Commandments of Storytelling.'</sub>

Yorke's central claim: five-act structure is not different from three-act, it is the same template
with acts two and four split out, giving 'five girders rather than three'; and the same three-
movement shape (encounter something new, explore it, assimilate it) recurs at every scale from the
beat to the scene to the act to the whole. Coyne makes the same fractal claim with a different unit
— his Five Commandments (inciting incident, progressive complication, crisis, climax, resolution)
must be satisfiable at beat, scene, sequence, act and global level, and if a scene fails them the
scene is broken.

*Mechanism:* The fractal claim earns its keep as a DEBUGGING tool rather than a generative one. Because the
reader's comprehension machinery works the same way at every scale — set an expectation, complicate
it, resolve it — a broken unit at any scale produces the same symptom (flatness), and you can zoom
to the level where the break actually is. Its generative use is more dangerous: writing to the
fractal produces a book with the same rhythm at every scale, which is monotonous in exactly the way
a Sierpinski triangle is monotonous. Alison, Le Guin and Baxter are right that the fractal is a
description of one tradition's habit, not a law of mind, and Yorke overclaims when he grounds it in
cognition.

**Demote the Hero's Journey to one usable beat** · <sub>contested · Joseph Campbell (1949); Christopher Vogler, The Writer's Journey (1992, from his 1985 Disney memo); critiques by Alan Dundes, Barre Toelken, Robert Segal; Maureen Murdock, The Heroine's Journey (1990).</sub>

Campbell's monomyth (Hero with a Thousand Faces, 1949) and Vogler's twelve-stage Hollywood
adaptation supply a CONTENT template — mentor, threshold guardian, belly of the whale, elixir — not
a structural one. Folklorists have shown the pattern was constructed by selecting conforming myths
and discarding non-conforming ones: Toelken's charge is that Campbell built the monomyth by citing
stories that fit and leaving out equally valid stories that did not, and Alan Dundes treated
Campbell as a non-expert with systematic source bias. Oedipus Rex, Rumpelstiltskin and most creation
myths do not fit. Maureen Murdock developed the Heroine's Journey after Campbell told her, of women,
'they don't need to make the journey... she's the place that people are trying to get to.'

*Mechanism:* The monomyth's residue that actually does structural work is the threshold crossing: a commitment
that costs something and cannot be undone. That beat works for the same reason the midpoint works —
irreversibility converts a reader's interest from 'will they?' into 'at what price?' The rest of the
apparatus is furniture. The specific harm of the full template is that it tells you which characters
to buy rather than where the load-bearing walls go, so books built on it are populated correctly and
structured arbitrarily; and it silently imports a single protagonist with a single quest, which is a
poor fit for braided, ensemble, and network narratives.

**Save the Cat: keep the clock, discard the vocabulary** · <sub>contested · Blake Snyder, Save the Cat! (2005); Jessica Brody, Save the Cat! Writes a Novel (2018); critique positions from Jane Alison, Ursula Le Guin, Charles Baxter and the mainstream of literary-fiction pedagogy.</sub>

Blake Snyder's fifteen beats with specified page numbers (Opening Image p.1, Catalyst p.12, Break
into Two p.25, Midpoint p.55, All Is Lost p.75, Break into Three p.85 on a 110-page script), ported
to novels by Jessica Brody. The literary objection is not usually stated precisely enough to be
useful; stated precisely, it has two parts. The percentages are defensible — they encode real
regularities in reader attention and genre expectation. The beat NAMES are the problem: 'Fun and
Games,' 'Dark Night of the Soul,' 'Finale' encode a specific late-90s Hollywood emotional register,
and writing toward the name rather than the position produces tonal insincerity that literary
readers detect immediately even when they cannot name it.

*Mechanism:* A percentage is a constraint on WHEN; a beat name is a prescription of WHAT and, worse, of HOW IT
SHOULD FEEL. Constraints on when are productive because they force the writer to invent material
that fits a position; prescriptions of feeling are unproductive because they let the writer retrieve
material instead of inventing it, and retrieved material carries the affect of its source. The
deeper literary objection — from Alison, Le Guin and Baxter — is that the beat sheet presupposes
conflict as the engine and epiphany as the payoff, both of which are choices rather than
necessities. Snyder is right that placement matters; his critics are right that his placement comes
bundled with a worldview.

**Alison's non-arc patterns and the substitute engine problem** · <sub>strong · Jane Alison, Meander, Spiral, Explode: Design and Pattern in Narrative (Catapult, 2019), drawing pattern vocabulary from Peter Stevens, Patterns in Nature (1974).</sub>

Jane Alison's taxonomy of shapes borrowed from Peter Stevens' Patterns in Nature: wave and wavelet
(undulation without climax), meander (rivers, snakes, drift with local logic), spiral (return to the
same point at a different altitude), radial or explosion (a centre with spokes — daisy, splash),
cellular (discrete adjacent units, honeycomb, foam), fractal (self-similarity at scales — Nicholson
Baker's The Mezzanine and its footnotes), network, and tsunami. Her specimens include Anne Carson,
Raymond Carver, García Márquez, Jamaica Kincaid, Clarice Lispector, Mary Robison. Her opening
question is genuinely good: why did the dramatic arc, whose origin is Greek tragedy, become the
default shape of the NOVEL, a form with no such requirement?

*Mechanism:* The arc's grip on a reader comes from expectation management — the reader is running a prediction
about an outcome. Remove the arc and you must supply a substitute engine, or the book has none. The
three that work are ACCRETION (the reader is assembling a picture and each piece visibly enlarges it
— Sebald, Duras), RECURRENCE-WITH-DIFFERENCE (rhythm: the reader recognizes a return and measures
the change, which is a micro-payoff on every recurrence), and JUXTAPOSITION (the text refuses the
causal link and the reader supplies it, which converts reading into authorship and is powerfully
engaging when the gap is the right size). Non-arc books fail when the writer removes the arc without
installing one of these — the resulting book is not experimental, it is merely unpowered.

**Forster's pattern versus rhythm — and why rhythm is the safer binder** · <sub>strong · E. M. Forster, Aspects of the Novel (1927), chapter on 'Pattern and Rhythm.'</sub>

Forster separates PATTERN (a spatial shape visible only when the book is finished — he calls Anatole
France's Thaïs an hourglass and Percy Lubbock's Roman Pictures a chain) from RHYTHM (repetition plus
variation, operating on the reader in real time — his example is the 'little phrase' of Vinteuil's
sonata in Proust, which is 'not there all the time like a pattern, but by its lovely waxing and
waning fills us with surprise and freshness and hope'). Forster warns that pattern exacts a price:
the novel may have to be deformed to serve the shape.

*Mechanism:* Pattern rewards retrospective memory; rhythm rewards immediate perception. That difference
determines which one is safe in a long book. A reader 400 pages into a novel cannot perceive an
hourglass — they can only be told about one afterwards — so pattern's payoff is deferred to a re-
read most readers never perform, while its costs (scenes bent to fit) are paid in real time on the
first read. Rhythm's payoff is instantaneous and does not require the reader to notice consciously:
a returning image or cadence produces a small recognition pleasure whether or not it is identified
as a device. For a braided novel this is decisive — braiding naturally invites pattern (mirror
halves, symmetrical convergence), and the sound advice is to build the book on rhythm and let
pattern be a bonus for the second reader.

**Rhyming action: subplots that rhyme rather than feed** · <sub>strong · Charles Baxter, 'Rhyming Action,' Michigan Quarterly Review (Fall 1996), collected in Burning Down the House: Essays on Fiction (Graywolf, 1997).</sub>

Charles Baxter's argument that a subplot need not connect causally to the main plot; it can connect
by echo — a second situation that poses the same question in a different key, so that the two
interpret each other without touching. Related is his 'stutter-memory': a scene glimpsed or half-
remembered early, then re-encountered in full later, so the second encounter carries the first as a
shadow. The operational move: when a subplot feels bolted on, do not invent a causal link. Sharpen
the rhyme instead — put the same value at stake in a register where the answer comes out
differently.

*Mechanism:* Causal integration of a subplot usually requires coincidence, and coincidence has a detectable
authorial fingerprint that breaks the fictional dream. A rhyme has no fingerprint because the
connection is made in the reader's head, not on the page; the reader experiences it as their own
insight, which is far more durable than an insight they were handed. This is also the least
mechanical of the three braided-convergence options, and the one that scales to three or more
strands where causal convergence would collapse under its own coincidence budget.

**Ring composition and central loading** · <sub>useful · Mary Douglas, Thinking in Circles: An Essay on Ring Composition (Yale, 2007).</sub>

Mary Douglas's account of an ancient and cross-cultural structure. Her seven criteria: an exposition
or prologue that states the theme; a split into two halves; parallel sections mirroring across the
centre; explicit indicators marking the sections; CENTRAL LOADING (the key meaning sits at the
midpoint, not the end); rings within rings; and closure at two levels (the ending latches back to
the beginning AND completes the middle). Her key distinction from mere chiasmus: in true ring
composition the central section reinforces the rhetoric and themes of the outer frame.

*Mechanism:* Ring composition puts the emphatic position at the centre rather than the end, which is the opposite
of the Western climax instinct. It works because in a mirrored structure the reader's attention is
being drawn inward from both directions; the centre is the only place both halves point at. It also
produces a specific and desirable reading experience: the second half is haunted, because every
section is felt as an answer to its mirror. This is genuinely available to a braided novelist — a
braid with an even number of strand-switches produces mirror halves almost for free — and it is
almost never used deliberately in contemporary fiction, which makes it a live source of structural
novelty rather than a museum piece.

**Kishōtenketsu: the turn that recontextualizes rather than resolves** · <sub>useful · Classical Chinese qǐ chéng zhuǎn hé; Japanese kishōtenketsu, dominant across manga, anime and Japanese cinema.</sub>

The four-movement East Asian structure — ki (introduction), shō (development), ten (turn), ketsu
(conclusion) — descended from Chinese qǐ chéng zhuǎn hé quatrain structure. The distinguishing
element is TEN: a third movement that introduces something apparently unrelated, or shifts the
frame, or reveals an overlooked detail — and KETSU, which achieves closure by reconciling the new
frame with the first two movements rather than by resolving a contest. Conflict is optional
throughout.

*Mechanism:* Satisfaction here comes from a completed UNDERSTANDING rather than a completed CONTEST. The reader
is given two movements of stable material, then a third that makes the stability wrong, then a
fourth that shows what the material actually was. That is the same cognitive event as anagnorisis,
decoupled from fortune-reversal. This is why kishōtenketsu is not a curiosity for Western novelists:
it is the correct structure for the quiet chapter, the interlude, the standalone braided section
that must be shapely without advancing the war. A Western-structured book with no kishōtenketsu
chapters has no way to make a non-conflictual scene feel finished.

**Chapter shape: end on a new incompletion, not a completion** · <sub>strong · Consolidated practice; the delay principle is Lee Child's, the serial-cut discipline descends from Dickens's part-issue publication, the completion-reward account is standard attention psychology.</sub>

A chapter is a unit of permission-to-stop. The 'one more chapter' effect requires that the
completion the chapter offers arrive AFTER a new incompletion has been opened — resolve the
chapter's local question in the penultimate beat and open the next question in the final beat, or
simply cut before the scene's own resolution. Additional operational levers: vary chapter length
deliberately as a pace instrument rather than for tidiness; end chapters on a turn (an action taken,
a fact landed) rather than on summary or reflection; and consider ending mid-scene and resuming from
a different angle, which Dickens's serial practice normalized and which modern thrillers have
industrialized.

*Mechanism:* Chapter breaks are the reader's decision points, so they are where a book is actually abandoned or
continued. Two forces are in tension: an unclosed loop pushes the reader forward, but a completed
unit is intrinsically rewarding and rewards make readers feel the book is generous. Ending on
incompletion alone reads as manipulation; ending on completion alone gives permission to stop. The
sequence 'close the small one, open the larger one' gets both. Short chapters raise perceived pace
independently of content because they raise the DENSITY of completion events per hour of reading —
which is why a book can be slow in event and fast in feel.

**Part and act breaks as a licence for discontinuity** · <sub>useful · Consolidated practice; the discontinuity-licence account follows from Zwaan & Radvansky's event-indexing model.</sub>

A Part break (as opposed to a chapter break) is a renegotiation of the reader's contract. It
licenses moves that would read as betrayal mid-flow: a jump of years, a change of POV or of
narrative person, a tonal shift, a new dramatic question, the arrival of a new central character, a
change in the rules of the world. Practical consequence: if you need one of those moves, do not
smuggle it into chapter 14 — put a Part break in front of it. And treat the first chapter after a
Part break as a SECOND FIRST CHAPTER: it must re-establish stakes and re-make a promise, because the
reader has genuinely put the book down.

*Mechanism:* Readers hold an implicit model of what kind of book they are reading, and violations of that model
are experienced as authorial incompetence unless they are marked. A Part break is a marker that says
'the violation is intentional,' which converts the same content from a fault into a feature — the
identical time jump reads as sloppy inside a Part and as architecture across one. In event-indexing
terms, a Part break gives explicit advance warning that all five situational indices are about to
break, so the reader does not attempt the (failing) integration and instead builds a fresh model.

**Novella shape is a working-memory constraint, not a word count** · <sub>useful · Ian McEwan, 'Some Notes on the Novella,' The New Yorker, 29 October 2012.</sub>

McEwan's formulation in 'Some Notes on the Novella' (New Yorker, 29 Oct 2012): the novella is the
perfect form of prose fiction, short enough to be read in a sitting or two and 'the whole structure
to be held in the mind at first encounter' — the experience of watching a play or a longish film.
Structurally this cashes out as: a single line of action; one major reversal, not three; a unifying
constraint of time or place (one night, one journey, one building, one deadline); and at most one
subplot, which must rhyme rather than complicate.

*Mechanism:* If the reader can hold the whole architecture in working memory at first encounter, then Forster's
PATTERN becomes perceptible in real time — symmetry, mirror, ring, echo all pay off on the first
read rather than being deferred to a re-read that never happens. This is why novellas can be
formally daring in ways novels cannot. It also explains the form's brutality about subplot: a
subplot costs a slot in a memory budget that is nearly full, so a novella with two subplots does not
feel richer, it feels blurred. Practically, the novella is the one length where you should design
pattern first and scenes second — the reverse of novel practice.

**The obligatory scene (scène à faire) must happen on the page** · <sub>strong · Term coined by the nineteenth-century French critic Francisque Sarcey, from Eugène Scribe's pièce bien faite tradition; extended to genre conventions by Shawn Coyne, The Story Grid.</sub>

The scene the reader has been promised — the confrontation between the two people who matter, the
revelation delivered face to face, the reckoning — must be dramatized in full, with the necessary
parties present, at the point of maximum consequence. Reporting it, summarizing it, having it occur
off-page, or having a third party relate it afterwards will break the book even when the plot is
technically complete. Coyne generalizes this to genre CONVENTIONS: each genre carries a small set of
scenes the reader has effectively paid for.

*Mechanism:* The obligatory scene is the payoff position of the largest promise in the book, so its absence is a
default on the largest debt. Sarcey's original insight, from the well-made-play tradition, is that
this scene is where reversal and recognition are supposed to coincide — it is the structural slot in
which Aristotle's two mechanisms fire together. Writers skip it for identifiable and bad reasons: it
is the hardest scene to write, the characters may not be articulate enough to carry it, or the
writer fears melodrama. All three are craft problems, not reasons to relocate the scene. When
readers say an ending 'felt rushed,' they very often mean the obligatory scene was summarized.

**Chart the valence curve as a diagnostic, not a template** · <sub>useful · Kurt Vonnegut, 'Shapes of Stories' (rejected University of Chicago master's thesis; later a lecture); A. J. Reagan, L. Mitchell, D. Kiley, C. M. Danforth & P. S. Dodds, 'The emotional arcs of stories are dominated by six basic shapes,' EPJ Data Science (2016).</sub>

Vonnegut's 'shapes of stories' — the Man in Hole, Boy Meets Girl, Cinderella curves, from the
master's thesis the University of Chicago rejected — later given empirical support by Reagan,
Mitchell, Kiley, Danforth and Dodds, who ran sentiment analysis over 1,327 Project Gutenberg works
and found emotional arcs dominated by six shapes: rise (rags to riches), fall (tragedy), fall-rise
(man in a hole), rise-fall (Icarus), rise-fall-rise (Cinderella), fall-rise-fall (Oedipus). The
working use is diagnostic: plot your own manuscript's emotional valence chapter by chapter and look
at the line.

*Mechanism:* Valence trajectory is a coarse but genuinely separate variable from plot — two books with identical
event structures can have opposite curves. Because it is separable, it fails separately, and a flat
or oscillating-without-drift middle section shows up on the chart before you can name its cause in
the prose. This is a rare case where a mechanical measurement precedes the craft diagnosis. The
failure mode is treating the six shapes as templates to write toward; they are a description of an
English-language corpus, not a set of options, and 'Cinderella' is not a plan.
