**Le Guin's carrier bag: the container as the shape** · <sub>useful · Ursula K. Le Guin, 'The Carrier Bag Theory of Fiction' (1986), collected in Dancing at the Edge of the World.</sub>

Le Guin's 1986 essay proposes that the proper shape of the novel may be a sack rather than an arrow
— 'a medicine bundle, holding things in a particular, powerful relation to one another and to us.'
She is explicit that this does NOT abolish conflict: conflict, competition, stress and struggle
remain necessary elements of a whole whose purpose is 'neither resolution nor stasis but continuing
process.' The commonly cited line is the test: 'You put him in a bag and he looks like a rabbit,
like a potato. This is why I like novels: instead of heroes they have people in them.'

*Mechanism:* The essay's structural content, usually lost under its politics, is a claim about what supplies
unity. In an arrow-shaped narrative unity comes from a goal: everything is present because it bears
on the outcome. In a bag-shaped one unity comes from RELATION: everything is present because of how
it sits against everything else. That is a genuinely different selection criterion for what belongs
in a book, and it is the criterion that governs most successful braided, ensemble and network novels
— which is why arc-based structural advice misfires on them so consistently. The frequent misreading
of Le Guin as anti-conflict is worth correcting, because it makes the essay sound like permission
for shapelessness when it is actually a proposal for a different principle of coherence.

### Non-obvious findings — Story architecture & structure

- Freytag's climax is the MIDPOINT, not the ending. Die Technik des Dramas puts the Höhepunkt at the
  centre of the five-act play, with the second half devoted to consequence and ending in
  catastrophe; the familiar school diagram with the climax at ~80% is a later corruption. What
  modern writers call the midpoint is literally Freytag's climax — which legitimizes a shape (peak
  of fortune at 50%, second half a controlled fall) that most contemporary structure advice has no
  vocabulary for.

- Mary Douglas's ring composition CENTRALLY LOADS: in a properly ringed text the most important
  material sits in the middle, framed by mirrored halves, and the ending's job is only to latch back
  to the opening. This inverts the Western climax-at-the-end instinct, and a braided novel with an
  even number of strand-switches produces the mirrored halves almost for free.

- Trabasso and van den Broek showed empirically that what readers recall and judge important tracks an
  event's NUMBER OF CAUSAL CONNECTIONS — not its emotional intensity, not its prose quality, not its
  position. A quiet scene many later scenes depend on will outrank a spectacular scene nothing
  depends on. This converts 'kill your darlings' from a temperament into a measurement: compute
  causal in-degree, cut the zeros.

- Zwaan, Langston and Graesser found that reading times rise significantly at PROTAGONIST and TEMPORAL
  shifts but only weakly at SPATIAL ones. For a braided novel this is directly actionable and cuts
  against instinct: the expensive part of a strand switch is who and when, not where — so opening a
  returning chapter with a paragraph of landscape spends the reader's reorientation budget on the
  cheapest index. Lead with person and time; the room can wait.

- A dual-timeline novel runs on two DIFFERENT engines and therefore cannot be paced by one rule. In
  Sternberg's terms the present strand runs on suspense (a gap about the future) and the past strand
  on curiosity (a gap about the past). They fail in opposite directions: suspense fails by stalling,
  curiosity fails by answering too early. 'The historical chapters drag' is almost never a prose
  problem — it is a curiosity strand being paced like a suspense strand.

- Save the Cat's page PERCENTAGES are more defensible than its beat NAMES. The positions encode real
  regularities in attention and expectation; the names ('Fun and Games,' 'Dark Night of the Soul')
  encode a specific 1990s Hollywood affect that literary readers detect as insincerity. A constraint
  on WHEN forces invention; a prescription of HOW IT SHOULD FEEL permits retrieval, and retrieved
  material carries the tone of its source. Take the clock, leave the vocabulary.

- Aristotle's requirement is not surprise but surprise DI' ALLĒLA — events contrary to expectation yet
  occurring on account of one another. The pleasure is in the instantaneous silent re-reading of the
  preceding pages, not in the shock. This is why an unplanted twist is structurally inert even when
  it is genuinely startling: it gets the prediction-error half of the effect and none of the model-
  repair half.

- Barthes catalogued the KINDS of delay available between a question and its answer — snare,
  equivocation, partial answer, suspended answer, jamming. Most novelists have one withholding move
  and use it until readers notice. Having five is the difference between sustaining an enigma for
  thirty pages and sustaining it for three hundred without the reader ever feeling withheld from.

- Forster's pattern/rhythm distinction predicts which structural devices are SAFE in a long book, and
  nobody uses it that way. Pattern (the hourglass, the chain) is perceptible only retrospectively,
  so its payoff is deferred to a re-read most readers never perform while its costs — scenes bent to
  fit — are paid on page one. Rhythm (repetition plus variation) works in real time and requires no
  conscious noticing. Braiding invites pattern; rhythm is the binder that actually holds.

- The novella's real constraint is working memory, not word count. McEwan's phrase — 'the whole
  structure to be held in the mind at first encounter' — explains both why novellas can be formally
  daring in ways novels cannot (pattern pays off on the FIRST read) and why one extra subplot wrecks
  them (it costs a slot in a nearly full budget, so the book reads blurred rather than rich).

- The sagging middle is a GOAL problem wearing the costume of an EVENT problem. If the protagonist's
  want has not changed shape since act one, every middle obstacle is structurally the same obstacle
  no matter how varied its surface — and readers pattern-match rather than count. This is why the
  standard remedy (add a subplot, add incident) reliably makes it worse: it adds more instances of
  the pattern the reader is already bored by.

- Swain's SEQUEL is the unit that modern 'keep it moving' advice deletes, and its deletion is the
  actual cause of plots that feel arbitrary. The sequel is where a new goal is manufactured out of
  the last disaster; remove it and the reader sees the events but not the reasoning that connects
  them — a causal chain with the causes cut out. Prose that feels fast at the sentence and random at
  the book is nearly always a sequel deficiency.

- Le Guin's carrier bag is not anti-conflict, and reading it that way makes it useless. She explicitly
  retains conflict as an element inside the container. Her actual structural claim is about what
  supplies UNITY: an arrow-shaped narrative includes material because it bears on an outcome; a bag-
  shaped one includes material because of how it sits against everything else. That is a different
  selection criterion for what belongs in a book, and it is the one that governs most successful
  braided and ensemble novels.

- Campbell's monomyth is a CONTENT template masquerading as a structural one — it tells you which
  furniture to buy, not where the load-bearing walls go. Folklorists (Dundes, Toelken) showed it was
  assembled by selecting conforming myths and discarding non-conforming ones, including Oedipus Rex,
  Rumpelstiltskin, and most creation myths. Its one genuinely structural residue is the irreversible
  threshold crossing, which works for the same reason a midpoint works.

- Kishōtenketsu is not an exotic alternative to Western structure — it is the missing tool for the
  QUIET chapter inside a conflict-driven Western book. Its 'ten' delivers anagnorisis
  (recontextualization) decoupled from fortune-reversal, which is precisely how you make a non-
  conflictual scene feel finished rather than merely stopped. A conflict-structured novel with no
  kishōtenketsu-shaped sections has no way to end a peaceful chapter satisfyingly.

## Character, desire and interiority — what makes a reader care, and how desire, belief and rendered thought are built, tested and kept honest on the page (with particular attention to idea-driven fiction that must steel-man positions its author rejects)

**Yearning as the through-line, present on every page** · <sub>load-bearing · Robert Olen Butler, From Where You Dream (2005, ed. Janet Burroway): fiction concerns human beings, human emotion, and desire; 'yearning' is his preferred word because it 'suggests the deepest level of desire.'</sub>

Distinguish surface desire (get the job, find the killer) from deep yearning (to be forgiven, to be
known, to be the person one pretends to be). Before drafting a scene, be able to name both, and be
able to point to the sentence in the scene where the yearning is exerting pressure on behaviour —
not stated, but exerting pressure. Butler's discipline is that no page is exempt.

*Mechanism:* Fiction is a temporal art: the reader must hold a long sequence in working memory, and an unresolved
vector is the cheapest way to do that. Desire converts a list of events into a directed shape, so
the reader can compress what they have read into one object ('he is still trying to get X') rather
than storing episodes. A page with no active yearning is a page the reader cannot compress, which is
experienced as 'nothing is happening' even when events occur.

**Scene-level interrogation: who wants what from whom, right now** · <sub>load-bearing · Konstantin Stanislavski, An Actor Prepares (units and objectives); Alexander Mackendrick, On Film-making (scenes as negotiation); David Mamet, On Directing Film and True and False.</sub>

Before writing a scene, write one line per present character: 'A wants B to do/say/give X, and will
try W.' Every character in the room gets one, including the silent ones. If two characters share an
objective and no third party resists, the scene is a briefing, not a scene. Borrowed wholesale from
rehearsal practice (Stanislavski's 'units and objectives'), and imported into screen and prose craft
by Mamet and Alexander Mackendrick, who taught scenes as negotiations.

*Mechanism:* Readers infer minds from discrepancy: what a person says minus what they want. Give every speaker a
want and the gap generates subtext automatically, with no authorial nudging. Remove the wants and
dialogue collapses into information exchange, which readers process as exposition and skim. It also
gives the writer a stop condition — the scene ends when the want is met, blocked, or replaced.

**Put the target outside the character, at a measurable distance** · <sub>load-bearing · Declan Donnellan, The Actor and the Target (2002). A pointed correction to lazy applications of Stanislavski.</sub>

State desire as an act performed on something external and specific, never as an internal state. Not
'she wants to feel respected' but 'she wants Hale to say the word sorry before the tea goes cold.'
Donnellan insists the target is always outside, always specific, always changing, and that it is
emphatically not an objective, want, plan, motive or goal — those live inside and cannot be acted
on.

*Mechanism:* An internal-state desire can be 'satisfied' on the page by mood description, which is why so much
writerly desire produces static prose. An external target forces observable behaviour, permits
failure (which is what makes it dramatic), and gives the reader a scoreboard. It also relieves the
writer of introspection they cannot dramatise: you cannot write 'wanting to feel respected,' but you
can write someone waiting for a word.

**Transitive action verbs, beat by beat** · <sub>strong · Sanford Meisner's repetition and action work; codified in Marina Caldarone and Maggie Lloyd-Williams, Actions: The Actors' Thesaurus (2004).</sub>

Annotate each speech (in the margin, not the text) with a verb done to another person: to shame, to
enlist, to disarm, to bait, to absolve. Reject intransitive or attitudinal labels ('to be angry',
'to explain'). Actors keep thesauri of these for exactly this purpose.

*Mechanism:* It forces the writer's attention from content to function, which is where character actually lives.
A reader's model of intelligence in a character is built almost entirely from the fit between tactic
and obstacle — smart characters pick apt verbs, desperate ones repeat verbs. It also produces line-
level variety without the writer having to think about 'voice' abstractly.

**At least one tactic change per scene** · <sub>strong · Robert McKee, Story (1997), on beats and scene turns.</sub>

Audit every scene for a moment where a character, meeting resistance, switches approach while
keeping the same want. If nobody changes tactic, either nobody met resistance or nobody is thinking.
McKee's beat is exactly this unit: an exchange of behaviour in action and reaction.

*Mechanism:* Agency is legible to readers only through adaptive behaviour. A character who pursues one tactic
through resistance reads as a mechanism; a character who reroutes reads as a mind. This is also the
cheapest available surprise: the want stays constant (consistency) while the behaviour changes
(novelty), which is precisely Forster's convincing surprise at scene scale.

**Status transactions as the scene's second track** · <sub>strong · Keith Johnstone, Impro: Improvisation and the Theatre (1979).</sub>

Mark every exchange as a status raise or lower, and track who is up at the start of the scene and
who is up at the end. Johnstone's claim is that we are always playing status, that nobody genuinely
plays equal, and that scenes come alive when status is unstable — a servant raising, a master
lowering.

*Mechanism:* Human social cognition is exquisitely tuned to dominance and deference cues, and reads them pre-
consciously from posture, interruption, latency and choice of topic. Status therefore delivers
interiority without a single interior sentence, and it works on readers who would resist an explicit
emotional cue. It is also the reliable diagnosis for the 'well-written scene where nothing happens':
the status is flat.

**Scene and sequel: interiority's job is to manufacture the next goal** · <sub>load-bearing · Dwight V. Swain, Techniques of the Selling Writer (1965).</sub>

Swain's two-part structure: scene = goal, conflict, disaster; sequel = reaction, dilemma, decision.
Interiority is quarantined into the sequel, and it must terminate in a decision that becomes the
next scene's goal. Any interior passage that does not convert emotion into intention is cut or
moved.

*Mechanism:* This makes thought load-bearing rather than decorative, which is the structural cure for rumination.
Readers track state change; interiority that only restates known feeling registers as delay and
trains the reader to skim interiority in general — so one indulgent passage costs you the reader's
attention for all subsequent ones. Making the decision the output means the reader learns that
thought pays, and reads it.

**Motivation-Reaction Unit ordering at the line level** · <sub>useful · Dwight V. Swain, Techniques of the Selling Writer (1965). Unfashionable, derided as mechanical, and empirically sound.</sub>

After an external stimulus, render the character's response in fixed order: feeling (involuntary,
visceral), then action or reflex (partly involuntary), then speech or rational action (intentional).
Never speech before pulse. Swain's rationale is explicitly the ordering from most involuntary to
most intentional.

*Mechanism:* It matches actual processing latency — affective and interoceptive responses precede deliberation —
so the prose maps onto the reader's own embodied expectation and reads as true. Violations produce
the specific uncanniness of a character being operated by the author: someone who speaks a
considered sentence before their body has registered the shock. This is the most reliable mechanical
fix for 'the emotion feels fake' notes.

**The psychic-distance ladder** · <sub>load-bearing · John Gardner, The Art of Fiction: Notes on Craft for Young Writers (1983).</sub>

Gardner's five levels, from 'It was winter of the year 1853. A large man stepped out of a doorway'
(level 1, formal, exterior) through 'Henry hated snowstorms' (level 3) to 'Snow. Under your collar,
down inside your shoes, freezing and plugging up your miserable soul' (level 5, inside the idiom).
Operationally: tag each paragraph 1–5, move one step at a time, and reserve the descent to 4–5 for
moments of maximum pressure.

*Mechanism:* Closeness is set by diction and syntax, not by pronoun — third person can be closer than first.
Because the reader calibrates to the prevailing level, a controlled descent registers as
intensification without any emotional vocabulary; an uncontrolled jump registers as the author
stepping in front of the character. Reserving the bottom of the ladder gives you an intensity gear
you have not already spent.

**Choose the rendering mode deliberately: psycho-narration, narrated monologue, quoted monologue** · <sub>load-bearing · Dorrit Cohn, Transparent Minds: Narrative Modes for Presenting Consciousness in Fiction (1978); James Wood's treatment of free indirect style in How Fiction Works (2008); the technique from Flaubert and Austen.</sub>

Cohn's taxonomy of consciousness-rendering. Psycho-narration: the narrator reports the mind in the
narrator's idiom ('She had never understood her own fury'). Narrated monologue (free indirect
style): the character's idiom inside the narration, tenses and pronouns of narration retained ('God,
the fury of it; she had never asked for any of this'). Quoted monologue: direct thought. Rule of
thumb: narrated monologue for judgements and colouring; quoted monologue only for what the character
consciously formulates in words; psycho-narration for what the character cannot articulate about
themselves.

*Mechanism:* Free indirect style lets the reader hold the character's idiom and the author's evaluation in the
same sentence, which is the only technique that produces irony without condemnation. That double
register is exactly what steel-manning requires: you can inhabit a worldview fully at the level of
grammar while the surrounding narration declines to endorse it. Quoted monologue, by contrast,
always implies the character has words for it — a claim about self-knowledge you may not want to
make.

**Never let the narrator name the emotion; let the character name it wrongly** · <sub>load-bearing · The mechanism follows Lisa Zunshine's theory-of-mind account in Why We Read Fiction (2006); the practice is standard workshop lore, sharpened.</sub>

Ban narrator-summary labels ('she felt furious', 'he was ashamed'). Permit — and exploit — the
character's own labelling, especially inaccurate labelling: 'I'm fine, she thought, and meant it.'
The distinction is not show-versus-tell; it is whose claim about the mind is on the page.

*Mechanism:* A narrator's label completes the inference and removes the reader's work; the felt experience of a
character's emotion in reading is largely the reader's own mind-reading activity, so pre-empting it
flattens affect. A character's self-label does the opposite: it adds a proposition the reader must
evaluate ('is she?'), which raises the inferential load rather than lowering it. This is the
precise, defensible version of 'show don't tell' — the received version, which bans emotion words
outright, throws away self-deception, the most useful interiority device there is.

**Work near, but not past, four levels of embedded intentionality** · <sub>useful · Lisa Zunshine, Why We Read Fiction: Theory of Mind and the Novel (2006). Note that critics have disputed both her level-counts and how much fiction actually embeds deeply.</sub>

Count the recursive nesting in a passage: 'Otto suspected that Hale believed the widow knew what the
report said' is four. Aim for three in scenes of social manoeuvre and drop to one or two in action
or grief. Zunshine reports that readers reliably follow about four levels and lose track beyond
that.

*Mechanism:* Recursive mind-reading is capacity-limited, and fiction's distinctive pleasure comes from operating
near that ceiling — which is why comedies of manners and spy novels feel cognitively delicious.
Below two levels a scene reads flat regardless of prose quality, because the reader's mind-reading
machinery is idling; past four, comprehension fails and the reader rereads or disengages. The number
is a design parameter, not a metaphor.

**Separate alignment from allegiance (the unlikeable-protagonist engine)** · <sub>load-bearing · Murray Smith, Engaging Characters: Fiction, Emotion, and the Cinema (1995); Nabokov, Paris Review interview with Herbert Gold, The Art of Fiction No. 40 (1967).</sub>

Smith's three-part structure of sympathy: recognition (the reader constructs a person), alignment
(spatio-temporal attachment plus access to what they know and feel), allegiance (moral evaluation).
For a repellent protagonist, drive alignment to maximum — total access, uninterrupted attachment —
and deliberately withhold allegiance. Nabokov did this openly, calling Humbert 'a vain and cruel
wretch who manages to appear touching,' and insisting the epithet properly belonged to the child.

*Mechanism:* Caring is not liking. Engagement is driven by information flow and attachment, not approval: if I
know what you know and see what you see, I am invested in what happens to you whatever I think of
you. Most 'likability' advice conflates the two channels and then tries to fix an allegiance problem
with alignment techniques (or vice versa). Naming the two channels lets you tune them independently
— which is how Ripley, Humbert, Macbeth and Sabbath work.

**'Is this character alive?' replaces 'is this character likeable?'** · <sub>strong · Claire Messud to Annasue McCleave Wilson, Publishers Weekly, 2013; the counter-tradition includes Patricia Highsmith on Ripley and Roxane Gay's 'Not Here to Make Friends.' The kindness-beat orthodoxy is Blake Snyder's Save the Cat!</sub>

Messud's reply, when asked whether she'd want to be friends with her protagonist: 'Would you want to
be friends with Humbert Humbert? … If you're reading to find friends, you're in deep trouble. We
read to find life… The relevant question isn't is this a potential friend for me? but is this
character alive?' Operational aliveness tests: does the character want something a reader would not
have predicted; do they notice things no one else in the book notices; can they be funny about their
own situation; do they ever act against their own interest for a reason they will not state.

*Mechanism:* Readers tolerate enormous moral distance provided the character's perception is generative — the
reader is paid, page by page, in noticing. Wit and specificity of attention are the currency; the
'save the cat' kindness beat is a much weaker substitute because it buys approval, which readers
discount as authorial management, rather than buying interest. Competence and idiosyncratic
attention outperform niceness in nearly every case.

**The antagonist wins one argument uncontested, on the merits** · <sub>load-bearing · John Truby, The Anatomy of Story (2007), on the opponent and the moral argument; Robert McKee, Story, on the forces of antagonism; Jean Renoir's Octave in La Règle du jeu (1939): 'the awful thing about life is this: everybody has their reasons.'</sub>

Somewhere in the book, the opposing position gets a win that no one rebuts and that events do not
falsify. Not a strong speech that the plot then punishes; an actual unanswered point. Truby's
structural version: the opponent must be the person best equipped to attack the hero's greatest
weakness and must carry a genuine moral argument, not merely obstruct.

*Mechanism:* Readers detect rigging with great sensitivity and respond by discounting the whole book's argument,
including the parts they agreed with. An unanswered point leaves a live question in the reader's
mind, and a live question is what keeps a reader thinking after the last page — which is the actual
goal of idea-driven fiction, as against agreement, which is forgotten within a week. It also raises
the protagonist: a hero is exactly as interesting as the pressure they survive.

**Steel-man by Rapoport's rules, then make someone pay for the position** · <sub>load-bearing · Anatol Rapoport's rules for successful criticism, popularised by Daniel Dennett in Intuition Pumps and Other Tools for Thinking (2013).</sub>

Two stages. Analytic: write the opposing worldview so its adherents would sign it; list what you
have learned from it; state explicitly where you agree; only then let your side speak. Dramatic: the
strongest version must be spoken by a character who has paid for it — whose body, career, marriage
or grief is the receipt. The argument arrives attached to a cost.

*Mechanism:* The analytic half fixes the writer's cognition: it is nearly impossible to argue against a position
you have just articulated at full strength, so the caricature never gets drafted. The dramatic half
fixes the reader's: readers assign belief to persons rather than to propositions, and a costly
signal (this person has suffered for holding this) is the cue our social cognition uses to rate
sincerity. A free opinion is discounted; a paid-for one is weighed.

**Shakespearise, don't Schillerise — the anti-mouthpiece rule** · <sub>load-bearing · Karl Marx to Ferdinand Lassalle, 19 April 1859, on Franz von Sickingen; Engels's related letter of 18 May 1859; Engels to Margaret Harkness (1888) on the author's opinions remaining hidden.</sub>

Marx's complaint to Lassalle about his verse drama: the fault is 'Schillerising,' using individuals
as mere mouthpieces for the spirit of the times, rather than 'Shakespearising.' Operational tests
for any idea-bearing character: is the argument inflected by their appetite, body, job and fear; are
they interrupted at least once; are they partly wrong about their own position; do they say it badly
at least once before saying it well; does someone in the room want something from them other than
the argument?

*Mechanism:* A position delivered without biographical friction is recognised by readers as the author clearing
their throat, and is filed as authorial opinion rather than as evidence about a person — at which
point it stops doing character work and starts doing essay work badly. Interruption and inarticulacy
are the cheapest available markers of embodiment: they prove the speech is being produced by a
person under conditions, not transcribed. The irony worth noting is that the sharpest anti-didactic
formulation in the tradition comes from its most doctrinaire quarter.

**Unfinalizability: never let the narration have the last word on a character** · <sub>load-bearing · Mikhail Bakhtin, Problems of Dostoevsky's Poetics (1929/1963).</sub>

Bakhtin's account of Dostoevsky: the major heroes are not only objects of authorial discourse but
subjects of their own directly signifying discourse, and the idea acquires power by fusing with an
unfinalized consciousness in dialogue with others. Operationally: the character must always be able
to say something about themselves that the narration has not already said; ideas are tested by
giving them to someone who will live them to the end; and no summary verdict on a major character is
ever allowed to be the final statement about them.

*Mechanism:* A character the reader can finish is a character the reader stops attending to — closure terminates
the mind-reading process that constitutes engagement. Withholding finalisation keeps the model open
and therefore keeps the reader working. For idea fiction specifically, it converts the idea from an
illustration (dead, since the reader already knows the verdict) into an event whose outcome is
genuinely pending, which is the only condition under which a reader will re-examine their own
position.

**Forster's test read correctly — and flatness as a resource, not a failure** · <sub>strong · E. M. Forster, Aspects of the Novel (1927); Alex Woloch, The One vs. the Many: Minor Characters and the Space of the Protagonist in the Novel (2003), on character-space, character-systems, and minor characters as the novel's 'proletariat.'</sub>

'The test of a round character is whether it is capable of surprising in a convincing way. If it
never surprises, it is flat.' Forster is describing a capacity for convincing surprise, not
psychological depth, and he defends flat characters as memorable, economical and never needing
reintroduction. Operationally: decide which characters are flat by design, and make them flat in a
direction — one specific, thwarted desire — rather than merely thin.

*Mechanism:* Readers cannot maintain many complex person-models at once; flatness is attention economy, and the
crowded-world effect of a great realist novel depends on it. Woloch's refinement explains why a
well-made minor character reads as a whole life glimpsed: the character-space is compressed by the
protagonist's dominance, and a single visible, frustrated want makes the reader infer the
compression rather than an absence — the flatness reads as distortion under pressure, which implies
the rest of the life off-page.

**Dimension as planted contradiction, paid off later** · <sub>strong · Robert McKee, Story (1997); Aristotle's Poetics licenses the inconsistent character provided they are 'consistently inconsistent.'</sub>

McKee: 'Dimension means contradiction: either within deep character (guilt-ridden ambition) or
between characterization and deep character (a charming thief)' — and the contradictions must be
consistent. Operationally, give each major character one contradiction pair, plant an early
behaviour that will only be fully explicable after the later surprise, and require that any
surprising act be retro-justifiable from at least two earlier details the reader can find on a
reread.

*Mechanism:* Surprise-plus-inevitability is a memory retrieval effect: the pleasure comes from successfully
retrieving an earlier detail and re-encoding it with new meaning. This dictates the craft
requirement precisely — the clue must be encoded on first pass (salient enough to store) but
misattributed (assigned to a different, plausible cause). A clue the reader did not notice yields no
retrieval and reads as cheating; a clue the reader interpreted correctly yields no surprise.

**Backstory placement: question before answer; wound behavioural before explanatory** · <sub>strong · The ghost/wound machinery: John Truby, The Anatomy of Story; K. M. Weiland, Creating Character Arcs. The corrective: Parul Sehgal, 'The Case Against the Trauma Plot,' The New Yorker, December 2021.</sub>

No backstory until the reader has formed a question it answers. The first appearance of a wound is
always behavioural — a refusal, an over-reaction, an incongruous competence — and the explanation is
withheld until the reader has been irritated by the behaviour. Best practice for the explanation
itself: let the wound account for the character's style (how they do everything) rather than the
content (what they do in this plot).

*Mechanism:* Unrequested information is encoded as description and forgotten; the same information supplied as an
answer to a live question is encoded as resolution and retained. This is the real content of the 'no
backstory in the first fifty pages' rule, and it means the rule can be broken freely if you have
manufactured the question in the first paragraph. Sehgal's critique supplies the second half:
trauma-as-explanation directs curiosity backward instead of forward, and 'flattens, distorts,
reduces character to symptom' — she also notes the research finding that post-traumatic growth is
far more common than post-traumatic stress, which makes the ubiquitous wound model a genre
convention wearing a lab coat.

**Want vs need, the Lie, and arc types — with the caveat** · <sub>contested · John Truby, The Anatomy of Story (2007); K. M. Weiland, Creating Character Arcs (2016); Lisa Cron, Story Genius (2016).</sub>

Truby's split: a psychological need is a flaw that hurts only the hero; a moral need is a flaw that
hurts others; both resolve at the self-revelation. Weiland's version: the Lie the character
believes, the Thing They Want versus the Thing They Need, and three arc shapes — positive change,
flat (the character is already right and changes the world), and negative (disillusionment, fall,
corruption). Lisa Cron's 'misbelief' adds the requirement that it originated in a specific past
moment where it was a reasonable conclusion.

*Mechanism:* The Lie converts theme from an atmosphere into a falsifiable proposition that plot can test, which
gives every scene a second axis of stakes: what did this cost the belief? It also gives the writer a
decision procedure at every fork — the character chooses in accordance with the Lie until the price
becomes unpayable. The caveat is real: this machinery is screenplay-derived, and applied uniformly
it produces books that all arc the same way and all end in articulated self-knowledge. Literary
practice from Bakhtin to Kundera to Murdoch resists exactly that closure, and the flat and negative
arcs are underused because the manuals are optimised for the positive one.

**The contradictory want: two legitimate desires that cannot both be satisfied** · <sub>strong · Aristotle, Poetics (character revealed by moral choice); Dwight Swain's 'dilemma' step of the sequel; the practice is standard in tragedy and unfashionably rare in commercial craft manuals.</sub>

Instead of (or alongside) want-versus-need, give the character two desires that are both defensible
and mutually exclusive — loyalty to a person and loyalty to the truth about that person; the
vocation and the marriage. The scene forces a choice; the choice is the characterisation.

*Mechanism:* Want-versus-need usually resolves to 'the character was wrong', which is a closed structure the
reader can predict from chapter three. Two legitimate wants stay open, because there is no correct
answer for the reader to anticipate, and because whatever the character chooses they lose something
the reader also valued. Aristotle located character in prohairesis — the choice made under
conditions where the choice is not obvious — and the mechanism is the same: choice under genuine
cost is the only reliable evidence about a person, in life and on the page.

**Chekhov's coldness: when the material is pitiable, cool the narration** · <sub>load-bearing · Anton Chekhov, letters (notably to Lydia Avilova, 1892) — commonly quoted in the form above; the same principle underlies Flaubert's impersonality and Hemingway's iceberg.</sub>

When writing grief, cruelty, or a character the reader is meant to pity, remove the narrator's
emotional endorsement entirely — flat syntax, concrete nouns, no adjectives of feeling, attention to
irrelevant physical detail. Chekhov's advice in his letters is commonly quoted in the form: when you
depict sad or unlucky people, try to be colder — it gives their grief a background against which it
stands out in greater relief.

*Mechanism:* Reader empathy is a reactive system that fires in response to a perceived state; when the narration
supplies the affect, the reader's own response is pre-empted, and the reader ends up evaluating the
author's feeling instead of having their own. This is the crowding-out mechanism behind
sentimentality: excess signal produces less feeling, not more. It is also why the coldest passages
in fiction are the ones people cry at.

**Staging: express desire spatially, and refuse the manufactured epiphany** · <sub>strong · Charles Baxter, The Art of Subtext: Beyond Plot (2007) on staging and the half-visible; 'Against Epiphanies' in Burning Down the House (1997).</sub>

Baxter's two disciplines. First, staging: where characters stand, who moves toward whom, what they
do with objects, who is holding what and who is looking away — desire rendered as geometry and
business. Second, resisting the epiphany: distrust the scene-ending realisation that arrives as an
insight the character can articulate.

*Mechanism:* Readers infer wanting from proximity and orientation faster and more reliably than from statement,
because that is how the inference works in life — we read approach and withdrawal before we read
words. And an epiphany substitutes an announcement for the reader's inference, which is the same
error as naming an emotion, committed at structural scale: it closes the character (see
unfinalizability) at exactly the moment the reader was most engaged in modelling them.

**The delete test for interiority** · <sub>strong · Composite: Swain's sequel logic, Cohn's mode distinctions, and standard developmental-editing practice. Stated as a mechanical test.</sub>

Delete each interior passage and ask what changes. Keep it only if it does at least one of three
things: contains a misreading of another character, produces a decision or refusal, or reveals
something the character is concealing from themselves. Cut everything that restates a feeling the
reader already inferred.

*Mechanism:* Readers implicitly track state change and allocate attention accordingly; interiority that changes
no state is processed as delay, and — this is the expensive part — it teaches the reader that
interiority in this book can be skimmed, which devalues your good passages retroactively. The three
retained categories are precisely the ones that change state: a misreading sets up a future
collision, a decision makes the next scene, and self-concealment adds a level of embedded
intentionality.

**Voice-first character development instead of the questionnaire** · <sub>useful · Elizabeth George, Write Away (2004); the discard-the-pages discipline is common workshop practice.</sub>

Abandon the character questionnaire (favourite colour, childhood pet). Instead write pages in the
character's own first person about anything at all — a complaint, a defence, a description of a room
— until the syntax stops sounding like yours, then throw the pages away. Elizabeth George's
'character analysis' is a long-form version of this, written in first person until the voice
arrives.

*Mechanism:* Questionnaires yield declarative facts, which are stored as author-knowledge and do not transfer
into prose — which is why writers with thick character bibles still produce thin characters. Voice-
first drafting encodes the character procedurally, in sentence rhythm, in what gets noticed first,
in what gets skipped — and procedural knowledge is what actually executes when you draft a scene. It
is the difference between knowing about someone and being able to do them.

**Kundera's counter-model: the character as experimental self, biography optional** · <sub>contested · Milan Kundera, The Art of the Novel (1986). Directly opposed to Truby/Weiland/Cron; both camps produce good books, and the disagreement is about what kind.</sub>

Rather than a life history, isolate three to five words that constitute the character's existential
problem (Kundera does this for Tereza: body, soul, vertigo, weakness, paradise) and construct the
character as a sequence of situations that interrogate those words. Kundera's position is that a
character is not a simulation of a living being but an imaginary being — an experimental self — and
that Kafka's K. functions perfectly with no past, no mother and no job.

*Mechanism:* For idea-driven fiction this is the escape hatch from the wound model: it lets a character be fully
specific without being psychologically explained, which preserves unfinalizability. Cognitively, it
also reorganises the writer's search — instead of retrieving biographical facts (which generate
exposition), you retrieve situations that stress a term (which generate scenes). The failure mode to
watch is the character becoming a term with legs, which is why this practice must be paired with the
anti-mouthpiece tests.

**Let the character resist the author's meaning (the opacity test)** · <sub>strong · Iris Murdoch, 'Against Dryness' (Encounter, 1961); her related claim that love is the extremely difficult realisation that something other than oneself is real.</sub>

Murdoch's demand in 'Against Dryness' is for novels containing real, various, free and opaque
persons, against both the crystalline (myth-like, allegorical) and the journalistic novel. The
working test: can you name at least one thing about each major character that does not serve your
theme, is not charming, and that you cannot repurpose? If everything about a character is load-
bearing for the argument, the character is scaffolding.

*Mechanism:* Readers detect total functional integration as design and respond by treating the person as a device
— engagement collapses into interpretation. Non-functional detail is the signal of independent
existence, which is what licenses the reader to treat the character as a person and therefore to
care. Murdoch is worth citing here partly because she admitted, repeatedly and honestly, that her
own novels fell short of this standard — which is the most useful thing anyone has said about how
hard it is.

**Give the reader knowledge the character lacks, early** · <sub>load-bearing · Alfred Hitchcock to François Truffaut (Hitchcock/Truffaut, 1966); the ancient version is Sophoclean irony.</sub>

Establish, in the first quarter, one thing the reader can see about the protagonist that the
protagonist cannot — the blind spot, the lie, the misreading of another character's motive.
Hitchcock's bomb-under-the-table formulation is the same device applied to plot: fifteen seconds of
surprise versus fifteen minutes of suspense, purchased by telling the audience.

*Mechanism:* Dramatic irony is the cheapest and most durable form of caring, and it works independently of
liking: once the reader knows something the character does not, every subsequent scene acquires a
second layer of stakes at zero cost in words. For character specifically, it converts the reader
from a spectator of the arc into someone waiting for a recognition — which is why the flat and
negative arcs remain gripping even though nobody improves.

### Non-obvious findings — Character, desire and interiority — what makes a r

- Forster's famous test is about surprise, not depth: 'The test of a round character is whether it is
  capable of surprising in a convincing way.' He also explicitly defends flat characters as
  memorable and economical. The standard classroom reading — round good, flat bad — inverts him.

- The sharpest anti-didactic rule in the tradition was written by Karl Marx. In his letter to Lassalle
  of 19 April 1859 he complains of 'Schillerising' — using individuals as mere mouthpieces for the
  spirit of the times — and demands Shakespearising instead. The most doctrinaire quarter of
  literary theory produced the best formulation of why characters must not carry doctrine.

- Alignment and allegiance are separable channels (Murray Smith, Engaging Characters, 1995), and the
  entire 'likeable protagonist' debate conflates them. You can drive a reader's investment to
  maximum through access and attachment while withholding all moral approval — which is what Nabokov
  did deliberately, calling Humbert 'a vain and cruel wretch who manages to appear touching.' Caring
  is an information-flow phenomenon, not a moral one.

- 'Show, don't tell' is wrong about emotion in a specific, correctable way. Naming an emotion is fatal
  when the narrator does it (it completes the reader's inference) and valuable when the character
  does it (it adds a claim the reader must evaluate, and opens the possibility of self-deception).
  The blanket ban on emotion words throws away the single most useful interiority device.

- Zunshine's finding gives interiority a design parameter, not a metaphor: readers reliably follow
  about four levels of embedded intentionality and lose track beyond. Below two levels, social
  scenes read flat regardless of prose quality — the reader's mind-reading machinery is idling.

- The wound-and-backstory model is a recent genre convention wearing a lab coat. Sehgal notes that
  research finds post-traumatic growth far more common than post-traumatic stress, and that the
  trauma plot 'flattens, distorts, reduces character to symptom' while pointing curiosity backwards.
  The mandatory ghost in the Truby/Weiland/Cron lineage is a convention, not a psychological law.

- Donnellan's argument that playing an internal objective is a trap — 'the target is always outside,'
  specific, measurable, and constantly changing, and is emphatically not an objective, want, motive
  or goal. Novelists who write desire as a mood are making precisely the mistake he spent a book
  diagnosing in actors.

- Woloch's account reframes thin minor characters as a distribution problem, not a failure of
  imagination: character-space is finite and the protagonist's dominance distorts everyone else. The
  fix for a flat minor character is therefore one visible thwarted desire (which makes the flatness
  read as compression, implying a life off-page), not more description.

- Dwight Swain's Motivation-Reaction Unit ordering — feeling, then involuntary action, then speech,
  always after an external stimulus, ordered from most involuntary to most intentional — is widely
  mocked as mechanical and is the single most reliable line-level fix for 'this emotion feels fake.'
  It works because it matches actual processing latency.

- Kundera's position that characters need no biography at all — the character is 'an experimental
  self,' not a simulation of a living being, and Kafka's K. has no past, no mother and no job — is a
  live and defensible alternative to the entire want/need/wound apparatus, particularly for idea-
  driven fiction where the wound model tends to explain the idea away.

- Mamet's denial that character exists at all ('there are just lines on a page') is not a provocation
  to be dismissed but a medium-specific truth: on stage and screen, where interiority cannot be
  rendered, character is entirely constructed by the audience from actions. It is wrong for the
  novel precisely because free indirect style exists. The schools are not disagreeing about taste;
  they are working in different media and neither says so.

- Character questionnaires are actively counterproductive. They produce declarative facts, which are
  stored as author-knowledge and do not execute during drafting — which is why writers with thick
  character bibles still produce thin characters. Voice-first free-writing encodes the character
  procedurally, in rhythm and in what gets noticed, and that is what actually runs when you write a
  scene.

- Chekhov's 'be colder' — restraint increases reader emotion rather than diminishing it, because
  empathy is a reactive system and narratorial affect pre-empts the reader's own. This is the
  mechanism of sentimentality: excess signal produces less feeling, not more.

- Bakhtin's method for idea-driven fiction is the opposite of the screenwriting 'thematic argument':
  give the idea to someone who will live it to the end, let their self-consciousness exceed any
  verdict the author could pass on them, and never finalise them. Ideas become events between
  consciousnesses rather than illustrations of a conclusion — and a character the reader can finish
  is a character the reader stops attending to.

- Aristotle both subordinates character to action and licenses the surprising character: an
  inconsistent character is permitted provided they are 'consistently inconsistent.' The ancient
  authority most often cited for plot-primacy also supplied the escape clause for characters who
  behave unpredictably.

- Suzanne Keen's Empathy and the Novel (2007) casts serious doubt on the empathy-altruism
  justification for fiction: there is little reliable evidence that novel-reading makes readers
  behave better. The practical implication for craft is that 'making the reader care' should be
  engineered as a problem of sustained attention and investment, not of moral improvement — which is
  a more honest and more tractable target.

## The cognitive science of writing and creativity — process models of composition (Flower & Hayes, Hayes, Kellogg), knowledge-telling vs. knowledge-transforming (Bereiter & Scardamalia) and its rival (Galbraith), creativity research on serial order/fixation/incubation, memory research on generation and encoding, construal-level theory in editing, and psycholinguistics of reader processing — translated into operational practice for a working novelist, with the mechanism stated for every rule.

**Run two problem spaces, not one: force the rhetorical problem to rewrite the content problem** · <sub>load-bearing · Carl Bereiter & Marlene Scardamalia, 'The Psychology of Written Composition' (1987); the two-problem-space architecture is theirs. The recursive planning/translating/reviewing substrate is Linda Flower & John R. Hayes, 'A Cognitive Process Theory of Writing', College Composition and Communication 32 (1981).</sub>

Keep two explicit, separately-maintained representations while drafting: (a) the content space —
what is true in this world, what happened, what the character wants; (b) the rhetorical space — what
this chapter must do to a reader who has read only chapters 1-8. Operationally: after each scene,
write two sentences, one for each space, and then deliberately look for a conflict between them.
When the rhetorical demand ('the reader must not yet believe her') cannot be met by the content as
currently known, the correct move is to change what is true in the world, not to add narration.
Bereiter & Scardamalia's diagnostic is that in knowledge-telling, information flows one way (memory
-> page) in retrieval order; in knowledge-transforming, the flow is bidirectional and the text
problem alters the knowledge.

*Mechanism:* Novices compose by cue-driven retrieval: a topic cue activates associated content in semantic
memory, which is emitted in activation order, and each emitted item becomes the cue for the next.
That produces locally coherent, globally shapeless prose, and it is fluent — which is why it feels
like it is working. Knowledge-transforming inserts a problem-solving loop between retrieval and
transcription: the rhetorical constraint acts as a filter that rejects the highest-activation
candidate, which forces a second retrieval pass with a modified cue, which surfaces material the
first pass could not reach. The gain is not 'better writing'; it is access to content that pure
retrieval order never returns. This is also why transforming is effortful in a way telling is not —
the loop runs in working memory.

**Discard the first tier of ideas by policy, not by taste** · <sub>load-bearing · Serial order effect: Christensen, Guilford & Wilson (1957); associative account, Sarnoff Mednick (1962). Executive account: Roger E. Beaty & Paul J. Silvia, 'Why do ideas get more creative across time?', Psychology of Aesthetics, Creativity, and the Arts 6 (2012).</sub>

For any generative decision (premise, the antagonist's real motive, how a scene ends, a metaphor),
require a fixed count of candidates — 15-20 — before evaluating any of them, and adopt a standing
rule that candidates 1-5 are not eligible unless they survive a second, later pass. Log the
candidates in generation order so the ordinal is recoverable. Do not evaluate mid-list; evaluation
stops generation.

*Mechanism:* The serial-order effect: in divergent-thinking tasks, later responses are reliably more original
than earlier ones. Two mechanisms compete and both are probably real. Mednick's associative account:
retrieval traverses semantic memory by spreading activation from the cue, so early responses are the
high-frequency, strongly-associated, prototypical members of the category, and remoteness (hence
originality) increases with search depth. Beaty & Silvia's executive account: later responses
reflect deliberate strategy — category switching, suppression of the obvious, interference
management — so the effect is partly a signature of controlled retrieval, not passive spread.
Practically, both predict the same policy: the conventional centre of the category is what arrives
first, and it arrives feeling right precisely because it is fluent. Note Beaty & Silvia's finding
that high fluid intelligence flattens the slope: strong ideators start further out, so a talented
writer's tier-1 ideas are better than a novice's — but they are still that writer's most
conventional ideas.

**Generate before exposure — including exposure to a language model** · <sub>load-bearing · Steven M. Smith, Thomas B. Ward & Jay S. Schumacher, 'Constraining effects of examples in a creative generation task', Memory & Cognition 21 (1993), 837-845. Design fixation: Jansson & Smith (1991). LLM homogenization: Anil R. Doshi & Oliver P. Hauser, 'Generative AI enhances individual creativity but reduces the collective diversity of novel content', Science Advances (2024), DOI 10.1126/sciadv.adn5290.</sub>

Ordering rule: produce your own candidate list, in writing, before reading comparable novels,
editorial suggestions, research notes on how others handled the problem, or any model-generated
draft. When you do consult, consult after your list exists and is timestamped. If you have already
been exposed, do not assume a break will clear it; instead, explicitly enumerate the features of
what you saw and ban them for the next generation round.

*Mechanism:* Unintentional conformity. Smith, Ward & Schumacher had subjects sketch new exemplars of a category
after seeing three examples; across three experiments the designs of exposed subjects reliably
contained features of the examples, more examples produced more conformity, and — this is the
operational point — a 23-minute interpolated task did not significantly reduce the effect. Exposure
does not merely suggest; it re-weights the retrieval distribution so that example features become
the high-activation candidates, and it does so below the threshold of intention, so the writer
experiences the conforming idea as their own. The mechanism explains why 'read widely in your genre
before drafting' is dangerous advice at the wrong moment and good advice at the right one. Applied
to models: an LLM samples from a frequency-shaped distribution, so its first outputs are,
structurally, tier-1 ideas — the conventional centre of the category — and reading them installs
exactly the fixation Smith et al. measured. Doshi & Hauser found the empirical consequence at
population scale: writers given LLM story ideas produced stories rated more creative individually,
but measurably more similar to each other.

**Inject arbitrary constraints to leave the prototype** · <sub>strong · Patricia D. Stokes, 'Creativity from Constraint: The Psychology of Breakthrough' (2006). Compatible with the Geneplore model — Ronald Finke, Thomas Ward & Steven Smith, 'Creative Cognition' (1992) — in which preinventive structures are generated then explored under constraint.</sub>

When search stalls at the conventional centre, add a constraint that is not derived from the story's
needs: the scene must contain no dialogue; the reveal must happen to someone who is not present; the
chapter must be told by the least informed character; the villain must be right. The constraint is
disposable — it is a search heuristic, not a commitment. Keep what the constraint surfaced; discard
the constraint.

*Mechanism:* Unconstrained search returns the prototype because the prototype has the highest activation and
satisfies every constraint trivially. An arbitrary constraint makes the prototype inadmissible,
which forces the retrieval process to re-enter semantic memory with a modified cue set — the same
mechanism that produces the serial-order effect, but induced deliberately rather than waited out.
Patricia Stokes's framing is that constraints work by 'precluding' the habitual response and thereby
directing search to a region that would otherwise never be sampled. This is the same reason formal
verse produces images the poet would not have found in free verse: the rhyme requirement is a random
re-seeding of the search.

**Ideate alone first; pool second (nominal-group ordering for writing groups and editors)** · <sub>strong · Michael Diehl & Wolfgang Stroebe, 'Productivity loss in brainstorming groups: Toward the solution of a riddle', Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 53 (1987). Contra Alex Osborn's original brainstorming prescription (1953), which the evidence does not support in its group form.</sub>

In any collaborative session — writers' group, editorial meeting, conversation with a spouse who
reads drafts — everyone writes their candidate list privately and silently first, then the lists are
combined. Never open with open discussion. Same rule applies to a session with a model: your list,
then its list, then the merge.

*Mechanism:* Production blocking. Diehl & Stroebe's decomposition of the brainstorming literature showed that
interacting groups reliably generate fewer and less varied ideas than the same number of individuals
working separately ('nominal groups'), and the dominant cause is not evaluation apprehension or
social loafing but the simple serial constraint that only one person can speak at a time: while
waiting, participants rehearse their own idea (blocking further generation) or forget it, and
meanwhile the speaker's content re-cues everyone's retrieval toward a common region — the conformity
effect operating in real time. The pooled-after-private ordering preserves independent search paths,
which is the only thing that produces variance in the candidate set.

**Dose incubation with a low-demand task, not with rest and not with another hard problem** · <sub>load-bearing · Ut Na Sio & Thomas C. Ormerod, 'Does incubation enhance problem solving? A meta-analytic review', Psychological Bulletin 135 (2009), 94-120. Fixation-forgetting account: Steven M. Smith & Steven Blankenship (1991). Descriptive four-stage frame: Graham Wallas, 'The Art of Thought' (1926) — folklore that turned out partly right.</sub>

When stuck on a structural problem, stop and do something undemanding and non-verbal for 20-60
minutes: walking, driving a known route, washing up, weeding, swimming. Not: napping in silence, and
not: answering email, reading, or working on a different hard scene. Before you break, spend real
time on the problem — the incubation payoff scales with preparation. Keep a capture method within
reach, since the return is often a partial reconfiguration rather than a sentence.

*Mechanism:* Sio & Ormerod's meta-analysis of 117 studies found a positive incubation effect (mean d ≈ 0.29),
with divergent-thinking tasks benefiting more than insight tasks, longer preparation producing
larger effects, and — the counterintuitive finding — an undemanding interpolated task outperforming
both rest and a demanding task. The proposed mechanisms are (a) selective forgetting / fixation
decay: the misleading initial representation loses activation faster than the problem's core
elements, so the return is to a less-constrained problem space; (b) spreading activation continuing
subthreshold during the break, recruiting remote associates; (c) set-shifting on return, because re-
encoding the problem after a delay produces a different initial parse. A demanding task suppresses
(b); complete rest permits rumination, which refreshes (a) — you keep reactivating the fixated
representation. The undemanding task occupies just enough executive resource to prevent rumination
while leaving associative processing free.

**Harvest sleep onset (N1), and only N1** · <sub>strong · Célia Lacaux, Thomas Andrillon, et al., 'Sleep onset is a creative sweet spot', Science Advances (2021), DOI 10.1126/sciadv.abj5866. Related: Ullrich Wagner et al., 'Sleep inspires insight', Nature 427 (2004), where sleep more than doubled hidden-rule discovery.</sub>

Load the problem deliberately, then nap sitting up holding an object (Edison's spoon; Dalí's key),
or set an alarm for roughly 10-15 minutes. Capture whatever is present on waking, immediately and
without editing. The point is to wake at the edge of sleep, not after it.

*Mechanism:* Lacaux et al. exposed 103 participants to problems containing a hidden shortcut rule. Spending at
least 15 seconds in NREM stage 1 tripled the chance of discovering the rule (83% vs 30% for those
who stayed awake) — and the benefit vanished in participants who reached deeper N2 sleep. The
proposed mechanism is that N1 combines still-accessible waking memory contents with loosened
associative constraints and reduced top-down inhibition: semantic activation spreads further than in
wake, but the material is still retrievable on arousal, which deeper sleep destroys. This is a
narrow window, which is why the folklore technique (hold something you will drop) is well designed —
the drop wakes you at the boundary.

**Schedule structural and creative work at your NON-optimal time of day** · <sub>strong · Mareike B. Wieth & Rose T. Zacks, 'Time of day effects on problem solving: When the non-optimal is optimal', Thinking & Reasoning 17 (2011), 387-401.</sub>

Invert the standard advice. Do the work that requires holding a system in mind and detecting
alternatives — restructuring, finding what a book is actually about, breaking a plot deadlock — at
your circadian trough (late evening for a morning person; early morning for an owl). Reserve peak
hours for work that rewards focus and inhibition: line editing, continuity checking, research, the
mechanical parts of drafting.

*Mechanism:* The synchrony effect, inverted. Wieth & Zacks had participants solve insight and analytic problems
at optimal and non-optimal times of day; insight performance was consistently better at the non-
optimal time, while analytic performance showed no consistent time-of-day effect. The mechanism is
reduced inhibitory control: at your trough, the executive filter that suppresses weakly-activated,
tangentially-related material is weaker, so remote associates that would normally be screened out
reach awareness. That is a liability for proofreading (you also fail to suppress the expected word)
and an asset for restructuring. This is the same variable the serial-order and incubation effects
turn on, manipulated by clock rather than by task.

**Brief daily sessions, and stop while you still know the next sentence** · <sub>strong · Robert Boice, 'Procrastination, busyness and bingeing', Behaviour Research and Therapy 27 (1989); 'Contingency management in writing and the appearance of creative ideas', Behaviour Research and Therapy 21 (1983). Stopping rule: Ernest Hemingway, 'Monologue to the Maestro: A High Seas Letter', Esquire, October 1935 — 'The best way is always to stop when you are going good and when you know what will happen next.' Interrupted-task memory: Bluma Zeigarnik (1927).</sub>

Two joined rules. (1) Write in short, scheduled, near-daily sessions (30-90 minutes) with logged
time, rather than saving work for long uninterrupted blocks. (2) End each session mid-momentum — at
a point where you know what happens next, ideally mid-scene or mid-sentence — rather than at a
natural stopping point. Write two lines of instruction to tomorrow's self before you close the file.

*Mechanism:* For (1), Boice's comparison of binge writers with matched novice academics on brief daily sessions
found the binge group produced less overall, got fewer acceptances, scored higher on the Beck
Depression Inventory, and — the finding that matters here — listed fewer creative ideas for writing.
His contingency-management condition produced the highest creative-idea output of all. The proposed
mechanism is that habitual short sessions keep the project in a state of chronic partial activation,
so incubation runs continuously in the gaps and re-entry costs stay low; binges require a full
reload of the problem representation each time and end in fatigue, which degrades exactly the
executive control that knowledge-transforming needs. For (2), the mechanism is the persistence of
activation for interrupted goals (the Zeigarnik principle) plus the elimination of the blank-page
re-entry problem: a session that begins with a known next move never pays the cost of reconstructing
the local plan in working memory. Caveat: Boice's studies are small, sparsely reported, and have
been re-analysed critically; treat the direction as reliable and the magnitudes as not. The
Zeigarnik effect itself has a mixed replication record — the practice survives on the re-entry
mechanism alone.

**Treat outlining and freewriting as different machines with different outputs, and never run them at once** · <sub>load-bearing · Ronald T. Kellogg, 'Attentional overload and writing performance: Effects of rough draft and outline strategies', JEP:LMC 14 (1988), 355-365. David Galbraith, 'Writing as a knowledge-constituting process' (1999) and 'The Work of Writing: Raiding the Inarticulate', Educational Psychologist (2018). Freewriting as method: Peter Elbow, 'Writing Without Teachers' (1973).</sub>

Use outlining to reduce load and improve organisation once you know roughly what you think. Use
continuous, unstoppable text production — no editing, no lookback — when you need to discover what
you think. Never outline during a discovery pass, and never freewrite during an arrangement pass. If
you are an intuitive, exploratory writer, outlining before you have drafted will cost you material
you cannot recover later.

*Mechanism:* This is the live disagreement in the field and both sides are right about different dependent
variables. Kellogg (1988) found that outlining before drafting did not improve fluency but did
improve text quality, by offloading high-level planning from working memory so that translating and
reviewing have capacity available. Galbraith's dual-process model contests the assumption
underneath: he argues text production is itself knowledge-constituting — content is synthesised on
the fly by constraint satisfaction over an implicit semantic network, not retrieved ready-made from
episodic memory. His experiments compared low and high self-monitors and found that low self-
monitors produce more genuinely new ideas after writing full text than after outlining, and that
only their new content is associated with development of the writer's own understanding; outline
planning raised organisational change without enhancing knowledge. Resolution: outlining optimises
arrangement and reduces cognitive load; unconstrained production is what generates content the
writer did not know they had. A novelist needs both, sequenced — and the Galbraith result predicts
that habitual outliners with a discovery-oriented temperament are systematically losing material.

**Externalise the goal stack so working memory can be spent on prose** · <sub>strong · Ronald T. Kellogg, 'Writing and working memory: A summary of theories and of findings' (2001) and the SCRIPTKELL dual-task programme; Deborah McCutchen's capacity theory of writing (1996). Working-memory architecture: Alan Baddeley.</sub>

Before a drafting session, write down — on paper, visible — the three to five active constraints for
this scene: what the reader knows, what must change, what must not be revealed, the emotional
target, the pending setups this scene can pay off. Keep a separate running list of open loops
(unresolved setups, promised payoffs, unexplained absences) outside the manuscript. Do not hold any
of this in your head.

*Mechanism:* Kellogg's dual-task studies establish that composing loads executive attention heavily and that the
load is additive across planning, translating and reviewing; when total demand exceeds capacity, one
process is silently starved — and it is almost always reviewing and global planning, because
sentence-level translation has the most immediate cue pressure. Externalising the goal stack
converts a maintenance load (costly, decaying, interference-prone) into a perceptual retrieval
(nearly free). This is the same reason expert chess players use position schemas rather than piece-
by-piece maintenance: expertise partly consists of reducing what must be held. McCutchen's capacity
theory makes the developmental version of the claim — transcription fluency and schema automation
free capacity for higher-level processes, which is why practice raises quality even without any new
knowledge about craft.

**Produce volume, because quality is not separable from quantity** · <sub>strong · Dean Keith Simonton, 'Creative productivity: A predictive and explanatory model of career trajectories and landmarks', Psychological Review 104 (1997); the equal-odds rule appears throughout his work from the mid-1980s.</sub>

Aim at total output — finished scenes, finished chapters, finished books — rather than at
guaranteeing that each unit is good. Accept a high discard rate as the cost of the process, not as
evidence of failure. Do not attempt to pre-select which idea, scene, or book will be the good one.

*Mechanism:* Simonton's equal-odds rule: within a creator's career, the ratio of high-impact to total works does
not reliably rise with experience or eminence; the eminent are eminent because they produce more,
and their successes and failures are drawn from the same distribution. The implication is that
quality is a probabilistic function of quantity and that the creator has poor ex ante discrimination
between their own hits and misses — which is empirically the case. This intersects with the serial-
order effect: high output means deeper search per problem and more problems searched. It also
predicts that a writer who refuses to write anything they are not sure is good will have
systematically fewer good things, because the selection step they trust does not work at the moment
of generation.

**The reconstruction drill: deliberate practice with a real feedback signal** · <sub>strong · Benjamin Franklin's Autobiography (the Spectator method) is the classic protocol. Deliberate-practice framework: K. Anders Ericsson, Krampe & Tesch-Römer (1993); applied to writing by Ronald T. Kellogg, 'Training writing skills: A cognitive developmental perspective', Journal of Writing Research 1 (2008) and Kellogg & Whiteford, 'Training advanced writing skills: The case for deliberate practice' (2009). Verbatim/gist: Jacqueline Sachs (1967).</sub>

Take a passage by a writer whose effects you want (2-4 pages). Read it, take only skeleton notes —
who wants what, what changes, in what order — then wait long enough to lose the wording (a day is
enough; a week is better). Rewrite the passage from the notes in your own prose. Then compare line
by line against the original and write down, specifically, every place the original made a choice
you did not consider. Do this on a schedule against varied models. Franklin's version added a
shuffling step: he scrambled his notes before rewriting, to practise arrangement separately.

*Mechanism:* Ordinary writing practice fails Ericsson's criteria for deliberate practice on one axis
specifically: no immediate, informative feedback. Reader response arrives years late, aggregated,
and confounded. The reconstruction drill manufactures the missing signal — the original is a ground
truth against which every choice is diagnosable, and the comparison isolates the decisions you did
not know were decisions, which is the definition of the skill gap. It also exploits the generation
effect: because you produced the passage yourself, the discrepancies are encoded far more durably
than they would be from reading a critique. And the delay before rewriting forces reconstruction
from gist rather than surface form (memory for verbatim wording decays within seconds to minutes
while gist persists — Sachs, 1967), so you are practising the actual craft operation, not
transcription.

**Maintain an explicit model of the reader's evolving mental representation (knowledge-crafting)** · <sub>load-bearing · Ronald T. Kellogg (2008), knowledge-crafting stage. Curse of knowledge: Colin Camerer, George Loewenstein & Martin Weber (1989); Elizabeth Newton's 1990 Stanford dissertation (tappers/listeners). Egocentric anchoring: Boaz Keysar and colleagues. Popularised as a theory of bad prose in Steven Pinker, 'The Sense of Style' (2014).</sub>

Keep a running document, per chapter, of what the reader now believes, what they suspect, what they
have forgotten, and what they have been promised. Update it as a separate pass, not while drafting.
At each major reveal, check the document rather than your own knowledge. Where you cannot decide
whether something has landed, this is the one thing readers cannot be replaced on — test it.

*Mechanism:* Kellogg's three-stage developmental model puts knowledge-crafting above knowledge-transforming: the
expert manipulates three representations concurrently in working memory — planned content, the text
as written, and the prospective reader's interpretation of that text — and this is the stage most
writers never reach, because it requires enough automation elsewhere to afford a third
representation. The default failure is the curse of knowledge: once you know the twist, you cannot
reconstruct not knowing it, and privileged knowledge intrudes on judgments about what others know
(Camerer, Loewenstein & Weber's economic demonstration; Elizabeth Newton's tappers-and-listeners
study, where tappers predicted ~50% recognition and got ~2.5%). Keysar's work shows the correction
is late and effortful — speakers and writers are egocentric by default and only adjust afterwards,
which means audience design cannot be done during composition. It has to be a scheduled separate
pass with an external record, precisely because introspection is the faulty instrument.

**Use readers as detectors, never as diagnosticians** · <sub>load-bearing · John R. Hayes, Linda Flower, Karen Schriver, James Stratman & Linda Carey, 'Cognitive processes in revision' (1987). Quote: Neil Gaiman, rules for writers, The Guardian (2010).</sub>

Ask readers only for location and experience: where were you bored, where did you skim, where did
you reread, where did you stop believing her, where did you put the book down. Record the
coordinates. Discard, systematically, their proposed causes and their proposed fixes — including
your own first diagnosis, which is usually a rationalisation of the passage you already wrote. Then
diagnose independently, and consider that the cause frequently lies upstream of the reported
location.

*Mechanism:* Hayes, Flower, Schriver and colleagues decomposed revision into detect → diagnose → operate, and
showed the bottleneck is detection: writers can generally repair problems they have detected, and
fail because they do not detect. Readers are excellent detection instruments — their reading times,
rereads and abandonments are direct behavioural measures of processing difficulty — and poor
diagnostic instruments, because they have no access to the causal structure and will confabulate a
local cause for a global failure. Neil Gaiman's formulation of the same asymmetry is exact: 'when
people tell you something's wrong or doesn't work for them, they are almost always right. When they
tell you exactly what they think is wrong and how to fix it, they are almost always wrong.' The
upstream point follows from situation-model theory: a reader who disbelieves a decision on page 200
is usually reporting a failure of setup on page 60, because the belief was never installed.

**Revise for shape before revising for words — and know that the difference is a detection difference** · <sub>load-bearing · Nancy Sommers, 'Revision Strategies of Student Writers and Experienced Adult Writers', College Composition and Communication 31 (1980), 378-388. Lester Faigley & Stephen Witte, 'Analyzing revision', CCC 32 (1981).</sub>

Structure the revision as an ordered sequence of passes with a declared object: (1) shape — what is
the book about, what is the sequence of changes, what scenes exist; (2) scene function — what each
scene does to the reader; (3) paragraph and reference; (4) sentence and word. Do not permit word-
level work during pass 1; it is not merely wasteful, it manufactures sunk cost in sentences that
should be deleted. If you find yourself reaching for a thesaurus during pass 1, the pass has failed.

*Mechanism:* Sommers's think-aloud comparison of 20 first-year students with experienced adult writers
(academics, editors, journalists) found students conceived revision as a 'rewording activity'
governed by a 'thesaurus philosophy of writing' — words as the unit of discourse, revision as
lexical substitution — while experienced writers described their primary revision goal as 'finding
the form or shape of their argument', 'a framework', 'a pattern', 'a design'. Faigley & Witte's
taxonomy formalises the distinction as surface changes vs. text-base (meaning-changing) changes. The
mechanism behind the difference is not skill at fixing but scale of perception: the novice's
representation of the text is a sequence of sentences, so sentences are the only objects available
to operate on. Sequencing passes by declared object forces the coarse representation to be built,
which is what makes coarse problems detectable at all. It also protects against the specific failure
where a beautifully-revised sentence survives into the final draft because it was too good to cut,
in a scene that should not exist.

**Switch construal level deliberately between passes — distance for structure, proximity for the line** · <sub>strong · Yaacov Trope & Nira Liberman, 'Construal-level theory of psychological distance', Psychological Review 117 (2010). Jens Förster, Ronald S. Friedman & Nira Liberman, 'Temporal construal effects on abstract and concrete thinking: Consequences for insight and creative cognition', JPSP 87 (2004), 177-189.</sub>

Before a structural pass, run a distancing induction: describe the book in one sentence as if to a
stranger; imagine a reader encountering it in fifteen years, or in another country, or as a
translation; read the synopsis rather than the prose. Before a line pass, run a proximity induction:
read aloud in the room, in the character's present tense, at reading speed, on the medium the reader
will use. Never attempt both in the same sitting.

*Mechanism:* Construal-level theory: psychological distance on any dimension — temporal, spatial, social,
hypothetical — raises the level of construal, so distant objects are represented abstractly by their
central, goal-relevant features and near objects concretely by their incidental, contextual
features. Förster, Friedman & Liberman had participants imagine their lives one year hence versus
the next day and then attempt insight problems; the distant-future group solved substantially more
(means of 1.28 vs 0.35 in the reported comparison). The craft consequence is direct: structural
problems are gestalt problems whose features are only visible at high construal, and line problems
are texture problems only visible at low construal, so a writer trying to see both simultaneously is
guaranteed to see one. Distance also strips the writer's privileged low-level knowledge — the
