reasons a scene exists, the intended effect — which is exactly the knowledge that blocks the curse-
of-knowledge correction. Caveat: some distance-creativity effects have had a mixed replication
record; the pass-separation practice is worth doing on the construal argument alone even if the
effect sizes are smaller than reported.

**Defeat perceptual expectation before proofreading — change the surface, not just the date** · <sub>load-bearing · Meredyth Daneman & Murray Stainton, 'The generation effect in reading and proofreading: Is it easier or harder to detect errors in one's own writing?', Reading and Writing 5 (1993), 297-313. Douglas Hacker, Plumb, Butterfield, Quathamer & Heineken, 'Text revision: Detection and correction of errors', Journal of Educational Psychology 86 (1994). Eye movements in reading: Keith Rayner.</sub>

Never proofread the text in the form you wrote it. Change the typeface, column width and line
breaks; print it; read on a different device; have it read aloud by text-to-speech at slightly-too-
fast a speed; read paragraphs in reverse order for spelling and line order forward for sense. The
delay ('come back tomorrow') is necessary but nowhere near sufficient.

*Mechanism:* The own-text proofreading deficit is not simple familiarity — it is authorship. Daneman & Stainton
found subjects were worse at detecting errors in essays they had generated themselves than in
unfamiliar other-generated essays, yet better at detecting errors in familiar other-generated essays
than in unfamiliar ones. Familiarity helps; authorship hurts. The explanation is that reading is a
prediction process — eye-movement research shows readers do not fixate every word, extract
parafoveal preview, and resolve identification top-down — and the author holds an unusually strong
internal representation of the intended text, which supplies the predicted word and suppresses the
evidence that the actual word differs. Changing the physical form of the text disrupts the visual
and rhythmic cues that trigger the memorised prediction, forcing more bottom-up processing. Machine
speech is the strongest single instrument because it removes your prosody, your pacing and your
visual layout at once, and because it will read what is on the page rather than what you meant.
Note: the fashionable 'disfluent fonts improve learning' finding (Diemand-Yauman et al., 2011) has
largely failed to replicate at scale — do not extend this practice into a general claim that hard-
to-read is better.

**Read aloud at performance speed as a rhythm and breath instrument** · <sub>strong · Long-standing practitioner method (Flaubert's 'gueuloir'; recommended by nearly every working editor). Mechanism from implicit prosody research — Fodor's Implicit Prosody Hypothesis (2002) — and from the perceptual-expectation account of proofreading above.</sub>

Read the draft aloud, standing, at the pace an audiobook narrator would use — not skimming, not
muttering. Mark every place you ran out of breath, stumbled, had to reread to get the intonation
right, or heard yourself getting bored. Do not fix while reading; mark and continue.

*Mechanism:* Two distinct mechanisms, both real. (1) Prosodic externalisation: silent reading generates implicit
prosody, but the writer's implicit prosody is the intended one; producing the text aloud imposes
actual articulatory and respiratory constraints, so sentences whose syntax fights their intonation
contour become physically detectable. A stumble is a garden path you cannot feel silently because
you already know the parse. (2) Attention to a different representation: reading aloud forces
serial, complete word identification, defeating the same top-down prediction described above. The
breath measure is not mysticism — clause length interacts with respiratory phrasing, and prose that
consistently outruns a breath is prose the reader's implicit prosody is also failing to phrase. This
is why the practice is near-universal among professional prose stylists and why it survives despite
sounding like folklore.

**Run a manuscript pre-mortem before the final structural pass** · <sub>strong · Deborah J. Mitchell, J. Edward Russo & Nancy Pennington, 'Back to the future: Temporal perspective in the explanation of events', Journal of Behavioral Decision Making 2 (1989), 25-38. Applied technique: Gary Klein, 'Performing a project premortem', Harvard Business Review (2007).</sub>

Sit down and write, as established fact: 'This book was published and it failed. Readers put it down
at chapter four. Reviewers said—.' Then generate reasons for twenty minutes without evaluating them.
Do the same for the near-miss version: 'It nearly worked but one thing was wrong.' Only afterwards
check which of the generated reasons are actually true of the draft.

*Mechanism:* Prospective hindsight. Mitchell, Russo & Pennington found that framing an event as having already
occurred — going forward in time and looking back — increased the number of correctly identified
reasons for outcomes by roughly 30% relative to the standard prospective framing. The mechanism is
that a certain past event licenses explanation-generation (a well-practised retrieval mode with rich
cue structure), whereas an uncertain future event licenses only prediction, which is generatively
poor and inhibited by not wanting the answer to be true. It also removes the motivated-reasoning
brake: you are no longer arguing about whether the book will fail, only about why it did. Klein's
premortem is the applied form. This is the single cheapest instrument for converting a writer's
diffuse unease about a draft into a specific, addressable list.

**Make the reader generate the inference — the actual mechanism behind 'show, don't tell'** · <sub>load-bearing · Norman J. Slamecka & Peter Graf, 'The generation effect: Delineation of a phenomenon', JEP: Human Learning and Memory 4 (1978), 592-604. The craft rule is commonly attributed to Chekhov and to Percy Lubbock's 'The Craft of Fiction' (1921), usually without its mechanism.</sub>

For any conclusion you want the reader to hold with conviction — that a marriage is over, that a
narrator is lying, that a character is afraid — supply the evidence and delete the conclusion.
Concretely: cut the sentence that names the emotion or states the judgment, and check that at least
two independent pieces of evidence remain. Calibrate: the inference must be reachable by an
averagely attentive reader on one pass, because an inference nobody draws is not subtext, it is an
omission.

*Mechanism:* The generation effect: material a person generates themselves is remembered substantially better
than the identical material read passively — Slamecka & Graf demonstrated it across five experiments
and it has replicated widely across materials and tests. The proposed mechanisms are increased
processing effort and, more importantly, the activation of a richer, more distinctive set of
semantic relations during generation, plus attribution of the conclusion to the self rather than to
an external source. That last part is the craft payoff: a conclusion the reader constructed is one
the reader owns and will defend, whereas a conclusion the narrator asserted is one the reader can
decline. So the real reason 'telling' is weak is not that it is unvivid — it is that it removes the
reader's generation opportunity and thereby both the encoding advantage and the ownership. This also
gives the boundary condition that the rule normally lacks: when a conclusion is not one the reader
needs to own — routine transitions, connective information, elapsed time — telling is correct and
showing is waste.

**Order every sentence given-before-new, and put the payload in the stress position** · <sub>load-bearing · Susan Haviland & Herbert H. Clark, 'What's new? Acquiring new information as a process in comprehension', Journal of Verbal Learning and Verbal Behavior 13 (1974). George D. Gopen & Judith A. Swan, 'The Science of Scientific Writing', American Scientist 78 (1990) — the topic-position/stress-position formulation.</sub>

Two joined rules operating at the sentence. (1) Open with material the reader already has — a
referent, a time, a place, a proposition just asserted — and close with what is new. (2) Put the
information you want emphasised at the end of the sentence, and the information you want
subordinated in the middle. Diagnostic pass: read only the first six words of every sentence in a
paragraph and check that they form a continuous thread; read only the last six and check that they
form the argument.

*Mechanism:* Haviland & Clark's given-new contract: comprehension proceeds by identifying the given information,
locating its antecedent in memory, and attaching the new information to it. When the given element
is absent or delayed, the reader must construct a bridging inference, and reading times rise
measurably — the cost is real and it accumulates invisibly across a chapter as low-grade friction
the reader experiences as the prose being 'heavy'. The stress position is an independent effect:
end-of-clause is where prosodic prominence falls and where working memory closure occurs, so
material there is encoded more strongly. Gopen & Swan's contribution was to show these are reader
expectations rather than stylistic preferences — readers assign emphasis by position regardless of
what the writer intended, so a writer who buries the payload mid-sentence is not being subtle, they
are being misread.

**Reference economy: use the pronoun when the character is the centre, and never elegantly vary a referent** · <sub>strong · Peter C. Gordon, Barbara J. Grosz & Laura A. Gilliom, 'Pronouns, names, and the centering of attention in discourse', Cognitive Science 17 (1993), 311-347. Morton Ann Gernsbacher & David Hargreaves, 'Accessing sentence participants: The advantage of first mention', Journal of Memory and Language 27 (1988), 699-717; Structure Building Framework, Gernsbacher (1990). 'Elegant variation' as a fault: H. W. Fowler (1926) — right for the wrong reasons.</sub>

When a character is the established discourse centre — the subject of the preceding sentence, or its
first-mentioned participant — refer to them with a pronoun. Do not repeat the proper name 'for
clarity'. Never rotate referring expressions for variety ('Sarah… the blonde woman… the detective…
the younger Hallam'). Repeat the name only when the centre has genuinely shifted, when a competitor
referent has intervened, or when you want a deliberate beat of formality or distance. And place the
character the scene is about in the first-mention slot of the scene's first sentence.

*Mechanism:* Two robust findings, both cutting against received copy-editing wisdom. (1) The repeated-name
penalty: Gordon, Grosz & Gilliom found that sentences with repeated-name subject anaphors are read
more slowly than sentences with pronoun subjects when the antecedent was the subject or first-
mentioned NP of the previous sentence. The centering account is that a full name signals to the
reader that the centre has shifted; when it has not, the reader performs a re-identification that
then has to be undone. So the well-meant repetition costs time and induces a false discourse-
structural signal. (2) The advantage of first mention: Gernsbacher & Hargreaves showed across seven
experiments that first-mentioned participants are more accessible in memory, independent of agency
or surface sentence-initial position, because the substructure representing the first clause becomes
the foundation on which the rest of the representation is built. Whoever you name first owns the
paragraph. Elegant variation compounds both failures: each new description is a new referring
expression that must be resolved against the discourse model, and the reader cannot tell whether the
variation is signalling a shift of perspective or is merely decorative.

**Manage the five situation-model indices at every scene joint** · <sub>load-bearing · Rolf A. Zwaan, Mark C. Langston & Arthur C. Graesser, 'The construction of situation models in narrative comprehension: An event-indexing model', Psychological Science 6 (1995). Underlying framework: Walter Kintsch & Teun van Dijk (1978); Zwaan & Radvansky (1998).</sub>

At each scene break, chapter opening, and time skip, explicitly check which of five things have
changed — time, space, protagonist, causality, intentionality (goals) — and orient the reader on the
changed ones inside the first two sentences, in that order of priority: protagonist, then time, then
space. Where three or more shift at once, either supply a full orienting sentence or split the
transition. Where you want disorientation, shift several deliberately and know you are spending the
reader's attention to buy it.

*Mechanism:* Zwaan, Langston & Graesser's event-indexing model: readers build a situation model in which events
are indexed on five dimensions, and integrating a new event is easier to the degree it shares
indices with the current one. Reading times rise measurably at discontinuities — the effect is
reliable for protagonist shifts and temporal shifts, weaker for spatial shifts, which is a useful
asymmetry: moving a character to a new room is cheap; changing whose head we are in, or jumping
three weeks, is expensive. When indices shift without being marked, readers do not fail loudly; they
build a wrong model and carry it, which surfaces pages later as 'I got confused' pointing at the
wrong location. This gives a principled basis for scene-break conventions that are usually taught as
arbitrary house style, and it explains why the standard advice to open a chapter by grounding the
reader in whose body and when is not throat-clearing — it is index repair.

**Write for good-enough processing: never put load-bearing information inside a hard parse** · <sub>strong · Fernanda Ferreira, Karl G. D. Bailey & Vittoria Ferraro, 'Good-enough representations in language comprehension', Current Directions in Psychological Science 11 (2002), 11-15; Ferreira & Patson (2007). Semantic illusions: Thomas Erickson & Mark Mattson (1981). Garden paths: Thomas Bever (1970); Lyn Frazier & Keith Rayner (1982).</sub>

Identify the sentences carrying information the plot depends on — a name, a time, a causal link, a
reveal — and check that each is syntactically simple, unambiguous locally, and positioned
prominently (sentence end, paragraph end, or its own paragraph). Eliminate reduced relative clauses,
stacked modifiers and long subject-verb separations from those sentences specifically.
Simultaneously: stop rewriting for continuity errors of the kind readers demonstrably do not notice,
and spend that effort on structural coherence instead.

*Mechanism:* Ferreira, Bailey & Ferraro's good-enough account: comprehension is often partial and semantic
representations often incomplete; readers frequently obtain a shallow understanding and sometimes
outright misunderstand, and the misinterpretation persists — the initial, wrong parse of a garden-
path sentence is not always cleanly revised away, so readers can leave a sentence holding a meaning
it does not have. The Moses illusion (Erickson & Mattson, 1981 — readers answer 'How many animals of
each kind did Moses take on the ark?' without noticing it was Noah) shows the same shallowness at
the semantic level: readers match to a good-enough approximation rather than verifying. Two craft
consequences follow and they point in opposite directions. First, you cannot rely on a reader having
correctly parsed a difficult sentence, so anything the plot depends on must survive shallow
processing — hence syntactic simplicity plus positional prominence for load-bearing content. Second,
readers glide over inconsistencies that are semantically close to expectation, which means
continuity slips are far less costly than novelists fear, while incoherence in the situation model —
the thing shallow processing is actually built on — is far more costly.

### Non-obvious findings — The cognitive science of writing and creativity — 

- The standard copy-editing instinct is backwards on pronouns. Gordon, Grosz & Gilliom's repeated-name
  penalty shows that repeating a character's name where a pronoun would serve makes sentences read
  MORE slowly when that character is the discourse centre — the full name falsely signals a shift of
  attention that the reader then has to undo. 'Repeat the name for clarity' imposes a measurable
  cost in exactly the case where it feels most helpful.

- Do your creative and structural work at your WORST time of day. Wieth & Zacks found consistently
  better insight-problem performance at participants' non-optimal circadian time, with no consistent
  effect on analytic problems — reduced inhibitory control lets weakly-activated remote associates
  through. This inverts the near-universal advice to protect your peak hours for your hardest work;
  peak hours are for proofreading and continuity, not for breaking a plot.

- Incubation works better if you do something mildly occupying than if you rest. Sio & Ormerod's meta-
  analysis of 117 studies found undemanding interpolated tasks beat both demanding tasks and
  complete rest. 'Sit quietly with the problem' is worse than 'wash the dishes', probably because
  rest permits rumination, which keeps refreshing the fixated representation you need to forget.

- You cannot wait out contamination from having read someone else's version. In Smith, Ward &
  Schumacher, a 23-minute interpolated task did not significantly reduce the conformity effect from
  exposure to three examples, and more examples produced more conformity. This is the strongest
  argument against reading a model's draft 'just to see what it does' before you have generated your
  own — the fix is ordering, not delay.

- Outlining and idea generation trade off against each other, and the trade is temperament-dependent.
  Kellogg found outlining improves text quality; Galbraith found that outline planning raises
  organisational change without enhancing the writer's knowledge, and that low self-monitors
  generate more genuinely new ideas by writing full text than by outlining — with only their newly-
  produced content associated with development of their own understanding. The literatures are not
  contradictory; they measure different outputs. An intuitive writer who has been trained to outline
  is losing material and will never see what they lost.

- The 10,000-hours framing is badly oversold. Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald's meta-analysis found
  deliberate practice explained 26% of performance variance in games, 21% in music, 18% in sports,
  4% in education and less than 1% in professions. Writing sits at the professions end. Practice
  matters — but the operative variable for a writer is almost certainly the quality of the feedback
  loop, which ordinary writing practice essentially lacks, not the hour count.

- The proofreading deficit is caused by authorship, not familiarity. Daneman & Stainton found subjects
  were WORSE at detecting errors in essays they had written themselves than in unfamiliar essays by
  others, yet BETTER at detecting errors in familiar other-generated essays than in unfamiliar ones.
  Familiarity helps; having generated the text hurts. So 'come back to it with fresh eyes tomorrow'
  addresses the wrong variable — a delay reduces recency but does not remove authorship, which is
  why the surface of the text has to be physically changed.

- Boice's binge writers listed FEWER creative ideas, not merely fewer words — and scored higher on the
  Beck Depression Inventory and got fewer acceptances than matched writers doing brief daily
  sessions. The romantic model of the intense multi-day immersion is associated with lower
  ideational output, not higher. (Caveat: small, thinly-reported studies; trust the direction, not
  the numbers.)

- Fifteen seconds of stage-1 sleep tripled discovery of a hidden rule in Lacaux et al. (83% vs 30%),
  and the effect vanished entirely in participants who reached deeper sleep. The Edison spoon trick
  has a real and very narrow window — this is one of the rare cases where a piece of artist folklore
  turned out to be mechanistically precise about timing.

- Purely imaginative distance changes what you can see. Förster, Friedman & Liberman had participants
  merely imagine their life one year hence rather than tomorrow, and insight-problem solutions rose
  from a mean of 0.35 to 1.28. This means the cheapest available revision instrument — imagining a
  stranger reading the book in fifteen years, before you open the file — is manipulating the same
  variable as putting the manuscript in a drawer for three months.

- Doshi & Hauser found LLM story ideas made individual stories rate as more creative, better written
  and more enjoyable — with the largest gains for the LEAST creative writers — while making the
  stories measurably more similar to one another. The tool's benefit is concentrated where a
  professional novelist does not need it, and its cost falls precisely on the axis the professional
  competes on: distinctiveness.

- Kosmyna et al.'s EEG study found LLM-assisted essay writers showed the weakest neural connectivity,
  reported the lowest ownership of their essays, and struggled to accurately quote their own work.
  That is the generation effect running in reverse: text you did not generate is not encoded in your
  model of the document — which for a novelist means AI-drafted passages are structurally invisible
  during later revision, degrading exactly the global representation that structural revision
  depends on. (Preprint, N=54 with only 18 in the crossover session; treat as suggestive.)

- Readers routinely fail to notice semantic anomalies — the Moses illusion, and Ferreira's good-enough
  processing, where misinterpretations of garden-path sentences persist rather than being cleanly
  repaired. The practical consequence is a reallocation: continuity errors matter far less than
  novelists fear (readers are matching to approximation, not verifying), while incoherence in the
  situation model matters far more, because shallow processing has nothing else to run on.

- Sommers's key finding is not that students revise badly but that they revise at the wrong grain
  because the wrong grain is the only one they can perceive — students described revision as
  rewording and used a 'thesaurus philosophy of writing', while experienced writers described
  looking for 'the form or shape of their argument'. Combined with Hayes et al.'s
  detect/diagnose/operate decomposition, the implication is that revision skill is overwhelmingly a
  DETECTION skill; teaching people to fix things they cannot see is wasted effort, and building
  external detection instruments (readers, machine speech, pre-mortems, distance) is the whole game.

- Group brainstorming is worse than the same people working alone and pooling afterwards (Diehl &
  Stroebe's nominal-group superiority, driven mainly by production blocking). Writers' groups and
  editorial meetings that open with discussion are structurally guaranteed to converge; the fix is a
  silent private-generation phase first, which costs nothing.

- Reduced complexity raises, not lowers, readers' estimate of the author's intelligence. Oppenheimer's
  'Consequences of erudite vernacular utilized irrespective of necessity' (2006) found that
  needlessly complex vocabulary lowered judged intelligence of the author, mediated by processing
  fluency. Related: the rhyme-as-reason effect (McGlone & Tofighbakhsh, 2000), where sonically
  patterned aphorisms are judged more TRUE than semantically identical unrhymed paraphrases — so
  voice has an epistemic effect on readers, not merely an aesthetic one.

## Failure taxonomy and the market contract — what actually gets books rejected, what makes readers quit, and where "literary quality" and "finishable" come apart

**The Slushkiller ladder: know which rung you are actually on** · <sub>load-bearing · Teresa Nielsen Hayden, 'Slushkiller,' Making Light (Feb 2004). Reproduced widely; John Scalzi's 'Ten Things About Literary Rejection' (2005) is a companion.</sub>

Teresa Nielsen Hayden's 14-step taxonomy of why manuscripts get rejected (Making Light, 2004),
ordered from most to least obvious. Abridged: (1) author functionally illiterate; (2) wrong kind of
book for this house; (3) formatting/typographic derangement; (4) 'on bad terms with the Muse of
Language' — parts of speech wrong, confusion-of-motion images; (5) sentences fine, paragraphs
incoherent; (6) can't tell when the subject has changed; (7) competent paragraphs and a functional
plot, but 'hackneyed, dull, and pointless'; (8) needs therapy more than publication; (9) 'nobody but
the author is ever going to care about this dull, flaccid, underperforming book'; (10) engaging
plot, but it isn't the author's — everyone's seen that movie; (11) 'someone could publish this book,
but we don't see why it should be us'; (12) talented author, wrong book; (13) good book the house
can't support; (14) buy this book. Operationally: diagnose which rung your manuscript sits on,
because the fix differs completely at each.

*Mechanism:* It separates line-level competence from book-level necessity, which writers habitually conflate.
Rungs 1–6 are craft failures fixable by revision; rung 7 is the killer — everything is technically
correct and nothing is interesting; rungs 9–13 are failures of proposition, not execution, and
cannot be fixed by more polishing. Most serious writers plateau at 7 and respond by improving
sentences, which is the one intervention that provably does nothing at that rung. The ladder works
because it forces the question 'what class of problem is this?' before 'what edit do I make?'

**Triage in the reader's order of dismissal (first-five-pages discipline)** · <sub>load-bearing · Noah Lukeman, The First Five Pages: A Writer's Guide to Staying Out of the Rejection Pile (Simon & Schuster, 2000).</sub>

Noah Lukeman arranged The First Five Pages (2000) explicitly 'in the order of what I look for when
trying to dismiss a manuscript' — presentation, then adjectives/adverbs, sound (rhythm, repetition),
comparison (metaphor/simile), style, then dialogue (tedious, unrealistic, on-the-nose), then the
bigger picture (characterization, hooks, subtlety, pacing and progression). The practice: revise
your own opening in exactly that order, because that is the sequence in which a reader looks for
permission to stop.

*Mechanism:* Gatekeepers are running a fast rejection heuristic, not an appreciation routine. Each early defect
is cheap to detect and, empirically, correlates with defects deeper in — so a reader stops on the
cheap signal and never tests the expensive one. This means opening-page defects are not weighted by
their intrinsic seriousness; they are weighted by their position in the dismissal sequence. Fixing a
page-200 structural problem raises quality; fixing a page-2 rhythm problem raises the probability
that anyone reaches page 200.

**The Turkey City Lexicon as a shared diagnostic vocabulary** · <sub>load-bearing · Turkey City Lexicon, compiled by Lewis Shiner (1st ed., 1988) and Bruce Sterling (2nd ed.); named for the Austin, TX workshop that was a cradle of cyberpunk. Hosted by SFWA.</sub>

A named-pathology list compiled for SF workshops: Infodump; As You Know Bob ('a pernicious form of
info-dump through dialogue, in which characters tell each other things they already know'); White
Room Syndrome; Brenda Starr Dialogue ('long sections of talk with no physical background');
Dischism; Signal from Fred; Squid in the Mouth; Squid on the Mantelpiece; Idiot Plot; Second-order
Idiot Plot; Card Tricks in the Dark; Bogus Alternatives; Fuzz ('an element of motivation the author
was too lazy to supply — "somehow" signals fuzzy areas'); Countersinking; Steam-Grommet Factory; You
Can't Fire Me, I Quit; Jar of Tang; Used Furniture; Space Western; Funny-hat Characterization;
Roget's Disease; Said Bookism; Burly Detective Syndrome; Gingerbread; Viewpoint Glitch; Eyeball
Kick; Ontological Riff; Abbess Phone Home; Not Simultaneous. Practice: keep it as a literal
checklist and pass the manuscript against it.

*Mechanism:* The Lexicon's own stated rationale is the mechanism: 'having an accurate and descriptive critical
term for a common SF problem makes it easier to recognize and discuss.' Naming converts a diffuse
feeling ('this is boring') into a located, falsifiable claim ('this is an As You Know Bob on p.14'),
which is what makes it actionable. Cognitively, the name is a retrieval cue — once you have 'Signal
from Fred' in memory you start catching it while drafting, not only while revising, which moves the
cost of the error from rewrite to keystroke.

**White Room Syndrome is a failure of invention, not of description** · <sub>load-bearing · Turkey City Lexicon (Shiner/Sterling). The 'Brenda Starr Dialogue' entry is the same failure in dialogue-only form.</sub>

Turkey City: the white room is 'a featureless set for which details have yet to be invented — a
failure of invention by the author... "She awoke in a white room." The white room is obviously the
white piece of paper confronting the author.' Note the diagnostic pairing: the character wakes up in
order to ponder her circumstances, which supplies an excuse for an infodump. The cure is not adding
adjectives; it is deciding what is true about the place, then letting the scene's action collide
with those facts.

*Mechanism:* Readers build a spatial model and run the action inside it; without a model, dialogue and blocking
have nowhere to happen and the reader's simulation stalls. Adding decorative detail to a room you
haven't invented produces Gingerbread (Turkey City: 'useless ornament'), which reads as evasion. The
reliable cure works because invented facts generate obstacles: a low ceiling, a door that only opens
inward, a listening neighbour. Setting becomes load-bearing only when it constrains behaviour.

**Grep your own boredom: Dischism and Signal from Fred** · <sub>strong · Turkey City Lexicon (Dischism named for critic Thomas M. Disch, who diagnosed it).</sub>

Dischism is 'the unwitting intrusion of the author's physical surroundings, or the author's own
mental state, into the text' — characters who are confused and indecisive because the author is;
endless cigarettes and drinks. Signal from Fred is 'a comic form of the Dischism in which the
author's subconscious makes unwitting critical comments' — a character saying 'this was getting
tedious,' 'he had heard all this before,' 'she wished he would get to the point.' Practice: run a
literal text search for tedious/boring/pointless/got to the point/heard this before/at
last/finally/dragged on, plus interiority verbs of confusion, and treat every hit as a marked scene.

*Mechanism:* These phrases are the writer's own reader-model leaking into the prose while the conscious mind is
defending the draft. They are unusually honest because they are involuntary. Empirically they
cluster exactly where the writer was writing on obligation rather than on interest, which is also
where readers quit — so the phrase is a free, mechanically detectable proxy for a felt state the
writer is otherwise motivated to deny.

**The but/therefore connector audit** · <sub>load-bearing · Trey Parker and Matt Stone, NYU storytelling seminar. Compatible with Robert McKee's 'gap between expectation and result' (Story, 1997) and with Aristotle's preference in Poetics for events arising 'because of' rather than 'after' one another.</sub>

Write the story out as a list of beats, then insert the connector between each pair. If 'and then'
fits, the beat is inert. Trey Parker and Matt Stone (NYU Tisch storytelling seminar, filmed for
mtvU's Stand In, 2011/2014): 'This happened, and then this happened, and then this happens — that's
not a movie, that's not a story.' Every beat should be joined by 'but' or 'therefore.' Practice as a
revision pass: one line per scene, connector between each, and rewrite or delete any 'and then' run.

*Mechanism:* It converts an unfalsifiable question ('is this scene working?') into a syntactic test that takes
seconds and cannot be argued with. The underlying reason it works: causal and adversative links
create prediction. 'Therefore' makes the reader forecast a consequence; 'but' violates a forecast
they just made. 'And then' asks the reader to hold new material with no predictive stake — which is
the precise cognitive state we experience as boredom. This is also the sharpest available detector
for the false-tension chapter: a chapter can be full of shouting and still be an 'and then.'

**Value-charge polarity per scene (the sagging-middle detector)** · <sub>load-bearing · Shawn Coyne, The Story Grid (2015); the value-shift-per-scene principle is McKee's (Story, 1997) before him. Jack Bickham (Scene & Structure) and Dwight Swain (Techniques of the Selling Writer) supply the scene/sequel and motivation-reaction machinery.</sub>

Shawn Coyne's Story Grid method: every genre has a core value (life/death, love/hate,
justice/injustice, in idea-driven SF often ignorance/knowledge or illusion/truth), and every scene
must end on a different charge than it began — positive to negative or the reverse. Build a
spreadsheet: scene number, word count, value at stake, charge in, charge out. A sagging middle
appears as a run of scenes whose charge does not flip, or flips trivially and flips back.

*Mechanism:* The 'sagging middle' is usually misdiagnosed as a pacing or energy problem and treated with added
incident, which makes it worse. Mechanically it is a run of scenes with no net state change: the
reader's model of the situation at scene 20 is identical to the model at scene 14. Because reading
is prediction-and-update, a scene that produces no update is experienced as a cost with no payoff.
Charting the charge makes the flat run visible as a literal column of identical signs, which no
amount of local prose energy can disguise.

**Micro-tension on every page** · <sub>load-bearing · Donald Maass, The Fire in Fiction (2009), ch. 8; developed further in Writing the Breakout Novel and The Emotional Craft of Fiction.</sub>

Donald Maass: micro-tension is 'the moment-by-moment tension that keeps the reader in a constant
state of suspense over what will happen, not in the story but in the next few seconds.' Its source
is conflicting emotions or ideas inside the point-of-view character, not external threat. Practice:
open the manuscript to any page at random; if nothing on that page is in contradiction with
something else on that page, the page is a candidate for cutting. Applies to description and
backstory too — Maass's claim is that readers won't care about any of it unless it carries micro-
tension.

*Mechanism:* This is the cure for the false-tension chapter and for 'talking heads.' Global stakes decay across a
page: knowing the world may end does not make paragraph 4 interesting. What sustains attention at
the paragraph scale is unresolved local contradiction, because contradiction blocks the reader's
model from settling. It also explains why quiet literary scenes can out-grip action scenes: a
character who wants two incompatible things generates more per-second uncertainty than a car chase
whose outcome the genre has already guaranteed.

**Match the ending to the question you opened (MICE)** · <sub>load-bearing · Orson Scott Card, Characters and Viewpoint / How to Write Science Fiction and Fantasy (1990); nesting/'MICE quotient as code brackets' extension by Mary Robinette Kowal.</sub>

Orson Scott Card's MICE quotient: a story is dominantly Milieu, Idea, Character, or Event, and each
type has a determined ending. A Milieu story ends when the character leaves the place. An Idea story
ends when the question posed at the start is answered. A Character story ends when the character's
role/identity changes. An Event story ends when the disrupted order is restored or replaced. Mary
Robinette Kowal extends this to nesting: threads open and must close in reverse order, like
brackets. Practice for literary SF: write down the exact interrogative sentence your first chapter
opens, and check that your last chapter closes that sentence and not a different one.

*Mechanism:* This is the single most useful structural tool for idea-driven SF, because the characteristic
structural failure of the form is an Idea story whose question gets answered at 60% and then
continues for 40,000 more words on inertia. The reader's sense of an ending is not a feeling about
page count; it is the closure of the specific uncertainty the opening installed. Conversely, an Idea
book that resolves the concept but leaves the character question dangling reads as 'clever but cold'
— reviewers will say 'the characters felt thin' when the real fault is an unclosed bracket.

**The genre contract: conventions and obligatory scenes** · <sub>load-bearing · Shawn Coyne, The Story Grid. Compatible with John Truby's 'genre beats' and with Blake Snyder's genre taxonomy in Save the Cat.</sub>

Coyne's formulation: genre is a contract. Each genre carries conventions (recurring roles, settings,
requirements — e.g. the mentor, the MacGuffin, a clock) and obligatory scenes (the scenes readers of
that genre will feel cheated without — the hero at the mercy of the villain in a thriller; the
lovers' first meeting and the proof-of-love in romance; the 'discovery of the body' and the
'exposure of the criminal' in crime). The practice is to list, before drafting, the obligatory
scenes of every genre you are invoking, and to check the draft against that list. For literary SF,
the obligatory scene is usually the moment the novum's full implication is confronted by a person
who cannot escape it.

*Mechanism:* Coyne's key observation: a reader 'may not be able to pinpoint what exactly it is they want from a
story, but they know when it's not there.' Genre expectations are procedural memory built from a
lifetime of narrative; violating them produces dissatisfaction the reader cannot name and will
therefore attribute to something else ('boring,' 'pretentious'). This is why negative reviews are
usually wrong about the cause. The upside is that a delivered obligatory scene is the cheapest
satisfaction available — and it can be delivered in an unexpected key, which is exactly what good
literary genre work does.

**Deliver the promise of the premise** · <sub>strong · Blake Snyder, Save the Cat! (2005).</sub>

Blake Snyder's 'Fun and Games' beat (Save the Cat, 2005), roughly pp. 30–55 of a screenplay, is
where 'the promise of the premise' plays out — the trailer section, the part the audience bought a
ticket for. In a novel this is the first third-to-half of Act II. Diagnostic: write the one-line
premise, then find the pages that deliver it. If the detective doesn't do detective work, if the
ghost never haunts, if the time machine is used twice in 400 pages, the premise is undelivered — a
top-five agent rejection reason.

*Mechanism:* The premise creates a specific appetite and the reader waits for it to be fed. 'Undelivered premise'
is felt as betrayal rather than boredom, which is worse — it produces one-star reviews from readers
who finished. For idea-driven SF the failure is particularly common in a specific form: the author,
having found the concept obvious, skips the exploration and jumps to the meta-commentary. The reader
never got the exploration. The rule is that the writer's boredom with their own idea arrives roughly
200 pages before the reader's.

**The back-cover-copy spine test** · <sub>load-bearing · Standard acquisitions-editor practice; formalized in Randy Ingermanson's Snowflake Method (step 1) and Coyne's Foolscap Global Story Grid. The commercial analogue is Amazon's 'working backwards' press-release-first memo.</sub>

Before drafting, and again after, write the 150-word jacket/back-cover copy: protagonist, their
situation, the disturbance, the antagonistic force, the cost of failing, and the flavour promise. If
you cannot write it without abstraction ('a meditation on memory and loss'), the book has no spine.
The specific failure to look for is inability to name the antagonistic force in a concrete noun
phrase. Related: Randy Ingermanson's Snowflake Method starts at step 1 with a single 15-word
sentence; Story Grid's 'Foolscap' compresses a novel onto one page.

*Mechanism:* Compression is a structural assay. A novel with causal spine compresses without losing sense; a
novel that is a sequence of accomplished scenes turns to mush under compression, and the mush is
diagnostic, not a limitation of the summary form. Writing the copy also forces you into the reader's
decision frame — the reader is not deciding whether your prose is good, they are deciding whether
the proposition is worth 12 hours. Second-order benefit: the copy you write in advance is a
commitment device that catches drift, because a draft that no longer matches its own jacket copy has
wandered.

**Comp titles as a positioning argument, not flattery** · <sub>strong · Jane Friedman, 'Comps Can Clinch Your Query' and her query-letter guidance (janefriedman.com).</sub>

Choose two comparable titles published within roughly the last two to three years, by authors still
actively publishing, that share something structurally major with your book and have demonstrated
audience. Do not use Harry Potter, Jack Reacher, or anything else enormous — Jane Friedman's point
is that huge comps signal you did not take the exercise seriously. Avoid unknown self-published
comps when querying agents. Formulate as 'X for readers of Y' only when the join is real.

*Mechanism:* Comps are shorthand for a P&L: they tell the acquiring editor what shelf, what print run, what
marketing template, and what previous sales curve to model against. They also work backwards on the
writer: choosing honest comps forces you to state what your book actually is, which frequently
exposes that you have written two half-books. The recency requirement exists because the pipeline is
slow — a Big Five acquisition typically publishes about two years out, so a comp from 2015 describes
a market that will be a decade stale at your pub date.

**Respect length norms, and know which direction the exceptions run** · <sub>useful · Composite of current agent/industry guidance (Reedsy, Writer's Digest, agency submission pages); KU/KENP economics from Amazon KDP's per-page-read royalty structure.</sub>

Adult debut fiction: roughly 70k–100k words is the uncontroversial band. SF/fantasy runs longer,
roughly 90k–120k, with averages around 100k–115k; mystery/thriller 80k–100k; romance 70k–90k;
literary fiction is penalized above about 120k. Over 120k from a first-time author is a hard sell,
because printing cost scales with length while a debut's sales do not. Under ~50k is a novella and
has no trade slot. The exception that inverts: in Kindle Unlimited, where authors are paid per page
read (KENP), long books and long series are economically favoured — so the trad norm and the KU norm
point in opposite directions.

*Mechanism:* Length is read by acquisitions as a proxy for craft control, independent of the economics. A
190k-word debut says 'this writer cannot cut,' which correlates strongly enough with true structural
looseness that the heuristic survives. So the norm functions twice: as a real cost constraint and as
a competence signal. Knowing which market you are in matters more than the number: the same 160k
manuscript is an auto-reject in a query pile and an asset in a KU series.

**Audit the sample window — the first 10% and pages 20–100** · <sub>strong · Jellybooks reader analytics, Andrew Rhomberg (reported via Jane Friedman, Publishing Perspectives, NPR). Amazon does not publish an exact sample percentage; ~10% is the widely observed figure.</sub>

Amazon's free sample is roughly the first tenth of the book. Treat it as a hard structural boundary:
compute where 10% falls in your word count and ensure a genuine turn — a reversal, an escalation, or
a reveal that changes the reader's model — lands inside it, and that the sample does not end mid-
lull. Jellybooks' reader-tracking data (Andrew Rhomberg) adds a second window: for male readers the
hooked-or-not decision falls roughly between pages 20 and 50; for female readers roughly between
pages 60 and 100. So a literary SF novel aimed at a mixed readership needs two hooks, not one.

*Mechanism:* The sample is a purchase decision, not a reading experience; it is evaluated against opportunity
cost among a dozen other samples. Peak readership occurs in Chapter 1 and declines through the first
couple of chapters — that decline is the abandonment curve, and every structural decision in the
first 10% either flattens it or steepens it. The two-window finding matters because the received
'hook them on page one' rule optimizes only the first window and can produce books that hook then
leak: an opening set-piece followed by 60 pages of consolidation is precisely the shape that
survives window one and dies in window two.

**Completion rate is the real leading indicator, and it is not correlated with prestige** · <sub>strong · Jellybooks (jellybooks.com, completion-rate benchmarks); Kobo Book Report 2014; Jordan Ellenberg, 'The Summer's Most Unread Book Is...', Wall Street Journal, 3 July 2014.</sub>

Jellybooks' benchmarks from tracked reader cohorts: under 25% completion is weak engagement; 25–50%
is a good result and is where the majority of books land; over 50% is very good; over 75% is
exceptional — fewer than 5% of titles they tested reach it, and those correlate highly with
performing very well in the market. Against that: Kobo's 2014 data showed only 44.4% of British Kobo
readers finished Donna Tartt's Pulitzer-winning The Goldfinch, and 28.2% finished Twelve Years a
Slave. Jordan Ellenberg's 'Hawking Index' (WSJ, 3 July 2014), estimating reach from the position of
Kindle's top-five popular highlights, put Piketty's Capital at about 2.4%.

*Mechanism:* Completion is the closest available proxy for the thing that actually sells books over time — word
of mouth — because a reader who did not finish does not recommend, and a reader who finished at 3am
does. Sampling, cover, and reviews move first-week sales; completion moves the long tail and the
next book. The Goldfinch number is the load-bearing fact for a literary novelist: prize recognition
and finishability are close to orthogonal, and a book can win the Pulitzer while more than half its
buyers put it down. This means 'literary' is not an excuse for a low completion rate, and also that
a low completion rate is not proof of literary seriousness.

**The mouthpiece audit: dramatize the counterargument** · <sub>load-bearing · Anton Chekhov, letter to A. S. Suvorin, 27 Oct 1888. Failure exemplars via the SF Encyclopedia entries on Rand and Heinlein.</sub>

Identify every character who exists to say what you think. Then apply the test: does any character
get to be right about something the author believes is wrong, and win the scene on the merits? If
not, you have a lecture with quotation marks. Standard remedies: give the strongest version of the
opposing case to the most attractive character; make the mouthpiece pay a cost for being right; put
the idea in the mouth of someone with a motive to lie about it; or convert the argument into an
action whose consequence, not whose rhetoric, settles it. Canonical failures: John Galt's speech in
Atlas Shrugged; the hectoring didacticism of Starship Troopers; Jubal Harshaw in Stranger in a
Strange Land.

*Mechanism:* Chekhov named the underlying distinction in a letter to Suvorin, 27 October 1888: 'you confuse two
things: solving a problem and stating a problem correctly. It is only the second that is obligatory
for the artist.' The mechanism is about reader agency. A dramatized question recruits the reader
into thinking; a solved question demotes them to audience for someone else's thinking, and readers
resent that specifically when they agree — the flattery is detectable. Also, ideas held by an author
are static, but ideas held by characters under pressure can be tested, and testing is what makes an
idea feel true rather than asserted.

**The invented-result ledger (against fake rigor)** · <sub>load-bearing · 'Gell-Mann amnesia' coined by Michael Crichton (2002 'Why Speculate?' talk). The prestige-borrowing failure mode is the fictional analogue of the Sokal affair (Alan Sokal, Social Text, 1996). 'Fuzz' and 'You Can't Fire Me, I Quit' from the Turkey City Lexicon.</sub>

For idea-driven SF: keep an explicit ledger of every technical claim in the book, sorted into three
columns — (a) true and checkable, (b) invented and flagged as invented within the fiction, (c)
invented but presented with the syntax and prestige of (a). Column (c) is the disease. The specific
move to hunt: naming a real theorem or law (Gödel, Bell's theorem, the halting problem, Landauer's
principle, the no-cloning theorem) and then deriving an invented consequence from it, so the real
result's authority silently underwrites the fake one. Related Turkey City pathologies: 'Fuzz'
(motivation the author was too lazy to supply — 'somehow' is the tell) and 'You Can't Fire Me, I
Quit' (pre-empting the reader's objection as if answering it).

*Mechanism:* Michael Crichton's 'Gell-Mann amnesia' names the ordinary reader's forgiving behaviour — noticing an
article is wrong in your own field and then trusting the next page anyway. Fiction does not get that
indulgence, because fiction's whole transaction is credence. One detected counterfeit in a domain
the reader knows retroactively devalues every claim in domains they don't, and the collapse is
irreversible within the book. Worse for the writer's own cognition: borrowed prestige removes the
pressure that produces real invention. The rigorous alternative (one big lie, then honest
consequences) is generative — it forces you to discover implications you would not have imagined,
which is where the actual novelty in SF comes from.

**Build the scale-shift; never assert the awe** · <sub>strong · Turkey City Lexicon (Eyeball Kick, Ontological Riff, Steam-Grommet Factory). The sense-of-wonder-as-sublime account is commonly attributed to Cornel Robu ('A Key to Science Fiction: The Sense of Wonder', Foundation, 1988).</sub>

Unearned awe is the sentence that tells the reader the thing is vast, ancient, incomprehensible,
sublime. The cure is to construct the comparison the reader performs themselves: give a human-scale
referent, then a measurement that breaks it, then a consequence for a body. Turkey City's positive
terms name what to aim at instead: the 'Eyeball Kick' ('vivid, telling details that create a
kaleidoscopic effect of swarming visual imagery') and the 'Ontological Riff' (a passage 'suggesting
our deepest convictions about reality, space-time, or consciousness have been violated or
transformed'). Its opposite is the Steam-Grommet Factory — 'a didactic SF story which consists
entirely of a guided tour of a large and elaborate gimmick.'

*Mechanism:* Awe is a perceptual event, not a proposition: it occurs when a reader's own scale model fails, and
it requires the reader to do the arithmetic. Stating 'it was unimaginably vast' does the arithmetic
for them and therefore prevents the experience — the same reason a joke explained is not funny. The
standard analysis links SF's 'sense of wonder' to the Kantian mathematical sublime, where the
pleasure comes precisely from the mind's failure to totalize the magnitude; on that account, any
sentence that supplies the totality destroys the effect. The guided-tour failure is the same error
at chapter scale: a tour has no failure of comprehension in it, only transmission.

**The capability ledger (deus ex machina prevention)** · <sub>load-bearing · Aristotle, Poetics ch. 15; Turkey City Lexicon (Deus ex Machina); 'Chekhov's gun' from Chekhov's letters (commonly quoted as 'if in the first act you have hung a pistol on the wall, then in the following one it should be fired').</sub>

Maintain a list of every capability, object, ally, piece of knowledge, and rule of the world, with
the page where the reader first learned of it. Rule: nothing may resolve the climax that was not on
the ledger before the midpoint. Aristotle's objection in the Poetics to Euripides' Medea is not that
the machine is implausible but that the resolution must arise from the plot itself. The Turkey City
definition: 'a miraculous solution to conflict, which comes out of nowhere and renders plot
struggles irrelevant.' Chekhov's converse rule — a pistol hung on the wall in Act I must be fired —
is the same ledger read in the other direction: everything on the ledger should be used, and nothing
off it may be.

*Mechanism:* Endings feel earned when they are surprising and, in retrospect, inevitable. Both halves are
produced by prior planting: surprise comes from the reader not having assembled the pieces,
inevitability from the pieces having been present. A late-introduced solution can only deliver
surprise, so the reader experiences relief instead of satisfaction — and relief is not memorable,
which is why unearned endings destroy word of mouth even when the reader finished. Cognitively, the
ledger also constrains the writer usefully: forbidding new capabilities in Act III forces you to
solve the climax with the material you already committed to, which is where thematic coherence comes
from as a by-product.

**The idiot-plot audit, first and second order** · <sub>strong · 'Idiot plot' possibly coined by James Blish, reported by Damon Knight, In Search of Wonder (1956); 'second-order idiot plot' coined by Knight; popularized by Roger Ebert.</sub>

First order: a plot 'kept in motion solely by virtue of the fact that everybody involved is an
idiot' — the conflict survives only because a character does not ask an obvious question, share
obvious information, or take an obvious action. Test: for each act of your plot, write the sentence
'this ends immediately if X simply says/does Y' and see whether you have a reason better than
authorial convenience. Second-order idiot plot (Damon Knight): a story 'in which not merely the
principals, but everybody in the whole society has to be a grade-A idiot, or the story couldn't
happen' — the characteristic failure of SF worldbuilding, where an invented society persists only
because no one in it has ever noticed the obvious.

*Mechanism:* Readers involuntarily model characters as agents with goals, and detect when a modelled agent acts
against its own interest. The detection is fast and it does not feel like analysis — it feels like
the character becoming fake, which withdraws the emotional investment the whole book depends on. The
second-order version is more dangerous for literary SF because it is invisible at the scene level:
every individual scene is plausible, and the implausibility lives only in the aggregate, so it
survives line-editing and workshop critique and then gets named in reviews. The cure is to write the
counterfactual explicitly — 'why has no one done the obvious thing?' — and either answer it inside
the fiction or change the premise.

**Deliberate control of psychic distance** · <sub>load-bearing · John Gardner, The Art of Fiction: Notes on Craft for Young Writers (1983). The 'psychic distance' ladder example is Gardner's.</sub>

John Gardner's model: fiction is 'a vivid and continuous dream,' and psychic distance is the
reader's degree of identification with a character, movable along a scale. Gardner's demonstration
passage steps inward: 'It was winter of the year 1853. A large man stepped out of a doorway. / Henry
J. Warburton had never much cared for snowstorms. / Henry hated snowstorms. / God how he hated these
damn snowstorms. / Snow.' Practice: mark each paragraph of a scene with its distance level (1–5) and
look for unmotivated jumps. Unintended shifts, inconsistent diction, overloaded sentences and
accidental rhyme are what Gardner identifies as the things that break the dream.

*Mechanism:* This is the unifying mechanism under several separately-named pathologies. White room = distance too
far to have a body in a place. Talking heads = distance too far for interiority. Purple prose = the
narrator's voice becoming more interesting than the character's perception, which jerks the reader
out to level 1 mid-sentence. Info dump = an abrupt jump from level 4 to level 1 to deliver data.
Naming them all as distance failures is more useful than treating them separately, because the fix
is the same operation — decide where the camera is, and move it only on purpose and by increments.

**Kill your darlings — correctly scoped — and the 10% cut** · <sub>strong · Arthur Quiller-Couch, On the Art of Writing (1916). Stephen King, On Writing (2000), crediting an editor's rejection slip.</sub>

Arthur Quiller-Couch, On the Art of Writing (lectures 1913–14, published 1916), Lecture XII 'On
Style': whenever you feel the impulse to perpetrate a piece of exceptionally fine writing, obey it
wholeheartedly — and delete it before the manuscript goes to press. 'Murder your darlings.' The
scope is precise: it targets self-conscious display, not everything you happen to like. Companion
rule from Stephen King's On Writing, which he attributes to a rejection slip he received as a
teenager: '2nd Draft = 1st Draft – 10%.' Practice: mark passages where you were performing rather
than writing, and separately impose a flat 10% cut on the whole draft.

*Mechanism:* The Quiller-Couch mechanism is that self-admiring prose shifts the reader's attention from the
fictional dream to the author's performance — it breaks Gardner's continuity precisely at the
moments the writer is proudest of, which is why writers cannot see it. The 10% cut works by a
different route: it is arbitrary, which is its virtue. A flat quota forces triage without requiring
you to judge each cut on merit, and quota-driven cutting reliably removes the connective throat-
clearing (characters arriving, sitting, considering) that merit-based editing preserves because no
individual sentence of it is bad. Note that 'murder your darlings' is frequently misattributed to
Faulkner, Fitzgerald, Wilde, or King.

**Read the draft aloud (Flaubert's gueuloir)** · <sub>strong · Gustave Flaubert's gueuloir (well attested in his letters and biographies); Turkey City Lexicon for Roget's Disease, Said Bookism, Tom Swifty, Burly Detective Syndrome.</sub>

Flaubert tested his prose by bellowing it in his study — his gueuloir, the 'shouting room' — on the
theory that a sentence that fails when spoken is defective on the page too. Modern equivalents: read
every scene aloud, or run text-to-speech and follow along. Specifically catches: mannered repetition
(an author's tic word recurring three times a page), unintentional rhyme and assonance, Roget's
Disease ('the ludicrous overuse of far-fetched adjectives'), Said Bookisms and Tom Swifties,
sentences whose grammar only survives on the page, and dialogue no mouth would produce.

*Mechanism:* Silent reading uses a partly visual route that skips over rhythm and repetition; the eye normalizes
what the ear cannot. Vocalizing forces serial processing at speech rate, which is roughly the rate
of a reader's inner voice, so it simulates the actual reception condition. It also defeats the
writer's memory of intention: you cannot skim a sentence you are pronouncing. This is the cheapest
high-yield revision practice that most contemporary writers skip, and its yield is concentrated
exactly in the categories agents use as first-pass dismissal signals.

**Understand the prize jury as a lottery of first readers** · <sub>strong · Rebecca Makkai, 'Book Prizes Don't Work How You Think' (Substack); Booker Prize official judging information; Goldsmiths Prize official criteria (Goldsmiths, University of London).</sub>

Rebecca Makkai's insider account: panels of three to five judges divide hundreds of submissions
(sometimes 600) alphabetically or randomly, each screening 200+ books; 'when a judge realizes they
don't love a book, they can put it down.' A book's fate can turn entirely on which judge drew it —
'it's entirely possible that while four of the five judges would have loved your book, your first
reader happens to be the one judge who really doesn't connect with it.' The Booker's 2025 panel read
163 submitted novels down to a 13-book longlist. Contrast the criteria: Booker rewards literary
merit broadly (style, originality, structure, resonance); the Goldsmiths Prize was founded in 2013
to reward, explicitly, 'fiction that breaks the mould or extends the possibilities of the novel
form' — creative daring, not 'best.'

*Mechanism:* The practical consequence is counterintuitive: because prize judges abandon books freely and read
under fatigue and volume, the property that wins prizes is largely the same property that wins
readers — being impossible for one tired reader to put down — layered on top of whatever the
specific prize's formal criterion is. So the literary novelist does not face a genuine choice
between 'finishable' and 'serious'; they face a choice about which formal ambition to add on top of
finishability. Knowing each prize's stated criterion also lets you aim: a formally daring novel is
misaimed at a merit prize and well-aimed at Goldsmiths, and the same manuscript's odds differ by an
order of magnitude between them.

**'Show, don't tell' is wrong as stated — and was refuted in 1961** · <sub>contested · Wayne C. Booth, The Rhetoric of Fiction (University of Chicago Press, 1961); Percy Lubbock, The Craft of Fiction (1921); Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft.</sub>

The blanket rule descends from Henry James's 'dramatize, dramatize' via Percy Lubbock's The Craft of
Fiction (1921). Wayne C. Booth's The Rhetoric of Fiction (1961) dismantled it: the author's voice is
inescapable, 'showing' is itself a rhetorical choice with its own costs, and telling — summary,
judgement, direct commentary — does work that scene cannot. Ursula Le Guin (Steering the Craft) is
similarly impatient with the rule. The operative replacement rule is about allocation: scene for
moments where the reader must be present because a value changes; summary for everything whose only
function is to get us there and to compress time. A novel that is all scene is bloated and
shapeless; a novel that is all summary is a synopsis.

*Mechanism:* The rule persists because it is a good corrective for a specific beginner error (narrating emotion
instead of rendering the situation that produces it), and workshops teach correctives as laws. But
applied globally it produces the characteristic modern literary-fiction fault: uniform close-third
scene-work with no compression, no authorial altitude, and no variation of pace — which reads as
flat regardless of sentence quality, because pace is created by the ratio of story-time to page-time
and a book with a constant ratio has no pace at all. Telling is also how a novel earns the right to
be about something: an author who never generalizes cannot land a theme except by sermonizing later.

**Style is the world: the Poughkeepsie test** · <sub>strong · Ursula K. Le Guin, 'From Elfland to Poughkeepsie' (1972/1979); Samuel R. Delany on SF reading protocols and levels of subjunctivity, The Jewel-Hinged Jaw (1977), incl. the 'the door dilated' example from Heinlein; Turkey City Lexicon (Used Furniture, Space Western).</sub>

Le Guin's argument in 'From Elfland to Poughkeepsie' (1972 speech; The Language of the Night, 1979):
in fantasy, style is not an ingredient of the book, it is the book. If your characters are from
Elfland they must not talk like they are from Poughkeepsie. Extended to SF: the diction, syntax, and
register of the narration must be produced by the invented world, not borrowed from contemporary
journalism. The related failure is Turkey City's 'Used Furniture' — 'use of a background out of
Central Casting rather than inventing one' — and its worst suite, the Space Western.

*Mechanism:* In secondary-world and future-world fiction the reader has no external referent; every inference
about the world is drawn from the texture of the sentences. Contemporary idiom in a far-future
narration silently tells the reader that nothing has changed, which contradicts the premise faster
than any stated worldbuilding can repair. Delany's account of 'reading protocols' explains the
positive version: SF prose is decoded differently — 'the door dilated' is meaningless as naturalism
and rich as SF, because the reader is licensed to reconstruct a whole culture from a verb. This is
why a single well-chosen word does more worldbuilding than a page of exposition, and why infodump is
not merely inelegant but actively wasteful of the form's strongest mechanism.

### Non-obvious findings — Failure taxonomy and the market contract — what ac

- Prize juries abandon books mid-read, exactly like ordinary readers. Rebecca Makkai's insider
  account: judges each screen 200+ submissions and 'when a judge realizes they don't love a book,
  they can put it down' — and a book can die because it happened to be drawn by the one judge of
  five who didn't connect with it. The practical inference reverses the usual dichotomy: the
  property that wins prizes overlaps heavily with the property that wins readers, because in both
  cases you are optimizing for a tired person's decision not to stop.

- Prestige and completion are close to orthogonal, with numbers to prove it. Only 44.4% of British
  Kobo readers finished The Goldfinch — a Pulitzer winner and the 37th-bestselling ebook of the year
  on Kobo. Only 28.2% finished Twelve Years a Slave. Meanwhile Jellybooks reports that a >75%
  completion rate is achieved by fewer than 5% of tested books and correlates strongly with strong
  market performance. So 'literary' is not an alibi for a low completion rate, and a low completion
  rate is not evidence of seriousness.

- There are two hook windows, not one, and they are gendered. Jellybooks' tracked-reader data puts the
  hooked-or-not decision for male readers roughly between pages 20 and 50, and for female readers
  roughly between pages 60 and 100. The received 'grab them on page one' rule optimizes only the
  first window, and produces a common failure shape: an arresting opening set-piece followed by
  sixty pages of consolidation, which survives window one and dies in window two.

- 'Show, don't tell' was refuted by a major critic in 1961 and the workshop world simply carried on.
  Wayne Booth's The Rhetoric of Fiction argued that the author's voice is inescapable and that
  showing is itself a rhetorical choice with costs. The modern consequence is a signature literary-
  fiction fault — uniform close-third scene-work with no summary, hence a constant story-time-to-
  page-time ratio, hence no pace at all, regardless of sentence quality.

- 'Murder your darlings' does not mean 'cut what you love.' Quiller-Couch's actual instruction (On the
  Art of Writing, 1916) targets the impulse to perpetrate 'a piece of exceptionally fine writing' —
  i.e. self-conscious display. It is a rule against performance, not against affection. It is also
  routinely misattributed to Faulkner, Fitzgerald, Wilde, and Stephen King.

- White Room Syndrome is classified in the Turkey City Lexicon as a failure of invention, not of
  description: 'the white room is obviously the white piece of paper confronting the author.' The
  standard cure — add sensory detail — treats the symptom and produces Gingerbread. The real cure is
  to invent facts about the place that constrain what the characters can do.

- 'Signal from Fred' means your subconscious writes accurate reviews of your own draft and leaves them
  in the text. Phrases like 'this was getting tedious' or 'he had heard all this before' are
  involuntary, mechanically greppable, and cluster exactly where readers quit. It is the only free,
  objective boredom detector available to a writer working on their own manuscript.

- The characteristic structural failure of idea-driven SF is not thin characters — it is a mis-
  bracketed ending. Card's MICE quotient predicts that an Idea story ends when its question is
  answered; a literary SF novel that answers its conceptual question at 60% will feel like it sags
  for the remaining 40%, and reviewers will misdiagnose the symptom as 'the characters were thin'
  because that is the only vocabulary they have.

- Damon Knight's 'second-order idiot plot' — where the whole invented society, not just the
  principals, must be idiots for the story to work — is invisible to line-editing and to workshop
  critique, because every individual scene is plausible and the implausibility exists only in
  aggregate. It therefore survives every stage of revision and gets named for the first time in
  published reviews.

- Length norms point in opposite directions in the two markets. Over 120k words is a hard sell for a
  trade debut because print cost scales with length; in Kindle Unlimited, where royalties are paid
  per page read (KENP), long books and long series are economically favoured. The same 160,000-word
  manuscript is an auto-reject in a query pile and an asset in a KU series.

- Naming a huge comp title is a negative signal, not an aspiration. Jane Friedman's point is that
  comping Harry Potter or Jack Reacher tells an agent you did not take the exercise seriously; comps
  are a P&L argument (shelf, print run, marketing template) and must be recent — within about two to
  three years — because a Big Five acquisition publishes roughly two years out.

- Aristotle's objection to deus ex machina was never implausibility. In the Poetics the requirement is
  that the resolution arise from within the plot itself. This reframes the fix: the problem with a
  late-arriving solution is not that readers disbelieve it but that it cannot be simultaneously
  surprising and inevitable, so it yields relief rather than satisfaction — and relief does not
  generate word of mouth even from readers who finished.

- Fake rigor is more damaging than an obvious impossibility. Borrowing a real theorem's prestige to
  license an invented consequence is worse than inventing openly, because Gell-Mann amnesia does not
  apply to fiction: fiction's whole transaction is credence, so a single detected counterfeit in a
  domain the reader knows retroactively devalues every claim in the domains they don't. Honest
  invention ('one big lie, then rigorous consequences') is also more generative, because it forces
  implications you would not otherwise have found.

- The 10% cut works precisely because it is arbitrary. Merit-based editing preserves connective tissue
  — characters arriving, sitting, considering — since no individual sentence of it is bad. A flat
  quota forces triage without per-sentence judgement, which is the only reliable way to remove
  material whose defect is aggregate rather than local.

- Purple prose, talking heads, white room, and info dump are all one defect under Gardner's model:
  unmotivated shifts in psychic distance. Treating them as four separate problems produces four
  separate fixes; treating them as camera placement produces one.

## Reader-experience engineering and beta readers: designing the forward-moving experience of reading (suspense/curiosity/surprise, question management, setup-payoff bookkeeping, tension curves, scene vs. summary), and the methodology of extracting reliable signal from human readers.

**The Sternberg triad audit (suspense / curiosity / surprise)** · <sub>load-bearing · Meir Sternberg, Expositional Modes and Temporal Ordering in Fiction (1978), and later work naming these 'the dynamics of prospection, of retrospection, and of recognition.' Sternberg explicitly argues no configuration produces a fourth.</sub>

Classify every open gap in the manuscript by its temporal direction and keep a per-chapter register.
Suspense = a gap about the future the reader knows exists (bomb is ticking). Curiosity = a gap about
the past the reader knows exists (who killed him). Surprise = a gap the reader did not know existed
until it closes. Operationally: for each chapter, list every gap with type, page opened, page
(partially) closed. Any chapter with no entries in any column is inert.

*Mechanism:* Sternberg's claim is exhaustiveness, not taxonomy for its own sake: these three are the only ways
the order of telling can be misaligned with the order of happening, so they exhaust the space of
'narrative interest' as a temporal phenomenon. This is why a beautifully written chapter can still
read as dead — if the reader is holding no gap of any of the three kinds, there is nothing pulling
attention forward, and prose quality only affects how pleasant the standing still is. It also gives
you three different repair options for the same flat chapter, which is more useful than the single
generic instruction 'add tension.'

**Hitchcock's bomb: convert surprise into suspense by default** · <sub>load-bearing · Alfred Hitchcock to François Truffaut, Hitchcock/Truffaut (1966).</sub>

For every planned reveal, run the counterfactual: what if the reader learned this thirty pages
earlier? Estimate the suspense-minutes bought against the surprise-seconds lost. The default should
be to inform the reader. Reserve concealment only for the case where the twist itself is the payoff
of the whole structure.

*Mechanism:* Hitchcock's arithmetic is the point: 'In the first case we have given the public fifteen seconds of
surprise at the moment of the explosion. In the second we have provided them with fifteen minutes of
suspense.' Surprise is a point event — a spike of affect with no duration and no participation.
Suspense is a sustained state that recruits the reader's prediction machinery across the whole span,
and prediction is what converts a spectator into a participant. Concealment is nearly always the
worse trade because it buys an instant and spends a span. Hitchcock's own caveat is the exact
exception: 'whenever possible the public must be informed. Except when the surprise is a twist, that
is, when the unexpected ending is, in itself, the highlight of the story.'

**Vonnegut's Rule 8 / the cockroach test (against strategic withholding)** · <sub>load-bearing · Kurt Vonnegut, introduction to Bagombo Snuff Box (1999). Mechanism from George Loewenstein, 'The Psychology of Curiosity' (Psychological Bulletin, 1994); the inverted-U in confidence/knowledge was supported experimentally by Kang et al. (2009).</sub>

'Give your readers as much information as possible as soon as possible. To heck with suspense.
Readers should have such complete understanding of what is going on, where and why, that they could
finish the story themselves, should cockroaches eat the last few pages.' Operational test: pick any
page at random; can the reader state (a) who wants what, (b) what is in the way, (c) what happens if
they fail? If not, the page is running on withheld orientation, not on mystery.

*Mechanism:* This is the single most useful corrective to received wisdom, and Loewenstein supplies the
mechanism. Curiosity follows an inverted-U in prior knowledge: it peaks when you know a great deal
and are missing one piece, and it is near zero when you know nothing. You cannot be curious about
something you have no frame for. Therefore withholding orienting information does not produce
mystery; it produces disorientation, which readers experience as boredom and report as 'I couldn't
get into it.' Mystery is manufactured by supplying the frame lavishly and removing exactly one load-
bearing piece. Note that Vonnegut himself flagged the limits — he said Flannery O'Connor broke all
of these except the first.

**The live-question ledger and the partial-answer discipline** · <sub>load-bearing · Roland Barthes, S/Z (1970), the hermeneutic code and its five species of delay. Lee Child's formulation from his widely reprinted essay/talks on suspense ('the way to write a thriller is to ask a question at the beginning and answer it at the end'; the 'how do you make your family hungry?' reframing).</sub>

Maintain an explicit list of the questions the reader is holding, with page opened and page closed,
and a running count per page. Two rules: the count never reaches zero, and no question stays open
beyond a set span without a partial answer that changes its shape. Barthes gives the actual toolkit
of delays: the snare (a deliberate evasion), the equivocation (a mixture of truth and snare), the
partial answer, the suspended answer, and jamming (an acknowledged failure to resolve).

*Mechanism:* A partial answer is the workhorse, and the reason is Loewenstein again: each partial answer moves
the reader up the knowledge curve, re-establishing the 'knows a lot, missing a little' condition
where curiosity is maximal. Pure withholding decays — the gap becomes stale, the reader stops
modeling it, and interest collapses even though the question is technically still open. This is why
long-delayed mysteries fail: not because the delay was long, but because nothing refreshed the gap.
Lee Child's operational version scales the same move down to the sentence: 'imply a question first,
and then answer it second... The reader learns to chase, and the momentum becomes unstoppable.'

**Two-directional setup/payoff ledger (Chekhov bookkeeping)** · <sub>load-bearing · Chekhov, letter to Aleksandr Semenovich Lazarev (pseud. Gruzinsky), 1 November 1889 — commonly rendered 'One must never place a loaded rifle on the stage if it isn't going to go off. It's wrong to make promises you don't mean to keep.' 'Fuzz' from the Turkey City Lexicon (ed. Lewis Shiner, 1988; 2nd ed. Bruce Sterling).</sub>

A literal two-column table maintained through revision. Column A: every planted element, with the
page of its payoff. Column B: every payoff, with the page of its plant. Both directions must be
checked, because they catch different defects: orphaned setups (a gun that never fires) and
unplanted payoffs (deus ex machina). The Turkey City Lexicon names the cheap version of the second
failure 'Fuzz' — 'an element of motivation the author was too lazy to supply,' usually flagged in
the prose by the word 'somehow.'

*Mechanism:* Readers automatically encode salient, otherwise-unmotivated detail as a promissory note, without
deciding to. An unpaid note produces a diffuse dissatisfaction that readers almost never localize
correctly — they report it as 'the ending felt flat' or 'something was missing.' An unplanted payoff
produces the opposite symptom, 'I didn't believe it,' because the reader cannot retro-fit a causal
chain and the fictional world stops behaving lawfully. Note that Chekhov's own framing was about
promises, not economy: 'It's wrong to make promises you don't mean to keep.' The version usually
taught — cut anything that doesn't pay off — is a misreading that licenses stripping texture. The
rule only ever applied to things marked as promises.

**Foreshadowing vs. telegraphing is placement, not subtlety** · <sub>strong · Peter J. Rabinowitz, Before Reading: Narrative Conventions and the Politics of Interpretation (1987), ch. 2, 'Trumpets, Please!: Rules of Notice.'</sub>

The same clue foreshadows or telegraphs depending entirely on where it sits. Rabinowitz's
'privileged positions' — titles, subtitles, first and last lines, chapter openings and especially
chapter closings, anything set off by white space — receive automatic reader attention before
interpretation begins. Operational rule: plant load-bearing clues in unprivileged positions (mid-
