  almost nothing.

- Grillo-Marxuach's warning against outside notes has a specific mechanism worth stealing: the room
  has already traversed and rejected the obvious solution space, so a fresh outside note has a high
  prior of being a rediscovered dead end. Acting on it costs a full re-litigation cycle. Which means
  the record of rejected alternatives is itself a load-bearing artifact — and almost nobody keeps
  one.

## Sentence craft, rhythm and sound — the sentence and paragraph as instruments, and which of their properties can actually be measured

**Variance-first cadence (the Provost principle)** · <sub>load-bearing · Gary Provost, 100 Ways to Improve Your Writing (1972); the same doctrine appears a century earlier in Robert Louis Stevenson, 'On Some Technical Elements of Style in Literature' (Contemporary Review, April 1885): 'successive phrases, like successive groups, must differ openly in length and rhythm.'</sub>

Do not aim for a target sentence length; aim for a target *distribution*. Gary Provost's
demonstration passage in 100 Ways to Improve Your Writing (1972) enacts its own argument: 'This
sentence has five words. Here are five more words. Five-word sentences are fine. But several
together become monotonous... I vary the sentence length, and I create music. Music. The writing
sings... And sometimes, when I am certain the reader is rested, I will engage him with a sentence of
considerable length, a sentence that burns with energy and builds with all the impetus of a
crescendo, the roll of the drums, the crash of the cymbals—sounds that say listen to this, it is
important.' The operational form: measure the coefficient of variation (SD/mean) of your sentence
lengths per scene, not the mean.

*Mechanism:* Two mechanisms. (1) Neural/perceptual habituation: a repeating stimulus interval stops being
registered. Once the ear can predict the next full stop, the prosodic contour carries zero
information and attention decays — the reader's eyes keep moving while comprehension drops. (2)
Prosodic expectancy: readers build an implicit prosodic parse as they read silently, so a violated
length expectation functions as a stress accent at the discourse level. The long sentence is only 'a
crescendo' because the short ones established a baseline against which it is a deviation. Note the
clause 'when I am certain the reader is rested' — the long sentence is a withdrawal from a working-
memory account that the short sentences deposited into.

**The anti-metre rule: prose must be rhythmical but never metrical** · <sub>strong · Robert Louis Stevenson, 'On Some Technical Elements of Style in Literature' (1885), verified verbatim from the Gutenberg text of Essays in the Art of Writing.</sub>

Stevenson's formulation, verbatim: 'The rule of scansion in verse is to suggest no measure but the
one in hand; in prose, to suggest no measure at all. Prose must be rhythmical, and it may be as much
so as you will; but it must not be metrical. It may be anything, but it must not be verse. A single
heroic line may very well pass and not disturb the somewhat larger stride of the prose style; but
one following another will produce an instant impression of poverty, flatness, and disenchantment.'
He names the offenders: the inexperienced writer, 'as Dickens in his earlier attempts to be
impressive,' and the *jaded* writer — 'as any one may see for himself.' Practice: scan for
consecutive stretches of regular alternating stress, especially two adjacent ten-syllable iambic
runs.

*Mechanism:* English is 'inherently rhythmical' (Stevenson's phrase), so a tired writer's hand defaults to iambic
because iambic is the language's path of least resistance. That is exactly why it signals fatigue:
accidental metre is evidence that the sentence was generated by motor habit rather than by attention
to meaning. On the reader's side, metre delivered at prose tempo loses the compensating density of
verse (verse's virtue is 'nearness of the pauses'), so the reader gets the monotony of metre with
none of its payoff. Crucially, this makes accidental blank verse a *diagnostic for the writer's own
cognitive state*, not just a defect in the prose.

**Engineering the close by consonant class (the hammer-stroke ending)** · <sub>useful · Stevenson (1885), the PVF/S-R/dental analysis. The underlying tradition is Ciceronian: Latin prose had named cadences (clausulae, e.g. 'esse videatur') and medieval Latin the cursus planus/tardus/velox. George Saintsbury, A History of English Prose Rhythm (1912), tried to systematise the English equivalent.</sub>

Stevenson's analysis of Milton — 'I cannot praise a fugitive and cloistered virtue, unexercised and
unbreathed, that never sallies out and sees her adversary, but slinks out of the race where that
immortal garland is to be run for, not without dust and heat' — observes that the sentence runs on S
and R throughout, then in the final phrase 'all these favourite letters... are discarded at a blow
and in a bundle; and to make the break more obvious, every word ends with a dental, and all but one
with T.' The operational move: let a sentence run on one consonant family (sibilants, liquids,
labials), then close it on a different, harder family — stops and dentals — to produce audible
finality. His general law: 'The vowel demands to be repeated; the consonant demands to be repeated;
and both cry aloud to be perpetually varied.'

*Mechanism:* Articulatory: plosives and dentals require a full closure of the vocal tract, which produces a
physical stop in subvocalisation. A sentence ending in continuants (-s, -l, -r, -ng, an open vowel)
trails; one ending in /t/ /d/ /k/ /p/ stops. Because silent readers still generate articulatory
motor commands, the reader *feels* the closure. Sound-class contrast at the close also cancels the
running pattern, which is why it reads as a paragraph-ending or a chapter-ending gesture rather than
a mere full stop. Stevenson's second point — that the pattern is partly *ocular*, writers favouring
a letter 'even when it is mute or bears a different value' — is the surprising one: some of prose's
sound-texture is an eye-effect, not an ear-effect.

**The cumulative sentence: base clause first, then free modifiers descending in generality** · <sub>load-bearing · Francis Christensen, 'A Generative Rhetoric of the Sentence,' College Composition and Communication (1963), collected in Notes Toward a New Rhetoric (1967), which also contains 'A Lesson from Hemingway,' 'Sentence Openers,' and 'A Generative Rhetoric of the Paragraph.'</sub>

Francis Christensen's generative rhetoric. A cumulative sentence states a short main clause and then
*adds* free modifiers — non-restrictive phrases and clauses set off by commas, dashes or parentheses
— each at a lower level of generality than the one above. Four principles: addition, direction of
modification (left-branch, medial, right-branch), levels of generality, and texture (the density of
added modifiers). The notation is a numbered indent: level 1 = main clause, level 2 = modifier of
level 1, level 3 = modifier of level 2. Christensen's empirical claim, from analysing twentieth-
century fiction and nonfiction: professional writers use vastly more free modifiers and far more
right-branching addition than undergraduates do, and this — not vocabulary — is the measurable gap.

*Mechanism:* Right-branching addition matches the reader's incremental parse. The main clause resolves the
sentence's core proposition immediately, so working memory is *cleared* before the modifiers arrive;
each modifier is then processed against an already-stable frame, which is why cumulative sentences
can run 60 words without feeling heavy while a 30-word left-branching sentence feels like work. For
the *writer*, the mechanism is generative rather than stylistic: the levels-of-generality schema
makes abstraction visible as a spatial hierarchy, so a writer who has written only level-1 and
level-2 material can literally see that the sentence has never descended to the sensory particular.
It converts 'be more specific' from exhortation into a diagram with an empty row.

**Coordination-to-subordination ratio, not sentence length, is what distinguishes prose voices** · <sub>load-bearing · Francis Christensen, 'A Lesson from Hemingway' (in Notes Toward a New Rhetoric, 1967); Stanley Fish, How to Write a Sentence and How to Read One (2011), on the subordinating, additive and satiric styles; Morris W. Croll, 'The Baroque Style in Prose' (1929), on the curt and loose periods. Ratios above measured in this session on Project Gutenberg texts.</sub>

The commonly cited difference between Hemingway and Faulkner or James is 'short vs long sentences.'
The real, measurable axis is parataxis vs hypotaxis: how many clauses are *coordinated* ('and',
'but', asyndetic juxtaposition) versus *subordinated* (because, although, which, that, while,
having). Christensen's essay 'A Lesson from Hemingway' makes precisely this case: Hemingway is not a
short-sentence writer but a low-subordination writer who builds long sentences out of coordinate and
free-modifier addition. Measured in this session on Gutenberg texts, a crude coordinate-clause-to-
subordinator ratio separates authors cleanly where mean length does not: Henry James (The
Ambassadors) 0.12, Trollope 0.24, Crane (Red Badge) 0.21, Woolf (Jacob's Room) 0.36, Milne 0.48 —
while James and Woolf have nearly identical mean sentence length (18.4 vs 16.7 words).

*Mechanism:* Subordination encodes causal, temporal and evaluative *relations between* events; coordination
refuses to encode them and leaves the relation to the reader. So the choice is epistemic before it
is aesthetic: hypotaxis asserts that the narrator knows why things follow one another; parataxis
asserts that events simply succeed each other and meaning is not the narrator's to supply. This is
why McCarthy's and-chains feel like fate and James's subordinate clauses feel like consciousness.
Stanley Fish makes the same point theologically: the subordinating style assumes the world can be
organised by the writer, the additive style assumes the world is larger than any organisation of it.

**Load the emphatic word into the stress position at the end** · <sub>load-bearing · Strunk & White, The Elements of Style; George Gopen and Judith Swan, 'The Science of Scientific Writing,' American Scientist 78 (1990); underpinned by M.A.K. Halliday's information structure and the given-new contract of Clark & Haviland (1977).</sub>

Strunk and White: 'Place the emphatic words of a sentence at the end.' Gopen and Swan formalised the
sentence as having two privileged slots: the *topic position* (the opening, which tells the reader
what the sentence is about and links backwards) and the *stress position* (whatever occupies the
space just before a full stop or a semicolon, which is where the reader unconsciously invests
emphasis). The revision move is mechanical: identify the word you want remembered, and rewrite so it
lands last. A sentence that ends on a prepositional phrase of housekeeping ('...at the time', '...in
the room', '...for a moment') has thrown away its loudest slot.

*Mechanism:* Reading is a sequence of closures. The reader holds material in a buffer until syntactic closure,
and emphasis is conferred by the act of closing — the last thing in the buffer when it flushes gets
the emphasis whether the writer wanted it or not. So the stress position is not a stylistic
opportunity you may decline; it is always operating, and neglect means the reader emphasises your
throat-clearing. This is also why appended qualifications ('...he said, though it hardly mattered')
defuse a line: the qualification, not the line, takes the stress.

**Old before new: begin sentences with material the reader already has** · <sub>load-bearing · Joseph M. Williams, Style: Lessons in Clarity and Grace; Clark & Haviland's given-new contract (1977); the diagnostic ('underline the first few words of every sentence') is Williams's.</sub>

Joseph Williams's cohesion principle: put familiar, previously mentioned, or short and simple
material at the front of the sentence and new, complex, long material at the end. Diagnostic: print
a passage, underline the first six or seven words of every sentence, and read only the underlined
strings. If they form a coherent, consistent set of topics, the paragraph coheres. If they jump
between unrelated referents, the paragraph will feel choppy no matter how good the individual
sentences are.

*Mechanism:* The given-new contract (Clark & Haviland): comprehension proceeds by finding an antecedent for the
given information, then attaching the new information to it. If new material arrives before an
anchor exists, the reader must hold it unattached, which costs working memory and produces the
sensation of 'dense' prose even when the vocabulary is simple. This is also the reason end-weight
works: long constituents at the end are cheap because the frame is already built. For fiction
specifically, this is the syntax of continuity of consciousness — topic strings are how a scene
feels like one mind moving through it rather than a list of facts.

**Characters as subjects, actions as verbs; kill the nominalisation** · <sub>load-bearing · Joseph M. Williams, Style; Richard Lanham, Revising Prose (Paramedic Method, Lard Factor).</sub>

Williams's two core principles: the grammatical subject should name the main character of the
sentence's little story, and the main verb should name that character's action. The commonest
violation is nominalisation — burying the action in an abstract noun ('made a decision', 'gave
consideration to', 'the arrival of'). Lanham's Paramedic Method is the executable version: (1)
circle the prepositions, (2) circle the 'is' forms, (3) find the action ('who's kicking whom?'), (4)
put that action in a simple active verb, (5) start fast — cut the slow wind-up. Lanham's Lard Factor
makes it scorable: (original word count − revised)/original; he expects competent bureaucratic prose
to shed a third to a half.

*Mechanism:* Readers do not store sentences as strings; they store them as event representations with
participants and roles. A nominalised sentence forces the reader to *reconstruct* the participants
and roles that the syntax has hidden, and that reconstruction is where the fog comes from. Verbs are
also where tense, aspect and modality live, so a nominalised sentence loses the ability to say
*when* and *how definitely* something happened. In fiction, the cost is specific: nominalisation
converts an event into a fact, and facts do not create scene.

**Keep subject and verb adjacent; avoid deep left-branching** · <sub>strong · Edward Gibson, Dependency Locality Theory (Cognition, 1998); Haitao Liu and colleagues on mean dependency distance as a cross-linguistic near-universal; the craft form in Williams and in Christensen's direction-of-modification principle.</sub>

Interruptions between subject and verb ('The letter, which had arrived on the morning of the third
day, after the storm, and which nobody had thought to open, lay...') cost far more than the same
material placed after the verb. Rule of thumb: no more than about seven or eight words between a
subject and its verb, and prefer right-branching modifiers (Christensen) to medial ones. The
computational form is mean dependency distance (MDD) — average linear distance between syntactic
heads and dependents, obtainable from any dependency parser.

*Mechanism:* Dependency Locality Theory: the cost of integrating a word with its head grows with the number of
new discourse referents intervening. A held-open subject is an unresolved prediction consuming
working memory continuously until the verb arrives; the reader is spending on storage rather than on
imagining. Cross-linguistic work finds MDD hovers near ~3 words in natural language — languages
actively minimise it — which means a passage whose MDD spikes is fighting a universal processing
preference, not merely a stylistic convention. Note the corollary: a *long* sentence is not costly,
a *suspended* one is. This is exactly why Christensen's cumulative sentence can be enormous and
still fast.

**Deliberate control of psychic distance across a five-step ladder** · <sub>load-bearing · John Gardner, The Art of Fiction: Notes on Craft for Young Writers (1984).</sub>

John Gardner's five levels, in his own examples: (1) 'It was winter of the year 1853. A large man
stepped out of a doorway.' (2) 'Henry J. Warburton had never much cared for snowstorms.' (3) 'Henry
hated snowstorms.' (4) 'God, how he hated these damn snowstorms.' (5) 'Snow. Under your collar, down
inside your shoes, freezing your miserable soul.' The craft rule is not 'always be close' but 'move
deliberately and usually one step at a time'; unsignalled jumps from level 1 to level 5 and back
read as authorial clumsiness. Note that the level is carried by *syntax and diction*, not by
pronoun: all five are third person past.

*Mechanism:* Distance is encoded by three co-varying signals: formality of naming (full name → first name →
pronoun → nothing), abstraction of the predicate ('had never much cared for' → 'hated' → sensory
fragment), and the presence of evaluative and deictic lexis belonging to the character rather than
the narrator ('these damn'). The reader infers a vantage point from these signals the way one infers
camera distance from framing. Because the inference is continuous and unconscious, an inconsistent
signal set produces a diffuse discomfort the reader cannot name — which is why 'something's off with
the POV' is such a common and such a vague note.

**Filter-word audit: strip perception verbs that re-narrate the act of perceiving** · <sub>load-bearing · The term 'filtering' in creative-writing pedagogy is commonly attributed to Janet Burroway, Writing Fiction: A Guide to Narrative Craft; the underlying narratology is Gérard Genette's focalization.</sub>

In close third or first person, constructions such as 'she saw', 'he noticed', 'she felt', 'he
heard', 'she realised', 'it seemed to him', 'he watched as' interpose a report of perception between
reader and thing. 'She saw the crow land on the fence' → 'The crow landed on the fence.' The audit
is mechanical: grep narration (not dialogue) for a fixed verb list with the POV character as
subject, count per 1,000 words, and cut the ones that carry no information about the *manner* or
*failure* of perception. Keep the filter when the perceiving is itself the event ('Only then did she
notice the second set of tracks').

*Mechanism:* Two effects. (1) Distance: naming the perceiver re-establishes the narrator as an intermediary,
moving the reader from Gardner's level 4 back toward level 2. (2) Predicate demotion: filtering
turns the interesting clause into a subordinate complement — the main verb becomes 'saw', a verb
with no image in it, and the stress position is stolen from the crow. Cutting the filter
simultaneously shortens the sentence, promotes the concrete verb to main-clause position, and closes
psychic distance — one edit, three gains, which is why it is the single highest-yield line edit in
close-third fiction.

**Free indirect style as a syntactic setting, not a vibe** · <sub>strong · James Wood, How Fiction Works (2008), gives the best modern craft account; the technique is Austen's and Flaubert's, and the term is a translation of erlebte Rede / style indirect libre.</sub>

Third person, past tense, but with the character's diction, deictics and evaluations: 'Tomorrow was
Tuesday, and Tuesday meant the bloody dentist.' The markers are checkable: present-tense deictics
('now', 'tomorrow', 'here', 'this') under past-tense narration; evaluative and colloquial lexis in
narration that matches the character's register; rhetorical questions and exclamations outside
quotation marks; intensifiers ('really', 'quite', 'bloody') in narration. To audit: extract
narration only (strip quoted dialogue) and count question marks, exclamation marks, intensifiers and
second-person 'you' — a high count means you are already in free indirect style whether or not you
meant to be.

*Mechanism:* Free indirect style exploits the fact that tense and person are the *only* obligatory markers of
narratorial identity in English; every other signal (word choice, deixis, evaluation) can be
silently reassigned to the character. The reader therefore hears two voices at once and cannot
cleanly separate them, which produces both intimacy and irony — the reader is inside the character
while the grammar quietly keeps the narrator's vantage available for judgement. It is the cheapest
way to get interiority without the machinery of quoted thought, and it is why it degrades sharply
when mixed with filter words: 'she thought that tomorrow meant the dentist' collapses the double
voice back into a single reported one.

**Chekhov's rule is selection and grouping, not the ban on abstraction** · <sub>load-bearing · Anton Chekhov, letter to Alexander Chekhov, 10 May 1886 (translated by Avrahm Yarmolinsky in The Unknown Chekhov, 1954); the 'glint of light on broken glass' phrasing is a twentieth-century paraphrase, documented by Quote Investigator, and should not be quoted as Chekhov.</sub>

The famous instruction is a later condensation, not Chekhov's words. What he actually wrote to his
brother Alexander (10 May 1886) is: 'In descriptions of Nature one must seize on small details,
grouping them so that when the reader closes his eyes he gets a picture. For instance, you'll have a
moonlit night if you write that on the mill dam a piece of glass from a broken bottle glittered like
a bright little star, and that the black shadow of a dog or a wolf rolled past like a ball.' Two
details, both small, both *grouped*, both metaphorically anchored — a star and a ball. The
operational rule is therefore: two or three particulars chosen for mutual coherence, not an
inventory.

*Mechanism:* A concrete particular is cheap to image and expensive to argue with; an abstraction ('the night was
beautiful') asks the reader to accept a judgement they have no grounds for. But the deeper mechanism
is *grouping*: two details that imply the same larger fact let the reader perform the inference
themselves, and self-generated inferences are held more confidently and remembered better than
asserted ones. The condensed 'show, don't tell' version loses this entirely and licenses the
opposite failure — page-long inventories of unranked sensory data, which are as unreadable as
abstraction because the reader cannot tell which particulars are load-bearing.

**Adverb discipline aimed at redundancy, not at -ly as such** · <sub>strong · Stephen King, On Writing (2000); Elmore Leonard, 'Easy on the Adverbs, Exclamation Points and Especially Hooptedoodle,' New York Times, 16 July 2001; Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft ('Chastity'); counts from Ben Blatt, Nabokov's Favorite Word Is Mauve (2017).</sub>

Stephen King's 'the road to hell is paved with adverbs' and Leonard's rules 3 and 4 ('Never use a
verb other than said to carry dialogue' / 'Never use an adverb to modify the verb said') are both
really about redundancy: the adverb that repeats what the verb already contains ('shouted loudly',
'whispered softly', 'grinned happily') and the adverb propping up a weak speech tag. Le Guin's
'Chastity' exercise is the training version: write 200–350 words of descriptive narrative prose with
no adjectives and no adverbs at all — verbs, nouns, pronouns, articles only. The point is not
permanent abstinence but recalibration of what a modifier has to earn.

*Mechanism:* An adverb that duplicates its verb adds a word without adding a discourse referent, so it dilutes
information density and, worse, signals that the writer did not trust the verb — a reader who
detects that distrust starts checking the writer's other choices. Le Guin's exercise works on the
writer's *search process*: banning modifiers forces retrieval of a more specific verb or noun, and
the specific verb is both shorter and more imageable. The empirical support is unusually good for a
craft rule. Ben Blatt counted -ly adverbs per 10,000 words across authors: Hemingway 80, Twain 81,
Steinbeck 93, Updike 102, Stephen King 105, E. L. James 155. Across books, those in the lowest band
(0–49 per 10,000) were rated great by 67% of critics; those in the highest band (150+) only 16% of
the time.

**Make 'said' invisible and budget the exclamation point** · <sub>strong · Elmore Leonard, ten rules, New York Times (2001) and Elmore Leonard's 10 Rules of Writing (2007); counts from Ben Blatt, Nabokov's Favorite Word Is Mauve (2017).</sub>

Leonard: use 'said' to carry dialogue; keep exclamation points 'under control — you are allowed no
more than two or three per 100,000 words of prose.' Both are countable in seconds. The measurable
version: percentage of dialogue tags that are said/asked (aim high — 90%+ in commercial fiction),
and exclamation points per 100,000 words. The important caveat is Leonard's own: 'If you have the
knack of playing with exclaimers the way Tom Wolfe does, you can throw them in by the handful.'

*Mechanism:* 'Said' is processed as punctuation rather than as a verb — it is frequent enough to be skipped by
the eye, so it attributes speech without spending attention. A said-bookism ('he expostulated') re-
recruits attention to the tag and away from the line, and simultaneously tells the reader how to
hear something they were about to hear for themselves. The exclamation point fails by emphasis
inflation: it asserts intensity typographically that the sentence has not earned, and repeated
assertion devalues the currency, so a page with six exclamations reads *flatter* than one with none.
Ironically, Blatt's audit found Leonard used 1,651 exclamation points across 3.4 million words —
about 49 per 100,000, roughly sixteen times his own allowance — though still the lowest rate among
the fifty authors he sampled. Amateur fan-fiction writers used about four times as many as published
authors. The lesson: the rule is calibrated as a *relative* discipline, and even its author treated
it as a direction, not a quota.

**Monosyllabic runs as a local rhythmic device — and the negative finding that global monosyllable rate is worthless** · <sub>useful · The device is standard prosodic craft (Stevenson; Saintsbury). The corpus rates are my own measurement in this session on Project Gutenberg texts, and I have not seen this negative result stated as such elsewhere.</sub>

Sequences of monosyllables slow tempo and hit hard ('He was a good dog and he was dead');
polysyllables accelerate and smooth. The device is real and worth deploying at moments of impact.
But when I measured monosyllable percentage across eight novels in this session, the range was
strikingly narrow: Woolf 68.6%, Austen 68.9%, Melville 69.1%, Crane 71.0%, Christie 71.0%, Trollope
71.2%, James 73.4%, Milne 76.5%. A book-level monosyllable rate does not discriminate Henry James
from Stephen Crane. Preposition density is similarly flat (110–135 per 1,000 words for seven of
eight books).

*Mechanism:* Monosyllables in English are overwhelmingly function words and Anglo-Saxon core vocabulary, which
any English text must contain in near-fixed proportion — so the global rate is a property of the
language, not of the author. The stylistic effect lives entirely in *local excursions*: a run of
eight or ten consecutive monosyllables in a clause is a deviation, and deviation is what the ear
registers. The lesson generalises: a measure that is stable across the corpus is a measure to
compute in a sliding window, not in aggregate. Any measurable stylistic property should be validated
by checking whether it actually varies between authors you can tell apart by ear.

**The paragraph is the unit of attention, and its length distribution is a pacing instrument** · <sub>strong · Francis Christensen, 'A Generative Rhetoric of the Paragraph' (in Notes Toward a New Rhetoric, 1967); Verlyn Klinkenborg, Several Short Sentences About Writing (2012), on the sentence and its neighbours as the working unit. Paragraph statistics measured in this session.</sub>

Paragraph shape does more pacing work than sentence length, and it varies far more between writers.
Measured in this session: mean words per paragraph — Milne 22.7, Christie 28.4, Woolf 47.4, Crane
48.1, Austen 56.8, Melville 78.4, Trollope 206.8. Percentage of paragraphs that are a single
sentence — Crane 13.6%, Trollope 11.3%, Melville 25.5%, Christie 39.3%, Austen 44.5%, Woolf 45.7%,
Milne 56.6%. The single-sentence paragraph is the strongest emphasis device in prose that costs no
words. Christensen extended his levels-of-generality notation from the sentence to the paragraph: a
topic sentence at level 1 with coordinate (same-level) or subordinate (descending) sentences under
it.

*Mechanism:* White space is a visual pause the eye registers before reading begins, so paragraph breaks set
expectations about tempo *pre-attentively* — a page of short paragraphs reads fast before a single
word has been processed. The single-sentence paragraph works because it is surrounded by more white
space than any other sentence on the page: it borrows emphasis from layout rather than from syntax,
which is why it survives being made of plain words. The reciprocal danger is inflation — a writer
who breaks every third sentence into its own paragraph has spent the device and has nothing left for
the actual turn.

**Periodic versus loose: choose which end of the sentence carries the knowledge** · <sub>strong · Morris W. Croll, 'The Baroque Style in Prose' (1929) and 'Attic Prose: Lipsius, Montaigne, Bacon'; Stanley Fish, How to Write a Sentence (2011); Virginia Tufte devotes a chapter of Artful Sentences to branching.</sub>

A periodic (suspended) sentence withholds grammatical completion until the end; a loose or
'trailing' sentence completes early and adds. Morris Croll distinguished the curt period (short,
asymmetric, each member a fresh start) from the loose period (linked by 'and', 'but', absolutes and
participles, following the mind's actual order of discovery). Fish's subordinating/additive pair is
the same distinction restated. Practically: a scene of dawning realisation wants trailing sentences
whose modifiers arrive in the order the character notices things; a scene of judgement or verdict
wants a period that lands.

*Mechanism:* The periodic sentence creates a syntactic debt the reader must carry, and discharging it at the
close converts accumulated tension into emphasis — this is suspense at the sentence scale, and it is
why periodic sentences feel authoritative: only a writer who knew the whole shape in advance could
build one. The loose sentence mimics the *sequence of cognition* rather than its result, so it reads
as thought happening rather than thought reported. Croll's insight is that the loose style is not
carelessness but a deliberate imitation of the mind in motion — 'the ardour of its conception in the
mind.' Choosing between them is choosing whether the reader watches a mind arrive at a conclusion or
receives one.

**Aphorism density and the closure-fatigue problem** · <sub>contested · Elmore Leonard's eleventh rule ('If it sounds like writing, I rewrite it'); James Wood, How Fiction Works, on the writer's too-fine style and the sentence that draws attention to its author. The specific density heuristic is my synthesis rather than a canonical rule, and practitioners disagree — Wilde, Wodehouse, Muriel Spark and Nabokov all run high aphorism density deliberately.</sub>

Aphorisms, epigrams and beautifully closed sentences are individually the most quotable thing a
novelist produces and collectively the fastest way to stall a book. Symptom: three or more fully
closed, generalising, chiastic or antithetical sentences within a page; every paragraph ending on a
summarising flourish. Leonard's unofficial eleventh rule is the test — 'If it sounds like writing, I
rewrite it.' The corrective is not to cut every aphorism but to ration them (roughly one per scene,
placed at a structural hinge) and to make sure the surrounding sentences are *open* — trailing,
unresolved, continuing.

*Mechanism:* An aphorism is a terminal structure: it generalises, so there is nowhere to go from it except a new
start. A reader encountering many in succession experiences repeated micro-endings, and narrative
momentum is precisely the absence of endings. Second mechanism: an aphorism is spoken in the
author's voice, so density of aphorism raises the narrator to the surface and pushes psychic
distance back to Gardner's level 1 — the reader stops being in the scene and starts admiring the
writer. Third, quotability is a scarce resource: the tenth good line on a page does not read as
good, it reads as strain, because the reader has calibrated to the local maximum.

**Read aloud, because silent reading is not silent** · <sub>load-bearing · Janet Dean Fodor, the Implicit Prosody Hypothesis ('Psycholinguistics cannot escape prosody,' 2002); Alan Baddeley's phonological loop; Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft ('Being Gorgeous'); the practice itself is universal craft advice.</sub>

The oldest advice in the trade — read the draft aloud, or have text-to-speech read it to you at 1.0x
— is also the one with the best cognitive-science justification. Le Guin's 'Being Gorgeous' exercise
makes it generative rather than merely diagnostic: write a passage *intended* to be read aloud,
playing with sound, rhythm, repetition and cadence for its own sake, before worrying about story
economy. Machine TTS has an advantage over your own voice: it will not silently repair a bad rhythm
the way a writer reading their own work does.

*Mechanism:* The Implicit Prosody Hypothesis: silent readers project a default prosodic contour onto text, and
that contour influences syntactic parsing and disambiguation. This means rhythm is not decoration
layered on comprehension — it is part of the comprehension mechanism, and a sentence whose implicit
prosody conflicts with its syntax will be misparsed and re-read. Subvocalisation via the
phonological loop is also where repeated sounds, unintended rhymes and tongue-tangling consonant
clusters become perceptible. Reading aloud exposes exactly the defects that are invisible to the
eye: accidental metre, jingle, sentences that run out of breath, and modifier stacks that the eye
skims but the mouth cannot.

**Orwell's six rules are a checklist with one real rule in it** · <sub>contested · George Orwell, 'Politics and the English Language' (1946), rules verified verbatim from the Orwell Foundation text; Geoffrey K. Pullum, 'Fear and Loathing of the English Passive' (Language & Communication), for the counts.</sub>

Verbatim: 'i. Never use a metaphor, simile or other figure of speech which you are used to seeing in
print. ii. Never use a long word where a short one will do. iii. If it is possible to cut a word
out, always cut it out. iv. Never use the passive where you can use the active. v. Never use a
foreign phrase, a scientific word or a jargon word if you can think of an everyday English
equivalent. vi. Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous.' Rule iv is
simply wrong as stated, and Orwell violated it heavily: Geoffrey Pullum's count found about 26% of
transitive verbs in 'Politics and the English Language' are passive, against roughly 17% in ordinary
prose — Orwell used passives at more than one and a half times the normal rate while forbidding
them. Keep i, iii and vi; treat ii as a claim about Latinate abstraction rather than about
syllables; discard iv and replace it with the real principle, which is agency and topic control.

*Mechanism:* Why rule i works: a stock metaphor is processed lexically rather than imagistically — 'toe the line'
produces no toe and no line — so the reader gets the meaning with none of the perceptual content the
figure was chosen for. Orwell's own formulation of the mechanism is the best one available: 'let the
meaning choose the word, and not the other way about.' Why rule iv fails: the passive's actual
function is information-structural — it lets you put given material in the topic position and new
material in the stress position, which is exactly what cohesion requires. Banning it forces
violations of old-before-new. The genuinely useful residue of the anti-passive tradition is
narrower: watch for agentless passives that *conceal* a responsible party, and for passives that put
an inanimate abstraction in the subject slot.

**Sentence-opener variety is largely a myth; topic-string coherence is the real goal** · <sub>strong · Francis Christensen, 'Sentence Openers' (Notes Toward a New Rhetoric, 1967); percentages measured in this session on Project Gutenberg texts.</sub>

Standard writing-textbook advice is to vary how sentences begin — start with a participle, a
prepositional phrase, a subordinate clause. Christensen's 'Sentence Openers' essay found
professional prose overwhelmingly subject-initial, and my measurement in this session agrees: the
proportion of sentences beginning with a preposition, subordinator, conjunction, adverb or
participle was Christie 17.3%, James 17.3%, Crane 18.6%, Austen 19.6%, Woolf 22.1%, Milne 24.0%,
Melville 29.3%, Trollope 30.6%. Deep left-branching openers (four or more words before the first
comma) are rarer still: 1.7% in James, 3.2% in Woolf, 11.1% in Trollope. In other words, roughly
seven to eight sentences in ten begin with the subject even in the most ornate literary prose.

*Mechanism:* Subject-initial is the default because it satisfies old-before-new: the subject is usually the given
topic, so starting there is what cohesion requires. Variety for its own sake in the opener actively
damages the topic string. What the advice is groping toward is real but misidentified — the fault it
diagnoses is *rhythmic* monotony (identical sentence lengths and identical clause shapes), not
lexical monotony at position one. The correct fix is length and structure variance, and the correct
use of a front-loaded adverbial is to signal a temporal or spatial shift ('By the time she reached
the bridge...'), which is a discourse function, not an ornament. Front-load when you need to
reorient the reader; otherwise start with the subject and vary something else.

**Punctuation rate as voice fingerprint: semicolons, dashes, commas per sentence** · <sub>useful · Standard stylometry practice (function words and punctuation as authorship markers, in the Mosteller-Wallace tradition); Virginia Tufte, Artful Sentences, treats these syntactic units chapter by chapter with over a thousand examples. Rates measured in this session.</sub>

Punctuation habits are among the most stable and most discriminating features of an individual prose
style — far more so than word choice. Measured this session, semicolons per 10,000 words: Christie
12, Crane 13, Milne 24, Trollope 57, James 99, Austen 134, Woolf 145, Melville 195 — a sixteen-fold
spread. Commas per sentence: Crane 0.68, Christie 0.80, Milne 1.23, James 1.28, Woolf 1.41, Trollope
1.64, Austen 1.73, Melville 2.11. Practical use: fix your own baseline rates over a finished book
you are happy with, then flag any chapter that deviates by more than about 30% — deviation usually
marks either a change in scene-type (dialogue-heavy vs interior) or a passage written on a bad day.

*Mechanism:* Punctuation encodes clause-boundary strength, so a punctuation profile *is* a syntactic profile in
cheap, parser-free form. Semicolon rate proxies for the writer's willingness to join two independent
clauses without a conjunction — the paratactic-but-not-oral move that produces Woolf's and
Melville's sense of a mind holding two things simultaneously. Comma rate proxies for free-modifier
density in Christensen's sense. Because these are motor habits below the level of conscious choice,
they are stable within a writer and are exactly what stylometric authorship attribution exploits —
and for the same reason, they are the earliest place your own fatigue or imitation of another writer
shows up.

**Uniform Information Density: smooth the surprisal, don't flatten it** · <sub>useful · Roger Levy and T. Florian Jaeger, 'Speakers optimize information density through syntactic reduction' (2007); Jaeger's later work on UID. The application to manuscript revision is not, to my knowledge, standard practice, but the measurement is straightforward with any small LM.</sub>

A modern and genuinely new measurable. Run a small language model over the manuscript and record
per-token surprisal (negative log probability). Plot it. Sharp isolated spikes are places where the
reader will stumble — an unglossed proper noun, an unmotivated jump, a compressed clause. Long flat
troughs are places where the reader is being told what they already know and will skim. Well-written
prose distributes information relatively evenly across the signal, with deliberate spikes at the
points you actually want to be turns.

*Mechanism:* Uniform Information Density: speakers and writers unconsciously modulate syntactic and lexical
choices to keep information rate near the channel capacity — they use optional 'that', full noun
phrases and longer forms exactly where the upcoming material is unpredictable. Deviation is costly
in both directions: exceeding capacity causes comprehension failure, and falling below it wastes
reader attention. This gives a principled account of several separate craft rules at once — why
exposition must be interleaved rather than dumped, why the sentence after a difficult one should be
easy, and why Provost's crescendo works (the short sentences lower the local rate, creating headroom
for the long one). It is also the only measure in this list that captures *content* pacing rather
than syntax.

**The schemes are learnable, countable devices — including the adjective-order rule nobody was taught** · <sub>useful · Mark Forsyth, The Elements of Eloquence (2013); the adjective-order sequence is standard descriptive linguistics that Forsyth made famous. Virginia Tufte's Artful Sentences covers the same territory syntactically across its fourteen chapters.</sub>

Mark Forsyth's The Elements of Eloquence catalogues the rhetorical figures that actually produce
memorable English sentences: alliteration, diacope (a word repeated with a small interruption —
'Bond. James Bond.'), anadiplosis (ending clause N and beginning clause N+1 with the same word),
hendiadys ('sound and fury' for 'furious sound'), tricolon and its ascending form. Forsyth also
popularised the descriptive fact that English adjectives obey a strict unwritten order — opinion,
size, age, shape, colour, origin, material, purpose — so that 'a lovely little old rectangular green
French silver whittling knife' is fine and any permutation sounds insane, and no native speaker has
ever been taught this.

*Mechanism:* Figures work because they impose a pattern the ear can detect *while* the sense is being delivered,
which gives the reader two simultaneous streams to lock onto and roughly doubles retention — this is
the same mechanism as rhyme in oral poetry, operating below rhetoric's conscious level. Tricolon
works specifically because three is the smallest number of instances that establishes a pattern
*and* completes it, so it satisfies and closes in the same gesture. The adjective-order case makes
the deeper point: much of what a reader experiences as 'good ear' is compliance with rules the
writer has never articulated, which is why reading aloud outperforms analysis for detection — the
ear knows rules the mind has not been told.

**Deliberate cadence variation as a scheduled revision pass** · <sub>strong · Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft ('Short and Long'); Verlyn Klinkenborg, Several Short Sentences About Writing, on the sentence-at-a-time revision discipline; the margin-count procedure is standard workshop practice. Run statistics measured in this session.</sub>

Do not try to control rhythm while drafting. Run it as a discrete late pass with a fixed procedure:
(1) print the scene; (2) write each sentence's word count in the margin; (3) circle every run of
three or more consecutive sentences within ±2 words of each other; (4) inside each circle, either
fuse two sentences or cut one to a fragment; (5) check that each paragraph's last sentence has its
most important word last; (6) read the scene aloud once and mark only where you stumble. Le Guin's
'Short and Long' exercise is the training version: write a narrative paragraph in sentences of seven
words or fewer, then write a page-long passage that is a single sentence.

*Mechanism:* Rhythm cannot be composed and invented simultaneously because both compete for the same limited
executive attention — drafting is a search problem, cadence is a pattern-matching problem, and
interleaving them degrades both. Separating them also gives the ear a stale text, and staleness is
what allows a writer to hear their own prose rather than hear their intention for it. The margin
word-count step matters more than it sounds: it converts an auditory judgement, which the writer's
intention contaminates, into a visual one, which it does not. In my measurements the proportion of
text sitting inside monotony runs of three-plus similar-length sentences ranged from 5.7% (Trollope)
and 7.9% (Austen) up to 22.5% (Milne, deliberately, for a child's read-aloud cadence) — so this is a
dial with a real range, not a defect to zero out.

### Non-obvious findings — Sentence craft, rhythm and sound — the sentence an

- Book-level monosyllable percentage does not discriminate authors at all. Across eight novels I
  measured a range of only 68.6% (Woolf) to 76.5% (Milne), with Henry James at 73.4% — higher than
  Stephen Crane at 71.0%. Monosyllabic texture is a purely local device; measured in aggregate it
  recovers a property of English, not of a writer. The same holds for preposition density (110–135
  per 1,000 words for seven of eight books). Any style metric should be validated by checking that
  it actually separates writers you can distinguish by ear.

- Orwell used passives at roughly one and a half times the normal rate in the very essay that says
  'Never use the passive where you can use the active.' Geoffrey Pullum's count: about 26% of
  transitive verbs in 'Politics and the English Language' are passive, against roughly 17% in
  ordinary prose.

- Elmore Leonard broke his own exclamation-point rule by a factor of sixteen. He allowed 'no more than
  two or three per 100,000 words'; Ben Blatt counted 1,651 across his 3.4 million words, about 49
  per 100,000 — which was still the lowest rate among the fifty authors sampled. The rule is
  calibrated as relative discipline, not as a quota, and even its author treated it that way.

- Chekhov never wrote 'Don't tell me the moon is shining; show me the glint of light on broken glass.'
  The actual 1886 letter to his brother says to 'seize on small details, grouping them so that when
  the reader closes his eyes he gets a picture,' and gives two details — a bottle shard glittering
  like a star, a shadow rolling past like a ball. The real principle is selection and grouping with
  metaphorical anchoring, not a ban on abstraction. The condensed version has licensed a generation
  of unranked sensory inventories.

- 'Vary your sentence openers' is close to backwards. Seven to eight sentences in ten begin with the
  subject even in the most ornate literary prose I measured (non-subject openers: Christie and James
  17.3%, Woolf 22.1%, Trollope 30.6%), and deep left-branching openers are under 4% in five of eight
  books. Subject-initial is what the given-new contract requires; the monotony the advice is really
  detecting is rhythmic, not lexical.

- Lag-1 autocorrelation of sentence length is a strong, apparently under-used discriminator. Henry
  James scores 0.54 — his long sentences cluster with long ones into sustained hypotactic movements
  — while Trollope (0.12) and Christie (0.14) alternate almost at random despite having wildly
  different mean lengths. Two writers can share an identical length distribution and feel completely
  different because of how the lengths are *ordered*.

- Woolf's prose is lexically more concrete than Austen's or James's. Her nominalisation rate is 10.3
  per 1,000 words against Austen's 27.7 and James's 23.4. Her difficulty is entirely syntactic — a
  semicolon rate of 145 per 10,000 (twelve times Christie's) and a length coefficient of variation
  of 0.97. Difficulty and abstraction are separate, separately measurable dials.

- Stevenson claimed in 1885 that part of prose's sound-pattern is an *eye*-effect, not an ear-effect:
  a writer running a particular consonant 'will not improbably rejoice to write it down even when it
  is mute or bears a different value,' and one running the open A shows 'a tenderness for the flat
  A.' Sound texture in prose is partly orthographic.

- Stevenson identified accidental blank verse as the signature of the *jaded* writer specifically —
  not the bad one. Because English falls into iambic by default, metrical drift is direct evidence
  that the sentence was produced by motor habit rather than attention. It is therefore a diagnostic
  of the writer's cognitive state, and one of the few style metrics that reports on the author
  rather than the text.

- Christensen's finding is that the measurable gap between professional and amateur prose is free-
  modifier density and right-branching addition — not vocabulary, not sentence length, not grammar.
  And his 'A Lesson from Hemingway' argues that Hemingway is not a short-sentence writer at all but
  a low-subordination writer whose long sentences are built by coordinate addition, which is exactly
  what the sentence-length caricature misses.

- Cutting a filter word buys three separate improvements from one edit: it shortens the sentence,
  promotes the concrete verb from a subordinate complement into the main clause (and therefore into
  the stress position), and closes psychic distance by removing the narrator as intermediary. That
  triple payoff is why it is the highest-yield line edit in close third person, and why it beats
  generic 'tighten your prose' advice.

- Uniform Information Density gives one unified mechanism for several craft rules previously treated
  as unrelated: why exposition must be interleaved rather than dumped, why the sentence after a hard
  one should be easy, and why Provost's crescendo works — the short sentences lower the local
  information rate and create the headroom the long sentence spends.

## Revision systems: how books actually get fixed — pass taxonomy, diagnostic reading, the symptom/cause distinction, and line-level craft

**Pass separation (developmental → line → copy → proof)** · <sub>load-bearing · Editorial Freelancers Association 'Editorial Service Definitions'; standard trade-publishing workflow (Chicago Manual practice); the attachment mechanism is folk-professional but consistent with Nancy Sommers' 1980 finding that writers' affection for text rises with proximity to writing it.</sub>

Four distinct passes, each with a single altitude, run in strict order and never blended.
Developmental/structural: does this book work — premise, arc, causality, order, what's missing,
what's redundant; output is prose-agnostic (an editorial letter, a revised outline). Line: does this
paragraph do its work — rhythm, psychic distance, compression, voice, transitions; output is
rewritten sentences. Copy: correctness and consistency — grammar, usage, continuity, timeline, name
spellings, style sheet; output is queries and marks. Proof: typography and typos against the set
pages only. The EFA's service definitions codify these as distinct, separately-priced services; the
professional rule is that a line edit is only performed 'in conjunction with developmental editing
after big-picture issues have been addressed.'

*Mechanism:* Two independent mechanisms. (1) Attention is a single-channel resource with a set criterion: you
cannot simultaneously hold the shape of a 400-page argument and the phoneme-level texture of a
clause. Every additional search target raises the miss rate for all targets (see 'satisfaction of
search'). (2) Sunk-cost/attachment: polishing prose creates ownership. A beautifully-cadenced scene
is measurably harder to delete than a rough one, so line-editing before structure is locked corrupts
the structural pass with sentences you can no longer bear to cut. The order is not fastidiousness;
it is protecting the expensive decisions from the cheap ones.

**The cold read with no pen (engagement trace)** · <sub>load-bearing · Standard developmental-editing practice; the 'read it once as a reader' rule is near-universal among trade editors. The underlying separation of experience from repair matches Sommers' finding that experienced writers imagine 'a critical and productive collaborator — a collaborator who has yet to love their work.'</sub>

Read the whole manuscript in one or two sittings, at reading speed, on a device you don't write on,
forbidden to fix anything. The only permitted marks are a single character in the margin recording
reader-state, not judgment: a dot where you skimmed, a slash where you wanted to stop, a '?' where
you were confused, an arrow where you flipped back, '+' where you were pulled forward. You get one
of these reads per draft — after it, you are contaminated. The output is a map of symptoms with page
numbers, and nothing else.

*Mechanism:* Pacing, boredom, and confusion are derivatives — they exist only across time and cannot be perceived
in a chapter-by-chapter revision. Reading at speed simulates the reader's actual sampling rate. The
no-pen rule exists because the instant you begin repairing, you switch from experiencing-mode to
problem-solving-mode, and problem-solving-mode has read the book before and cannot be bored by it.
You are harvesting a signal that is destroyed by the act of responding to it.

**Reverse outline, and its novelistic form, the scene grid** · <sub>load-bearing · Reverse outlining is codified by the UW–Madison Writing Center ('a bullet-point view of your paper's structure'); the scene-grid variant is standard among novelists and developmental editors; the value-charge column is Robert McKee's (Story: every scene must turn a value).</sub>

Written after the draft, not before. For nonfiction: one line per paragraph stating what it does
(not what it says). For fiction: a spreadsheet, one row per scene, with columns for POV, day/elapsed
time, location, cast, whose want drives it, what the reader learns, what changes (the value charge
at the start and at the end), narrative questions opened, narrative questions closed, and word
count. Then you stop reading prose and read only the table.

*Mechanism:* It converts a serial, time-bound artifact into a spatial one that can be scanned in parallel.
Structural pathologies that are invisible in linear reading become visually obvious as patterns in a
grid: nine consecutive rows with the same POV, a forty-page stretch where the 'what changes' column
reads 'nothing,' three rows with an identical emotional transaction, twenty rows where the
'questions opened' column is empty. The writer's memory of the book is a summary of intentions; the
table is a record of execution. The gap between the two is the book's real problem list.

**Diagnostic backtracking: the symptom is late, the cause is early** · <sub>load-bearing · The upstream principle is a professional-editing and film-editing commonplace (in cutting rooms, 'if a scene isn't working, the problem is in the scene before'). The empirical symptom/cause finding is Nancy Sommers, 'Revision Strategies of Student Writers and Experienced Adult Writers,' CCC 31:4 (1980): students 'do not diagnose lexical repetition as symptomatic of problems on a deeper level.'</sub>

When a reader reports boredom at chapter 20, treat chapter 20 as the location of the symptom and
search upstream for the cause. Work a fixed ladder, in order of frequency: (1) No narrative question
is live — find the chapter where the last one closed without a new one opening; that's the cause,
typically 3–10 chapters earlier. (2) Beat repetition — the same emotional transaction has now
happened a third time; the fault is the second occurrence, not the third. (3) Agency loss — the
protagonist stopped choosing and started reacting; find the last real choice. (4) Promise
substitution — the book the first chapter promised is not the book being delivered; the fault is in
chapter 1. (5) Cognitive load — count new proper nouns per chapter; a late spike means the reader is
doing bookkeeping instead of caring. (6) Psychic distance drifted out and never came back in. (7)
Stakes were established as external and never converted to internal. Only after locating the cause
do you decide what to change — and the change is usually not at either location but in the machinery
that connects them.

*Mechanism:* Reader engagement is a credit balance, not a per-page property. Interest is sustained by unanswered
questions and by anticipated change; both are opened earlier and consumed later. Boredom is the
moment the balance hits zero, which is necessarily downstream of the moment deposits stopped. Fixing
chapter 20 — adding a fight, a revelation, a cliffhanger — is adrenaline injected into a body with
no blood: it produces a lurch, then the same flatness two chapters on, because the deficit was never
funded. The same logic is empirically visible at the sentence level in Sommers' study: student
writers notice lexical repetition but not conceptual repetition, and by rewording to remove the echo
they destroy the only evidence that they were saying the same thing twice.

**Gaiman's asymmetry: readers are reliable detectors, unreliable diagnosticians** · <sub>load-bearing · Neil Gaiman: 'Remember: when people tell you something's wrong or doesn't work for them, they are almost always right. When they tell you exactly what they think is wrong and how to fix it, they are almost always wrong.'</sub>

A rule for processing all feedback, professional or amateur. Treat every report of an experience ('I
got bored,' 'I didn't believe her,' 'I put it down here') as data to be trusted almost
unconditionally. Treat every proposed fix ('you need a flashback,' 'cut the sister,' 'start with the
murder') as a hypothesis to be discarded unless it survives your own diagnosis. Practically: strip
every note down to the reader-state it reports, log the page, throw the prescription away, then run
the backtracking ladder yourself.

*Mechanism:* A reader has privileged, incorrigible access to their own experience and no access at all to the
causal structure that produced it — they can only guess, and they guess from the nearest visible
feature, which is almost always the symptom site. Implementing a reader's prescription literally
attaches the repair to the wrong location and to a mechanism the book doesn't have; it also gives
the writer permission to skip diagnosis, which is the expensive part. The rule preserves the signal
and quarantines the noise.

**The editorial letter as a form (Perkins)** · <sub>strong · Maxwell Perkins to F. Scott Fitzgerald, 20 November 1924; the practice generalizes across Perkins's work with Wolfe and Hemingway and remains the trade standard for the developmental letter.</sub>

A prose document, not a list of margin notes, structured as: (a) what the book is trying to do,
given back to the author in the author's own terms; (b) unqualified specific praise, naming what
must not be lost in revision; (c) three to five diagnoses in priority order, each stated as a
reader-experience plus a hypothesis about its cause, and offered as a question rather than an
instruction; (d) an explicit statement that the decisions are the author's. Perkins's 20 November
1924 letter on The Great Gatsby is the model: he opens with 'I think you have every kind of right to
be proud of this book,' names what he loves, and then reports symptoms — 'Gatsby is somewhat vague.
The reader's eyes can never quite focus upon him, his outlines are dim' — followed by hypotheses
(readers 'are going to feel entitled to an explanation' of the money; the biography arrives as a
block that 'depart[s] from the method of the narrative') and tentative suggestions ('I thought you
might,' 'I mention the point anyway'). Fitzgerald rewrote and the book was out five months later.

*Mechanism:* Three things at once. Naming the book's intention forces the editor to prove they have read the book
that exists rather than the one they'd have written, and it gives the author an authority to revise
toward. Naming what is working prevents the panic-rewrite in which a writer, told only what is
broken, destroys what was right. Stating a diagnosis as a symptom-plus-question rather than a
prescription exploits the Gaiman asymmetry from the other side: the author is the only person with
the model of the book required to generate a fix that is consistent with the rest of it.
Prescriptions get implemented literally and badly.

**Triage, and the fix-order rule** · <sub>load-bearing · Severity triage is borrowed from software/QA but matches trade editorial practice (developmental notes precede line notes). Walter Murch, In the Blink of an Eye (1995/2001), the Rule of Six; Murch himself stresses the order matters more than the percentages.</sub>

Before touching anything, sort every finding into three bins. Blocker: the book cannot be published
or a reader cannot proceed — a structural contradiction, a dead middle, an unearned ending, a
protagonist with no want. Major: the book works but is measurably weaker — a saggy act, a flat
secondary character, an inert 30 pages. Minor: local — a clumsy paragraph, a repeated word, an
anachronism. Then apply the fix-order rule: never repair a Minor inside any region that contains an
unresolved Blocker or Major, because that region is likely to be rewritten or deleted. Work strictly
top-down, and re-triage after every blocker fix, because fixing a cause dissolves symptoms
elsewhere. Walter Murch's Rule of Six is the corresponding priority stack for individual cut
decisions — Emotion 51%, Story 23%, Rhythm 10%, Eye-Trace 7%, 2-D plane 5%, 3-D space 4% — and the
point of the numbers is that emotion outweighs everything else combined.

*Mechanism:* Revision effort is not fungible: an hour spent on a sentence in a chapter you will cut is a total
loss, and worse than a loss because it builds attachment to the chapter. Triage also solves a
motivational pathology — the writer's instinct is to fix what is easy and legible (the sentence)
rather than what is expensive and vague (the structure), so an explicit priority order defeats the
gradient toward comfortable work. Murch's weighting encodes the further fact that readers forgive
technical faults but not emotional ones: a cut that is 'wrong' by every craft rule and right
emotionally wins.

**Drawer time (deliberate decay of the intended text)** · <sub>load-bearing · Zadie Smith, 'Ten rules for writing fiction' (Guardian, 2010); Nancy Sommers (1980), experienced-writer interviews; Tom Stafford (Univ. of Sheffield) on the generalization account of typo blindness.</sub>

Put the finished draft away for a fixed, non-negotiable interval — weeks for a story, months for a
novel — and do not open it, discuss it, or take notes on it. Zadie Smith: 'Leave a decent space of
time between writing something and editing it,' and, on returning, 'try to read your own work as a
stranger would read it, or even better, as an enemy would.' Sommers' experienced writers give the
same rule from the inside: 'My cardinal rule in revising is never to fall in love with what I have
written in a first or second draft... I am much more in love with something after I have written it
than I am a day or two later.'

*Mechanism:* When you read your own sentence, two texts compete: the one on the page and the one in your head,
and the head-version wins by prediction. Tom Stafford's account of typo blindness is the sharp
version — the brain generalizes low-level detail to free capacity for meaning, and because you
already possess the meaning, it fills in and autocorrects what is actually printed. This is not only
a proofreading phenomenon: it operates at every scale. You cannot see that a scene fails to
establish a want, because you know the want. Time is the only reliable solvent for the memory trace.
Everything else on this list — changing the font, reading aloud, retyping — is a cheaper substitute
for the same effect, and none of them is as good.

**Machine read-aloud (superior to reading aloud yourself)** · <sub>strong · Reading aloud as a revision imperative is Ursula K. Le Guin's central insistence in Steering the Craft ('the sound of the language is where it all begins'); the machine-voice refinement is contemporary practice and follows directly from the prosody-supply mechanism.</sub>

Have text-to-speech read the manuscript to you at slightly-above-comfortable speed while you follow
along on the page, marking without stopping. Use your own voice only for dialogue and for passages
where you are testing performability.

*Mechanism:* Reading aloud works because subvocalization forces strictly serial processing at speech rate, which
defeats skimming and exposes what the eye slides over: breath-length failures, unintentional rhyme
and assonance, repeated words, a clause that cannot be spoken in one arc, four sentences of
identical length. But when you read your own prose aloud, you supply the intended prosody — you land
the emphasis where you meant it, you pause where the comma should have been — and thereby conceal
exactly the defects you are hunting. A synthetic voice refuses to perform your intentions. Every
sentence that only works when the author reads it is revealed as a sentence that does not work. This
makes machine read-aloud a genuine diagnostic rather than a self-flattering ritual.

**The defamiliarization stack for proofing** · <sub>useful · Standard proofreading practice across editorial associations; the prediction account follows Stafford's generalization model; the disfluency caveat refers to failed replications of Diemand-Yauman et al. (2011).</sub>

For the final passes only, break every perceptual habit the manuscript has built: change the
typeface and size, change the column width so lines break in new places, print it, read it on a
phone, change the background color, read it in a different room. For the final proof, read backwards
— by sentence for line-level errors, by word or line for typos and doubled words — and read chapters
out of order. Copyeditors additionally maintain a style sheet (names, spellings, hyphenation,
capitalization, timeline) and check the manuscript against the sheet rather than against memory.

*Mechanism:* Fluent reading is prediction-driven: the eye samples a fraction of the marks and the language model
supplies the rest. What you have re-read twenty times is stored partly as line-shape and position on
the page, so you recognize the paragraph rather than reading it. Reflowing the text destroys the
positional index and forces re-parsing. Reading backwards is the strongest version because it
removes syntactic and semantic context entirely, so prediction cannot operate and each word must be
genuinely inspected — at the cost of making all higher-level judgment impossible, which is why it
belongs only in the proof pass. Note the mechanism carefully: it is the breaking of positional and
predictive memory, NOT 'desirable difficulty.' The disfluent-font-improves-learning result (Diemand-
Yauman, Oppenheimer & Vaughan 2011) has repeatedly failed to replicate in larger samples, so do not
justify font-switching with that literature.

**Cut-10% (King's formula) — and where the 10% actually lives** · <sub>strong · Stephen King, On Writing (2000), on an anonymous rejection slip he received in 1966 — he never learned who wrote it. Elmore Leonard, 'Easy on the Adverbs, Exclamation Points and Especially Hooptedoodle,' New York Times, 2001.</sub>

Second draft equals first draft minus ten percent, applied as a hard quota over a fixed span so that
it cannot be evaded. King took the formula from a scribbled note on a rejection slip in spring 1966
— 'Not bad, but PUFFY. You need to revise for length. Formula: 2nd Draft = 1st Draft – 10%' — copied
it onto shirt cardboard and taped it beside his typewriter. The operational refinement: the 10% is
almost never distributed evenly and is almost never adjectives. It lives in connective tissue —
arrivals and departures, characters crossing rooms, greetings and leave-takings in dialogue, the
paragraph after the good paragraph that explains the good paragraph, the second example, the
restatement of the beat you already dramatized, and the sentence that tells the reader how to feel
about the sentence before it. Elmore Leonard's formulation of the target: 'Try to leave out the part
that readers tend to skip.'

*Mechanism:* A quota converts an unbounded aesthetic judgment ('is this needed?') into a bounded search problem
('find 4,000 words that are least needed'), which people are far better at. It also forces
comparative rather than absolute evaluation — you cannot defend every passage at once. The reader-
side mechanism is density: attention is renewed by new information, and every sentence that carries
none spends credit without depositing any. Redundant explanation is worse than neutral, because it
signals distrust of the reader, and a reader who feels distrusted disengages. Saunders' version of
the test is a bouncer at the door of each line, asking: what are you doing here?

**Single-defect passes** · <sub>strong · Satisfaction of search: coined by Tuddenham (1962), extensively documented by Berbaum and colleagues in radiology; the editing application is standard professional practice.</sub>

Instead of one heroic 'line edit,' run a series of narrow passes, each hunting exactly one thing
across the whole manuscript, with the manuscript scrolled fast enough that nothing else can be
attended to: a pass for filter words; a pass for chapter openings; a pass for every instance of a
named tic; a pass reading only dialogue with the narration hidden; a pass reading only the last line
of every scene; a pass on psychic-distance jumps; a pass on timeline. Ten narrow passes beat one
wide one.

*Mechanism:* Visual search degrades sharply with the number of concurrent targets, and — the sharper effect —
finding one target makes you significantly less likely to find a second one nearby. This is the
'satisfaction of search' phenomenon documented in radiology, where a radiologist who spots one
abnormality misses a second at elevated rates. Prose editing has the same structure: you fix the
clumsy verb and, satisfied, sail past the continuity error in the same paragraph. A single-target
pass sets one detection criterion and holds it, and because the target is specifiable you can
partially mechanize the search. It also converts a task that requires taste into a task that
requires only attention, which means it can be done on days when taste is unavailable.

**Retype the draft from scratch (Bell's second draft)** · <sub>strong · Matt Bell, Refuse to Be Done: How to Write and Rewrite a Novel in Three Drafts (2022).</sub>

For the second draft, open a blank document and retype the entire novel, every word, with the first
draft on a second screen for reference only — redlining passages in the old draft as you carry them
across. Matt Bell's rule: 'When in doubt, rewrite instead of revise.' Nothing survives by default;
every sentence must be re-chosen by being retyped.

*Mechanism:* Editing in place is a defensive operation: the existing text is the default, and each change must
justify itself against inertia, so the draft's original architecture survives even when it is the
problem. Retyping inverts the default — now every sentence must earn inclusion, and the ones you
cannot be bothered to retype are, by revealed preference, the ones that were dead. There is also a
motor-cognitive effect: producing the sentence again puts you back in generative mode inside the
constraints of a known story, so improvements arrive as new writing rather than as patches, and the
prose keeps a single-pass consistency of voice that patched prose never has. The cost is enormous,
which is why it belongs to the structural draft and not the polish.

**Topic and stress positions; characters as subjects, actions as verbs** · <sub>load-bearing · George Gopen & Judith Swan, 'The Science of Scientific Writing,' American Scientist (Nov–Dec 1990); Joseph M. Williams, Style: Lessons in Clarity and Grace; the given–new contract is Clark & Haviland.</sub>

The one line-editing doctrine with a real psycholinguistic mechanism, and the first thing to run on
any paragraph that is 'clear but exhausting.' Three operations. (1) Underline the first seven or
eight words of every sentence: if they are not old, already-established information, the reader has
no place to attach the new material. (2) Put the information you want emphasized at the END of the
sentence, in the stress position, and make sure nothing trails after it. Gopen and Swan: 'in the
stress position the reader needs and expects closure and fulfillment; in the topic position the
reader needs and expects perspective and context.' (3) Find the sentence's real actor and make it
the grammatical subject; find its real action and make it the verb — de-nominalize ('made a
decision' → 'decided'). The governing reader-expectation: 'Readers expect a unit of discourse to be
a story about whoever shows up first.'

*Mechanism:* Working memory holds a sentence in a buffer until it can be integrated. Old-information-first (the
given–new contract) means the integration point is available before the load arrives; new-
information-first means the reader must hold everything unattached until the anchor appears, which
is where the sense of effortful prose comes from. The stress position works because sentence-final
material receives prosodic prominence even in silent reading, so emphasis placed there is felt
rather than asserted — which is why a sentence whose best word is buried mid-clause reads as flat
regardless of how good the word is. And because readers automatically construct an agent-action
model, a sentence whose grammatical subject is an abstraction forces them to run a translation step
on every clause. Crucially, this doctrine also explains when the passive voice is correct: if the
patient is the topic, the passive puts old information first and is the right choice.

**Consecution: sonic line editing** · <sub>useful · Gordon Lish's teaching, as described by Gary (now Garielle) Lutz, 'The Sentence Is a Lonely Place,' The Believer, January 2009.</sub>

Gordon Lish's sentence poetics, as transmitted by Gary/Garielle Lutz: build and revise the sentence
so that each word is generated out of the phonetic and letteral material of the one before it — 'a
recursive procedure by which one word pursues itself into its successor.' Operationally, when a
sentence is inert, do not reach for a synonym of higher register; instead look at the consonants and
vowels already present and find the successor word that shares them. Lutz's example, from Christine
Schutt: 'Here is the house at night, lit up tall and tallowy.' The revision question is not 'is this
the right word for the meaning' but 'do these words look and sound as though they have lived
together.' Lutz's diagnosis of ordinary prose: sentences left as 'undeveloped parcels of literary
real estate.'

*Mechanism:* Reading involves phonological recoding even when silent; sound is not decoration laid over meaning
but part of how meaning is processed. Words that share phonetic material bind more tightly in the
buffer, so a sonically-consecuted sentence feels inevitable and is more memorable — the felt quality
of 'rightness' in prose is very largely this. For the writer, the mechanism is different and
arguably more useful: forcing yourself to find the next word inside the previous word's sound blocks
the first, most available, most clichéd continuation, so consecution operates as a generator of
unexpected diction rather than merely a filter. The known danger is that it can be run to the point
where sound governs sense and the prose becomes a display case — which is precisely the charge laid
against the Lish school.

**Psychic distance audit** · <sub>strong · John Gardner, The Art of Fiction (1983); 'the vivid and continuous dream' is his central criterion.</sub>

Gardner's five-level scale, from the wholly external ('It was winter of the year 1853. A large man
stepped out of a doorway') through named identification, to interiority, to unmediated consciousness
('Snow. Under your collar, down inside your shoes'). Mark each paragraph 1–5. Then look for:
unmotivated jumps of more than one level; a whole chapter locked at a single level; a scene that
should be a close-up but is playing at level 2; a scene needing summary that is stuck at level 5.
Gardner's governing standard is 'the vivid and continuous dream' — anything that wakes the reader
out of it is a defect regardless of its local merits.

*Mechanism:* Psychic distance is the reader's camera. Because it is invisible as a technical device, its failures
are experienced as inexplicable feelings — a chapter that feels cold, a death that doesn't land, a
long stretch that seems to take forever. A sudden unmotivated jump breaks the dream in the same way
a jump cut does in film: the reader's model of where they are standing is invalidated, and re-
establishing it costs attention that was being spent on the story. Sustained distance is why
'nothing happens' chapters feel dead — not because nothing happens but because the reader is never
let close enough to care that it does. Auditing it converts a vague affective complaint into a
specific, locatable, fixable technical property.

**Filter words and RUE (Resist the Urge to Explain)** · <sub>strong · 'Filter' as a term is Janet Burroway's (Writing Fiction); RUE is Renni Browne & Dave King, Self-Editing for Fiction Writers; the adverb-in-dialogue-attribution case is Leonard's and King's.</sub>

Two mechanical line passes. Filtering: search for the perceptual verbs that insert the POV character
as an observing apparatus between the reader and the event — saw, heard, felt, noticed, watched,
realized, seemed, thought, wondered, could see. 'She saw the door swing open' becomes 'The door
swung open.' Delete unless the act of perceiving is itself the event. RUE: after every line of
dialogue and every dramatized action, delete the sentence that explains what it meant. 'You're
late,' she said angrily, resenting him for it — cut the adverb, cut the gloss, and if the anger
isn't there, fix the line, not the label.

*Mechanism:* Filtering adds a layer of narration between the reader and the thing, which increases psychic
distance without the writer deciding to; the reader is told that a perception occurred rather than
being given the perception, and the sensory simulation that produces immersion doesn't fire. RUE's
mechanism is about trust and inference: readers derive meaning by inference, and inference is
participatory — it produces the sense of having discovered rather than been told, which is where
reader investment comes from. An explanatory gloss preempts the inference, converting the reader
from participant to audience, and simultaneously signals that the writer did not believe the
