dramatization worked. Note the correct scope: this is an argument about proportion, not a
prohibition. Summary and telling are how a novel controls pace; a book that only shows is unreadably
slow.

**Escalation audit: refuse to repeat a beat** · <sub>strong · George Saunders, A Swim in a Pond in the Rain (2021).</sub>

List every scene's emotional transaction in a single verb phrase ('she almost tells him and
doesn't'; 'he is humiliated by his father'). Then look for duplicates. Saunders' rule: 'Refuse to
repeat beats. Once a story has moved forward, through some fundamental change in the character's
condition, we don't get to enact that change again.' Where a beat recurs, either delete the later
instance or raise it so that it is a different transaction — higher cost, worse timing, a witness,
an irreversible consequence. His related move, 'ritual banality avoidance': having identified the
obvious answer to the question the story has raised, deny yourself that answer and see what better
one arrives.

*Mechanism:* Reader attention is driven by change in state, and state change is only perceptible as difference
from the prior state. A repeated beat therefore registers as zero change no matter how well it is
written — which is exactly why 'this scene is beautiful but the book feels stalled' is such a common
note. This is the single most frequent cause of a middle that sags: not that nothing happens, but
that the same thing keeps happening. The audit works because repetition is invisible in serial
reading (each instance feels fresh in the moment) and glaringly obvious in a list. Ritual banality
avoidance works on the writer's cognition instead: the first answer to any narrative question is the
one that arrives fastest, which means it is the most available, which means it is the one the reader
has also already thought of.

**Energy accounting: the ending spends what the opening deposited** · <sub>strong · George Saunders, A Swim in a Pond in the Rain — 'causality is to the writer what melody is to the songwriter'; the planted/fired ledger is the operational form of Chekhov's gun run in both directions.</sub>

Saunders: 'A story is a system for the transfer of energy. Energy, hopefully, gets made in the early
pages and the trick, in the later pages, is to use that energy.' The revision operation: make two
lists. Everything the first third plants — objects, relationships, unresolved lines, images, stated
fears, capacities, obligations. Everything the last third spends. Then check the ledger both ways.
Planted-and-never-fired items are either cut or fired. Fired-but-never-planted items are the reason
an ending feels arbitrary; the fix is upstream, in the planting, not in the ending. A good ending is
not a new event; it is the escalation and cashing-out of material already in the reader's hands,
which is why it can feel simultaneously surprising and inevitable.

*Mechanism:* Endings are evaluated retroactively against the whole text. An ending that introduces new causal
material has nothing in the reader's memory to bind to, so it registers as imposed — the author
showed up. An ending built from planted material triggers a recognition event: the reader retrieves
the earlier item and re-reads it in a new light, which is experienced as meaning being discovered
rather than delivered. The satisfaction is produced by the reader's own retrieval, not by the event.
This is also why 'my ending is weak' is almost always a misdiagnosis: the ending is where the
deficit becomes visible, but the deficit was created in the first hundred pages when nothing was
deposited.

**The P/N meter: thousands of micro-decisions, no theory** · <sub>load-bearing · George Saunders, 'What Writers Really Do When They Write,' The Guardian, 4 March 2017; and his Story Club post 'First Thohts on Reviision,' where he describes 'Me Watching Me Watching Me.'</sub>

Saunders' actual daily method. Read your own page in the morning, cold, at reading speed,
'uninflectedly, the way a first-time reader might,' with an imagined meter: 'I imagine a meter
mounted in my forehead, with P on this side (Positive) and N on this side (Negative).' Where the
needle dips toward N, change something — immediately, without theorizing about why — and reread.
Then again. The unit of work is the micro-decision, made intuitively and fast: 'The artist, in this
model, is like the optometrist, always asking: Is it better like this? Or like this?' Repeat 'over
and over, for months, sometimes years, until that needle stays up in the P zone for the whole length
of the text,' through 'sometimes hundreds of drafts.' The result: 'Like a cruise ship slowly
turning, the story will start to alter course via those thousands of incremental adjustments.'

*Mechanism:* Three mechanisms stack. (1) Taste is a far higher-resolution instrument than theory: you can
reliably detect that a line is worse long before you can articulate why, and demanding an
articulation before acting discards most of the signal. Saunders' analogy is reaction, not analysis
— 'like trying to hit a baseball: you wait (you read), you react.' (2) Ten thousand micro-
preferences accumulate into a style that is genuinely the writer's, because each one is a sample
from her taste rather than from her ideas about writing; the text converges on 'more of her
essential nature' and 'outgrows her early conception of it.' (3) Because each pass changes what the
following pass encounters, the process is a search, not an application — which is why the story ends
up smarter than the writer's plan and why Saunders reports the result is 'a story that is better
than I am in real life.' The cost of this method is that it is unusable for structural problems: it
optimizes locally, and a cruise ship turning slowly will not find a different ocean.

**Darlings: kill, or save to the lifeboat?** · <sub>contested · Arthur Quiller-Couch, 'On Style,' On the Art of Writing (lectures 1913–14, pub. 1916); Peter Ho Davies, The Art of Revision: The Last Word (Graywolf, 2021), excerpted as 'Save Your Darlings.'</sub>

The received rule is Quiller-Couch's, from his 1913–14 Cambridge lectures: 'Whenever you feel an
impulse to perpetrate a piece of exceptionally fine writing, obey it — whole-heartedly — and delete
it before sending your manuscript to press. Murder your darlings.' Peter Ho Davies proposes the
correction: darlings are typically scaffolding — passages, usually early, 'that essentially enable
us to write' the book — and the operation is to save them to a separate file (he calls it a
lifeboat), because 'the totality of our writing' outlives any one project. His examples include Brit
Bennett moving a character from a 2012 workshop story into The Vanishing Half. The synthesis a
working novelist should adopt: the darling must come OUT of this draft, because the test is whether
it is doing work here, not whether it is good; but it must not be destroyed, because a darling is
usually the seed the book grew from and belongs in a book you haven't written yet. And when a
darling is the best thing in a chapter, suspect the chapter, not the darling.

*Mechanism:* Q's mechanism is about calibration: the writer's affection for a passage is a signal about the
writing experience, not about the reading experience, and the two systematically diverge. Prose
written in a state of self-delight is usually prose in which the writer was the audience. Davies'
mechanism is different and complementary: early material carries the cognitive load that let the
draft exist at all — it was the ramp — and ramps are load-bearing during construction and dead
weight afterward, which is why they are simultaneously the hardest to cut and the least necessary.
The lifeboat matters psychologically: writers under-cut because deletion feels like destruction, and
a file that guarantees nothing is destroyed measurably raises willingness to cut. Beware the
fashionable overcorrection — 'kill your darlings' recited as proof of rigor produces books that are
competent, safe, and have nothing anyone would want to steal.

**Radical subtraction, and its price (Lish on Carver)** · <sub>contested · D. T. Max's reporting from the Lish papers at Indiana University (New Yorker, 2007); Carver's original manuscript published as Beginners (Library of America, 2009); Carver's July 1980 letters to Lish; Giles Harvey, 'The Two Raymond Carvers,' NYRB, 27 May 2010.</sub>

The most extreme documented case of editorial revision. Gordon Lish cut Raymond Carver's manuscript
for What We Talk About When We Talk About Love by more than half; three stories lost at least 70
percent; ten stories were retitled; fourteen endings were rewritten. 'A Small, Good Thing' became
the truncated 'The Bath.' In 'One More Thing,' Carver's extended closing speech became: 'But then he
could not think what it could possibly be.' What was gained is real and is the reason Carver
matters: the reticence, the emphasis on the unsaid, the leanness that defined a decade of American
fiction. What was lost is also real: Carver's original 'Beginners,' published in 2009, is warmer,
more forgiving, more explicitly emotional. What it cost the writer is documented too — on 8 July
1980 Carver wrote to Lish begging him to stop production: 'I'm afraid, mortally afraid, I feel it,
that if the book were to be published as it is in its present edited form, I may never write another
story.' A week later he had reversed himself. The operational lesson: when subtracting, subtract
explanation, consequence, and consolation — and know that each of those removals converts a warm
story into a cold one, which is a decision about what the book is, not a cleanup.

*Mechanism:* Omission works because readers fill gaps automatically and involuntarily, and what a reader supplies
is felt more strongly than what they are given — the inference is theirs. Cutting the consolatory
ending leaves the reader holding an unresolved emotion with nowhere to put it, which is why the
Lish-cut Carvers hurt. But the same mechanism has a hard limit: past a certain density of omission
the reader stops inferring and starts guessing, and guessing produces detachment, not intensity. The
second, subtler mechanism is about the writer: a subtractive editor who is right about the page can
still be wrong about the writer, because the removed material was where the writer's actual feeling
lived, and a writer taught to distrust his own warmth may stop producing it. The aesthetic verdict
and the process verdict genuinely come apart here.

**Fixing the cause, not the symptom — as an explicit rule with a test** · <sub>load-bearing · Nancy Sommers, CCC 31:4 (1980) — including the verbatim student report above; the one-to-many test is the operational form standard in professional developmental practice.</sub>

Before making any change, state the fix as a sentence of the form: 'The reader felt X at page N
because Y at page M.' If you cannot fill in M with a location earlier than N, you have not diagnosed
anything and you are about to patch. The corollary test: a genuine cause-fix dissolves several
logged symptoms at once. If your repair eliminates exactly one item from the symptom list, it was
probably a patch. Keep the symptom list and re-check it after each structural change; the number of
symptoms retired per change is your diagnostic accuracy score.

*Mechanism:* Symptom-fixing is the default because symptoms are legible, local, and cheap, while causes are
diffuse, upstream, and expensive — the gradient of effort points the wrong way. It is also self-
concealing: a patch removes the evidence. Sommers' students demonstrate the pure form — they reword
to remove a repeated word and thereby hide the fact that they said the same thing twice, and they
report 'I knew something larger was wrong, but I didn't think it would help to move words around.'
They were not unwilling to revise; they lacked strategies for identifying the 'something larger.'
The one-to-many test works because a real cause has multiple downstream consequences by definition —
that is what makes it a cause — so a fix that retires one symptom has, by arithmetic, not touched a
cause.

**The anti-revision position (Heinlein's Rule 3), and where it is right** · <sub>contested · Robert A. Heinlein, 'On the Writing of Speculative Fiction' (1947); Dean Wesley Smith's Killing the Sacred Cows of Publishing; Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (Columbia lecture, 2008; in Changing My Mind); George Saunders, 'Method, Shmethod' (Story Club).</sub>

Robert A. Heinlein's business rules for writers include: you must finish what you start, and 'you
must refrain from rewriting, except to editorial order.' Dean Wesley Smith has built a contemporary
school on this, arguing that most revision is 'critical voice' damage — the writer sanding away
exactly the idiosyncrasy that made the work worth reading. Zadie Smith's distinction explains who
each position fits: Macro Planners outline and restructure heavily and revise in large architectural
moves; Micro Managers, like Smith herself, build 'floor by floor,' finishing each sentence before
proceeding, so their first draft is already their tenth. And Saunders — the writer most identified
with obsessive revision — refuses the dogma directly: 'Method is just a means to an end and can be
changed and discarded at will,' noting he wrote 'Sticks' in one afternoon with light revision.

*Mechanism:* Revision has a real cost curve, not just a benefit curve. Repeated passes flatten prose toward the
writer's median taste, because each pass removes what the current mood dislikes, and the
intersection of many moods is blandness; this is the honest core of the Heinlein position, and it is
why some heavily-revised books read as competent and dead. It is also true that for a Micro Manager,
the revision has already happened at the sentence, so a subsequent 'draft two' is redundant labor.
But Heinlein's rule is simply wrong for structural work: novel architecture cannot be discovered in
a first pass because the writer does not yet know what the book is about — Sommers' experienced
writers say precisely this ('I need to keep writing a first draft until I figure out what I want to
say. Then in a second draft, I begin to see the structure of an argument'). The correct synthesis:
never revise to remove strangeness, always revise to fix structure, and know which kind of writer
you are before choosing a system.

### Non-obvious findings — Revision systems: how books actually get fixed — p

- The most useful empirical result in the revision literature is about repetition, and it inverts the
  usual advice. Sommers (1980) found that weak revisers 'are aware of lexical repetition, but not
  conceptual repetition' — they hear a repeated word, reach for a synonym, and by fixing the symptom
  they destroy the only visible evidence that they had said the same thing twice. So the correct
  response to a repeated word is not to reword it but to check whether the idea is repeated. This
  makes a word-echo report a STRUCTURAL diagnostic, not a cosmetic one, which is the opposite of how
  every style checker treats it.

- Sommers' students were not lazy. Asked why they hadn't changed more, they said: 'I knew something
  larger was wrong, but I didn't think it would help to move words around.' They had detection and
  lacked only a procedure for the large scale. This reframes revision teaching entirely: the deficit
  is never willingness or taste, it is the absence of a named ladder to climb from a felt symptom to
  a structural cause.

- George Saunders — the writer most identified with obsessive iterative revision — explicitly
  repudiates method dogma. In 'Method, Shmethod' he writes that 'Method is just a means to an end
  and can be changed and discarded at will,' and cites his own story 'Sticks,' written in one
  afternoon and lightly revised. The P/N meter is a description of his cognition, not a
  prescription; and critically, it is a LOCAL optimizer. It cannot fix a book's architecture. A
  cruise ship turning slowly does not find a different ocean.

- Reading your work aloud in your own voice is partly self-defeating, and nobody says so. You supply
  the intended prosody — you land the stress where you meant it, you pause where the comma should
  have been — thereby concealing exactly the defects you are hunting. A synthetic voice refuses to
  perform your intentions, which makes text-to-speech a strictly better diagnostic than your own
  mouth for everything except testing dialogue.

- Changing the font for a proofread is good practice for the wrong reason. The 'disfluency improves
  processing' result (Diemand-Yauman, Oppenheimer & Vaughan 2011) has repeatedly failed to replicate
  at scale. The real mechanism is far more mundane and more reliable: a re-typeset page breaks lines
  in new places, destroying the positional and line-shape memory that lets you recognize a paragraph
  instead of reading it. Reflow the text, don't uglify it.

- Finding one defect makes you worse at finding the next one nearby. This is 'satisfaction of search,'
  documented in radiology since Tuddenham (1962) — a radiologist who spots one abnormality misses a
  second at elevated rates. It is the strongest argument for many narrow single-target passes over
  one heroic line edit, and it means the paragraph you just fixed is now the most dangerous
  paragraph in the manuscript.

- Perkins's famous Gatsby letter contains almost no instructions. He reports his own reading
  experience as symptom ('Gatsby is somewhat vague. The reader's eyes can never quite focus upon
  him, his outlines are dim'), offers a hypothesis, and hedges the suggestion ('I mention the point
  anyway'). The greatest editorial letter in American publishing is structured as diagnosis plus
  question, never prescription — the exact inverse of modern tracked-changes line-note culture, and
  it works for the reason Gaiman names: the author is the only person with the model of the book
  required to generate a fix consistent with the rest of it.

- Stephen King's 10% rule has no craft pedigree whatsoever. It came from an anonymous scribble on a
  rejection slip he received in 1966 ('Not bad, but PUFFY... Formula: 2nd Draft = 1st Draft – 10%'),
  and he never learned who wrote it. The most widely-followed revision heuristic in commercial
  fiction is an unattributed one-liner from a magazine slush pile.

- The Lish–Carver case is usually argued as 'was the edit good?' — but the documented record shows the
  aesthetic verdict and the human verdict genuinely coming apart. The edit was over 50% of the book,
  ten retitled stories and fourteen rewritten endings; it produced the reticent style that defined a
  literary decade; and it terrified Carver into writing 'I'm afraid, mortally afraid... I may never
  write another story,' before he reversed himself a week later. A subtractive edit can
  simultaneously make a better book and damage the writer's access to the material that made him
  worth editing.

- 'Kill your darlings' may be diagnosing the wrong thing. Peter Ho Davies argues darlings are usually
  SCAFFOLDING — early passages 'that essentially enable us to write' the book. That reframes them:
  they are load-bearing during construction and dead weight afterward, which explains why they are
  simultaneously the hardest to cut and the least necessary, and why the right operation is to ask
  what they were holding up rather than to admire your own ruthlessness. The corollary practitioners
  rarely state: when the darling is the best thing in the chapter, the chapter is the problem.

- Strunk & White's most-repeated rule is technically incompetent. Geoffrey Pullum ('50 Years of Stupid
  Grammar Advice,' Chronicle of Higher Education, 2009) showed that most of the examples E. B. White
  offers to illustrate the passive voice are not passives at all. The reader-expectation framework
  (Gopen & Swan; Williams) explains what the rule was groping at and gives the correct decision
  procedure: use the passive precisely when the patient is the topic, because that puts old
  information first, where the reader needs it.

- 'Write drunk, edit sober' is not Hemingway. The line is commonly traced to Peter De Vries's 1964
  novel Reuben, Reuben, where the character Gowan McGland says something closer to 'sometimes I
  write drunk and revise sober, and sometimes I write sober and revise drunk.' It survives because
  it encodes something true — generation and evaluation must be separated — attached to a name that
  lends it authority it never had.

## Drafting practice and the writer's working day: how a first draft actually gets made, and the cognitive mechanisms that make each working habit function

**The visible tally, not the daily rule** · <sub>load-bearing · Anthony Trollope, An Autobiography (1883), chs. VII and XV (verified in the Gutenberg text); empirically corroborated by Robert Boice, 'Contingency management in writing and the appearance of creative ideas,' Behaviour Research and Therapy 21:537-543 (1983).</sub>

Trollope wrote with a watch on the desk and required 250 words per quarter-hour, three hours a day,
from 5:30 a.m. But the load-bearing instrument was not the hourly rate: before starting each book he
ruled a diary divided into weeks and allotted himself so many pages per week (page = exactly 250
counted words; average 40/week, range 20 to 112), entering the day's pages so that 'if at any time I
have slipped into idleness for a day or two, the record of that idleness has been there, staring me
in the face, and demanding of me increased labour, so that the deficiency might be supplied.'
Weekly, not daily, accounting: a bad day is recoverable inside the week. Operationally: fix a per-
book weekly page target, log daily, and make the log physically visible.

*Mechanism:* Three separate mechanisms stack. (1) An unbounded goal ('write the novel') gives no signal whether
the current sentence is progress, and goals without progress signals generate anxiety, which
produces avoidance; a counted quota converts the day into a completable unit with a closed effort-
to-completion loop. (2) The chart exploits loss aversion — an empty row is a visible loss, which
motivates more reliably than a prospective gain. (3) Timing removes the decision. Trollope's point
that 'a small daily task, if it be really daily, will beat the labours of a spasmodic Hercules' is a
claim about avoiding restart costs: every lapse forces a fresh reconstruction of the situation
model, which is the most expensive cognitive operation in the whole enterprise. Weekly accounting
protects the streak's benefits without making a single missed day into a moral event that triggers
abandonment.

**Scheduled sessions beat inspiration — with a ceiling** · <sub>strong · Robert Boice (1983), Behaviour Research and Therapy 21:537-543; re-analysis by Stephen Krashen, 'Optimal Levels of Writing Management: A Re-Analysis of Boice (1983),' Education 122(3):605-608 (2002).</sub>

Boice (1983) randomly assigned 27 blocked academics to three conditions over ten weeks. Abstinence
(told not to write): 0.1-0.2 pages/day. Spontaneous (scheduled slots but write only when in the
mood): 0.3-0.4 pages/day. Regular (write in the scheduled slot whether in the mood or not, no quota,
no penalty): 0.9 pages/day. Contingency (five sessions/week, three pages each, $15 to a despised
organization for any miss): 3.2 pages/day. Creative ideas per day tracked the same order: 0.06-0.09,
0.21-0.32, 0.63, 1.39. Operationally: schedule the session and keep it regardless of mood; add
external stakes only if regularity alone fails.

*Mechanism:* Writing avoidance is anxiety-maintained operant behavior: not-writing is negatively reinforced
because it removes the discomfort of the blank page, so the habit strengthens itself. A scheduled
slot moves the behavior under stimulus control (time and place cue writing) rather than mood
control, which is where the folk model puts it. The idea-generation result is the important one:
producing prose is itself the generative act, not the transcription of ideas generated elsewhere —
so more sessions produce more ideas, not fewer. BUT Krashen's re-analysis found ideas *per page*
were identical for the spontaneous and regular groups (~0.8) and nearly halved under punishment
(0.43). Coercion buys throughput at the cost of per-page inventiveness. The regular condition, not
the punished one, is the optimum in the actual data.

**Stop while you still know what happens next** · <sub>load-bearing · Ernest Hemingway, Esquire (1935), Paris Review Art of Fiction No. 21 (1958), A Moveable Feast (1964); Ovsiankina (1928) vs Zeigarnik (1927), replication status per the 2025 meta-analytic review summarized in current literature.</sub>

End the session mid-momentum, with the next move already known and unwritten — Hemingway stopped
mid-scene, Graham Greene reportedly stopped mid-sentence at his 500 words. Hemingway, Esquire,
October 1935 ('Monologue to the Maestro'): 'The best way is always to stop when you are going good
and when you know what will happen next. If you do that every day when you are writing a novel you
will never be stuck.' Paris Review (Art of Fiction No. 21, 1958): 'You write until you come to a
place where you still have your juice and know what will happen next and you stop.' The corollary,
from A Moveable Feast: 'never to empty the well of my writing, but always to stop when there was
still something there in the deep part of the well.'

*Mechanism:* Usually justified by the Zeigarnik effect (better memory for interrupted tasks) — but that effect
largely fails to replicate. The real mechanism is the Ovsiankina effect: the *motivational urge to
resume* an interrupted task, which does hold up in meta-analysis. So the trick works by making
tomorrow's start attractive, not by helping you remember. Second mechanism: the expensive part of
any session is reloading the situation model into working memory. Starting from a known next move
means the first thirty minutes produce text instead of deliberation, and early production sets the
session's affective tone. Third: it is deliberate under-production. Quota-maximizers who write until
empty run the anti-Hemingway — they buy today's page count with tomorrow's cold start.

**Do not think about the book between sessions** · <sub>strong · Hemingway, A Moveable Feast (1964); Sio, U. N. and Ormerod, T. C., 'Does incubation enhance problem solving? A meta-analytic review,' Psychological Bulletin 135(1):94-120 (2009).</sub>

Hemingway, A Moveable Feast: 'I learned not to think about anything that I was writing from the time
I stopped writing until I started again the next day. That way my subconscious would be working on
it and at the same time I would be listening to other people and noticing everything.'
Operationally: after the session, do something absorbing but cognitively light — walking, driving,
cooking, physical work — rather than either ruminating on the manuscript or doing demanding
unrelated work.

*Mechanism:* Sio and Ormerod's meta-analysis (117 studies) found a reliable positive incubation effect, with
three moderators that make this actionable. (1) Low-cognitive-demand interpolated activity
outperforms both rest and high-demand activity for divergent problems — a walk beats a nap and beats
answering email. (2) Longer preparation before the break produces a larger incubation effect — you
must be genuinely stuck on a well-specified problem for incubation to pay. (3) The effect is larger
for divergent than for linguistic-insight tasks. The best-supported explanations are not mystical:
forgetting-of-fixation (the wrong solution path decays and stops blocking retrieval) and
opportunistic assimilation (the unsolved problem persists as a retrieval cue, so ordinary
environmental input can trigger the solution — which is exactly Hemingway's 'listening to other
people and noticing everything').

**Bounded re-entry: read yesterday's pages aloud, and only yesterday's** · <sub>load-bearing · Anthony Trollope, An Autobiography (1883), ch. XV; the read-aloud principle is also the organizing insistence of Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft (1998/2015).</sub>

Trollope: 'I always began my task by reading the work of the day before, an operation which would
take me half an hour, and which consisted chiefly in weighing with my ear the sound of the words and
phrases... by reading what he has last written, just before he recommences his task, the writer will
catch the tone and spirit of what he is then saying, and will avoid the fault of seeming to be
unlike himself.' Half an hour out of a three-hour session — one sixth. The boundary is the whole
point: yesterday's pages, aloud, then forward.

*Mechanism:* Voice consistency is not a decision; it is prosodic and syntactic entrainment. Reading the previous
day's prose aloud re-primes sentence-length distribution, rhythm, register, and lexical field at a
sub-deliberative level, so today's sentences match without the writer having to hold a style
specification in working memory. The 'seeming to be unlike himself' failure is what happens when a
draft is written across many disconnected re-entries. Reading *aloud* rather than silently matters
because the ear detects repetition, clumsy rhythm, and accidental rhyme far faster than the eye,
which predicts and smooths. The bound (yesterday only) is what separates this from the rereading
spiral, which is a different and destructive behavior.

**Never reread the whole draft mid-draft** · <sub>strong · Stephen King, On Writing (2000); mechanism grounded in Flower and Hayes's cognitive process model and Ronald Kellogg's attentional-overload account of writing (Kellogg, JEP:LMC 14:355-365, 1988).</sub>

Distinct from the practice above. Do not go back to page one to 'see where you are' while the draft
is unfinished. Track state in an external document (a running list of what has happened, who knows
what, open threads) rather than by rereading. Stephen King's version is writing the first draft with
the door closed and not going back; Zadie Smith's Micro Manager exception is instructive precisely
because it is bought at enormous cost (two years on twenty pages).

*Mechanism:* Three costs. (1) Generation and evaluation compete for the same executive resources; rereading
activates the evaluative mode and it does not switch off cleanly when you resume writing — the next
pages come out cautious and prefabricated. (2) Processing fluency: rereading your own text makes it
feel more finished than it is, because ease of processing is misread as quality. You come back from
a reread with a false completeness signal. (3) Sunk cost: polished early pages constrain what you
are still willing to discover, because changing them now costs visible work. Externalizing state
solves the legitimate need (continuity) without paying any of the three costs.

**Shitty first drafts: separate generation from evaluation** · <sub>load-bearing · Anne Lamott, Bird by Bird (1994); Peter Elbow, Writing Without Teachers (1973); knowledge-telling from Bereiter and Scardamalia.</sub>

Lamott's chapter in Bird by Bird (1994) and her staging: 'the first draft is the down draft — you
just get it down. The second draft is the up draft — you fix it up. And the third draft is the
dental draft, where you check every tooth.' The operational content is not encouragement; it is a
rule about which criteria are permitted to operate on which pass. On the down draft, the only
admissible criterion is 'is there text.'

*Mechanism:* Peter Elbow's freewriting argument (Writing Without Teachers, 1973) is the precise version:
generating and judging use incompatible criteria, and running them simultaneously does not average
them — it collapses generation to whatever survives immediate judgment, which is always the safe,
familiar, prefabricated phrase. That is Bereiter and Scardamalia's 'knowledge-telling': retrieving
what you already have formulated rather than constituting anything new. The permission is also
anxiety management: naming the draft as bad in advance removes the evaluative threat that drives
avoidance, which is the same lever Boice's contingency data pulls from the other side.

**Short assignments and the one-inch picture frame** · <sub>load-bearing · Anne Lamott, Bird by Bird (1994), 'Short Assignments'; load mechanism from Ronald T. Kellogg, 'Attentional overload and writing performance,' JEP:LMC 14(2):355-365 (1988).</sub>

Lamott keeps a one-inch picture frame on her desk: 'all I have to do is to write down as much as I
can see through a one-inch picture frame.' The day's assignment is not 'the chapter' but a single
perceptual unit — one room, one exchange, one gesture. The book's title comes from her father
telling her overwhelmed ten-year-old brother, facing a report on birds, 'Bird by bird, buddy. Just
take it bird by bird.'

*Mechanism:* Two distinct effects. (1) Goal proximity: an unbounded goal produces no completion signal, and the
absence of a completion signal is what turns effort into dread. A one-scene target restores a unit
you can finish today. (2) Working-memory load: Kellogg's overload research shows writing quality
collapses when the writer must simultaneously hold content, structure, and language. Narrowing the
frame means only one situation has to be held in mind at a time, freeing capacity for sentence-level
work. The visual prop matters more than it sounds — it re-cues the constraint at the moment of
drift, which is precisely when the abstract intention has stopped being available.

**Write with the door closed — and do not describe the book to anyone** · <sub>load-bearing · Stephen King, On Writing (2000); Gollwitzer, P. M., Sheeran, P., Michalski, V., and Seifert, A. E., 'When Intentions Go Public,' Psychological Science 20(5):612-618 (2009).</sub>

King: 'Write with the door closed, rewrite with the door open. Your stuff starts out being just for
you, in other words, but then it goes out.' The stricter form, widely practiced and rarely
justified: do not narrate the plot of the unwritten novel to friends, at parties, or to an agent.
Talk about the book only when you have a draft.

*Mechanism:* The obvious mechanism is that premature audience-modeling adds a rhetorical constraint to a working
memory already saturated by content generation. The non-obvious and stronger mechanism is measured:
Gollwitzer, Sheeran, Michalski and Seifert found that when an identity-relevant intention is noticed
by others, the social recognition partially substitutes for the act — participants whose goals were
acknowledged put in *less* subsequent effort and felt closer to the identity they had not yet
earned. Describing your novel well at dinner gives you a portion of the reward of having written it.
This is symbolic self-completion, and it is why the writers who talk best about their unwritten
books so often do not write them.

**Situation, not plot** · <sub>contested · Stephen King, On Writing (2000).</sub>

King's method: start from a predicament rather than a sequence. Put people you find interesting into
a situation with pressure and no obvious exit, and write to find out what they do. King's stated
distrust of plot is that it is 'the good writer's last resort and the dullard's first choice.'
Practically: the pre-writing artifact is a paragraph describing a situation and a cast, not a beat
sheet.

*Mechanism:* Two mechanisms, one about the reader and one about the writer. On the reader: prose written while
the writer genuinely does not know the outcome carries local surprise at the sentence level — the
syntax is interrogating rather than reporting, and readers detect the difference even when they
cannot name it. On the writer: characters under real pressure generate behavior an outline cannot
predict, and unpredicted behavior is the only reliable source of non-generic material, because
everything you can foresee is by definition already inside your existing repertoire. The cost is
real and King understates it: discovery drafts have structural failure rates that outlines do not,
and the failure is usually invisible until the last third.

**Know whether you are a Macro Planner or a Micro Manager — it is a measurable disposition, not a preference** · <sub>load-bearing · Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling,' lecture at Columbia University, 24 March 2008, published in The Believer (June 2008) and Changing My Mind (2009); David Galbraith, 'Writing as a knowledge-constituting process'; Baaijen, Galbraith and de Glopper, 'Effects of writing beliefs and planning on writing performance,' Learning and Instruction 33:81-91 (2014); Baaijen and Galbraith, 'Discovery Through Writing,' Cognition and Instruction 36(2):199-223 (2018).</sub>

Zadie Smith's terms: 'A Macro Planner makes notes, organizes material, configures a plot, and
creates a structure — all before he writes the title page. Because of this structural security, he
has a great deal of freedom, of movement. It's not uncommon for Macro Planners to start writing
their novels in the middle.' Versus: 'Personally, I'm a Micro Manager. I start at the first sentence
of a novel and I finish at the last... Micro Managers like me build a house floor by floor,
discreetly and in its entirety. Each floor needs to be sturdy and fully decorated with all the
furniture in place before the next is built on top of it.' And decisively: 'Because I edit as I go
along, I don't have first, second, third drafts. I only have one draft, and when it's done, it's
done.'

*Mechanism:* Smith's own explanation is the key one: 'for a Micro Manager, [structure, plot, characters] are
contained in the sensibility of a sentence.' The structure is stored in the tone, so finding the
tone *is* the planning — which is why she can plan without a plan. This maps onto a measured
psychological variable rather than mere taste. Galbraith's research on self-monitoring found that
low self-monitors generate more genuinely new ideas by producing prose than by making notes during
planning, and that only their new content is associated with growth in the writer's own
understanding; high self-monitors, oriented to rhetorical goals, do better with explicit planning.
Outlining is not neutral advice: for a low self-monitor it removes the machinery that generates the
ideas. This is the single most consequential fact in this whole lens, because the standard advice
('always outline') is a real intervention with a real cost applied indiscriminately.

**Swoopers and bashers: the same split at sentence scale** · <sub>useful · Kurt Vonnegut, Timequake (1997), ch. 35.</sub>

Vonnegut, Timequake ch. 35: 'Tellers of stories with ink on paper... have been either swoopers or
bashers. Swoopers write a story quickly, higgledy-piggledy, crinkum-crankum, any which way. Then
they go over it again painstakingly, fixing everything that is just plain awful or doesn't work.
Bashers go one sentence at a time, getting it exactly right before they go on to the next one. When
they're done they're done.' (His accompanying claim that women swoop and men bash is unsupported and
should be discarded.)

*Mechanism:* The distinction is about where you pay the quality cost. Bashing is only economical if your first-
pass quality bar is genuinely near-final, because you pay full polish cost on every sentence
including the ones you will later cut — and in a discovery draft you will cut 20-40% of scenes.
Swooping is only economical if you can tolerate holding a bad-looking artifact for months without
abandoning it, which is an affective capacity, not a technical one. The practical rule that falls
out: bash only if you also plan tightly, so the sentences you polish are sentences that survive. A
basher who also discovers is Zadie Smith spending two years on twenty pages — which she can afford
and most cannot.

**Write in order to protect the reader's epistemic state; write out of order to protect momentum** · <sub>load-bearing · Vladimir Nabokov, Paris Review, The Art of Fiction No. 40 (1967), interviewed by Herbert Gold; Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008).</sub>

Two genuinely different drafting orders with different payoffs. In-order (Smith, King): first
sentence to last. Out-of-order (Nabokov, most Macro Planners): 'The pattern of the thing precedes
the thing. I fill in the gaps of the crossword at any spot I happen to choose. These bits I write on
index cards until the novel is done.' The decision rule: write out of order if you plan; write in
order if you discover.

*Mechanism:* In-order drafting keeps the writer inside the reader's epistemic state. You know only what the
reader knows at that point, so withholding, surprise, dramatic irony and escalation calibrate
themselves against your own live ignorance rather than against an analytic reconstruction of it.
Causality also accumulates correctly: each scene inherits the full state of everything before it, so
consequences compound rather than being retrofitted. Out-of-order drafting buys two things: you
write only when hot on a given scene (avoiding the low-yield hours), and you write toward known
destinations, which raises per-session output. It costs reader-simulation — you must audit for what
the reader knows rather than feel it — and continuity errors become a QA problem rather than an
impossibility.

**Index cards as the unit of composition** · <sub>useful · Vladimir Nabokov, Paris Review No. 40 (1967) and Playboy interview (1964), collected in Strong Opinions (1973).</sub>

Nabokov composed on lined Bristol cards with pencils capped with erasers, one passage per card,
written in whatever order he chose, numbered and sequenced at the end. The card is simultaneously a
drafting unit, a structural token, and a length constraint.

*Mechanism:* Externalizing structure into physically manipulable objects offloads the working-memory cost of
holding order, which is the cost that makes structural revision feel impossible in a linear file.
When reordering is cheap, structural experiment becomes thinkable — you will try arrangements you
would never try if trying meant a day of cutting and pasting. The card's fixed size also imposes an
unavoidable leap discipline: you cannot ramble past the edge. Important caveat: Nabokov's
accompanying claim that the book was complete in his mind before writing is the least trustworthy
self-report in the canon — the same man wrote 'I have rewritten — often several times — every word I
have ever published. My pencils outlast their erasers,' and the Lolita card archive is heavily
revised. Take the cards; discard the omniscience.

**Scaffolding: build an arbitrary structure to make the work finite, then dismantle it** · <sub>strong · Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008), section 5, 'Dismantling the Scaffolding.'</sub>

Smith: 'When building a novel you will use a lot of scaffolding. Some of this is necessary to hold
the thing up, but a lot isn't. The majority of it is only there to make you feel secure.' Her
examples of scaffolding are frankly arbitrary — three sections of ten chapters, model each chapter
on the books of the prophets, or on the divisions of the Bhagavad-Gita, or the songs of Public
Enemy, or the twenty-seven speeches Donald Rumsfeld gave to the press corps. 'I use scaffolding to
hold up my confidence when I have none, to reduce the despair, and to feel that what I'm doing has a
goal, some endpoint that I can see.' And: 'don't forget to dismantle it later.'

*Mechanism:* An arbitrary constraint converts an infinite search space into a finite one, and constraint
satisfaction is tractable where open generation is not — this is the largest single driver of
generative fluency and it does not require the constraint to be *correct*, only binding. Second, the
scaffold provides progress legibility: with thirty numbered slots you can see how far you are, and
visible progress sustains effort where a featureless expanse does not. The reason it must come down
is that the scaffold optimizes for the writer's morale, not the reader's experience — a novel whose
chapters match the Psalms because the author needed thirty compartments will show seams the reader
has no reason to accept. Smith's Zeno joke is the honest cost: dividing the journey to make it feel
finite also infinitely extends it.

**The middle is fast, not slow — protect the state** · <sub>strong · Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008), section 4, 'Middle-of-the-Novel Magical Thinking.'</sub>

Smith: 'In the middle of a novel, a kind of magical thinking takes over... By middle of the novel I
mean whatever page you are on when you stop being part of your household... when there is nothing in
the world except your book... The middle of a novel is a state of mind. Strange things happen in it.
Time collapses. I sit down to write at 9 a.m., I blink, the evening news is on and I've written four
thousand words, more words than I wrote in three long months, a year ago.' Operationally: when you
detect the state, cancel everything — travel, teaching, new projects, meetings — and do not make
scope or schedule decisions while inside it.

*Mechanism:* Once the situation model is fully constructed, retrieval becomes cheap and nearly all cognitive
resources go to production rather than reconstruction — which is why the rate multiplies by an order
of magnitude and why interruption is disproportionately expensive here specifically. The
accompanying pathology Smith describes ('everything — I mean, everything — flows freely into your
novel'; every story in the paper is relevant) is priming and the frequency illusion, not evidence:
the book is now the dominant retrieval cue, so everything gets encoded through it. This is exactly
why mid-novel judgments about publication dates, scope, and genius should be distrusted. Note that
this contradicts the received wisdom about the 'sagging middle,' which is a problem in the
*manuscript's* middle for the reader — a completely different problem that shares a name and gets
treated with the same wrong advice.

**Skip the scene that beats you; leave a marker and go on** · <sub>strong · John Steinbeck's six tips on writing (commonly reproduced from a 1963 letter, republished by The Paris Review); the [TK] convention is standard magazine and newspaper practice.</sub>

Steinbeck's rule (from his often-reprinted six tips, given in a 1963 letter and circulated via The
Paris Review): if a scene gets the better of you and you cannot cut it, bypass it and go on; when
the whole is finished, come back. The journalist's convention supplies the notation: bracket a
placeholder — [TK], [describe the funeral], [check the year] — and keep moving. Never leave the
chair to research mid-session.

*Mechanism:* Flow states are expensive to enter and cheap to lose; a lookup is a context switch that costs the
reload of the entire situation model for the sake of a fact you can supply in ten seconds during
revision. More importantly, a scene that will not come is usually not a local problem — it is stuck
because of a structural decision made earlier that has not yet declared itself, and the information
required to write it lives in pages you have not written. Deferring converts an unsolvable local
problem into a solvable global one. The placeholder is doing real cognitive work: it discharges the
open loop so the sentence does not keep re-presenting itself, and it makes the deferral auditable
later.

**Write to one named person** · <sub>strong · John Steinbeck (six tips, 1963); Stephen King, On Writing (2000), on the Ideal Reader.</sub>

Steinbeck: forget the generalized audience and pick one person, real and known, and write to them.
King's version is the Ideal Reader — for him, his wife Tabitha — and the operational move is to ask,
at a specific moment in a specific paragraph, whether she laughs here, whether she is bored here,
whether she has guessed.

*Mechanism:* An abstract audience cannot adjudicate a specific choice, because 'readers' have no opinions and no
memory — asking what readers will think of a sentence returns nothing. A specific person does have
retrievable reactions: you have watched them read, laugh, put a book down. Simulating one known mind
is cheap retrieval; modeling a distribution is expensive and produces averaged, characterless
decisions. This also solves a targeting problem: prose aimed at everyone is aimed at the median, and
the median has no taste. Aiming at one person with taste produces work that other people with taste
can find.

**Second draft equals first draft minus ten percent** · <sub>strong · Stephen King, On Writing (2000); Arthur Quiller-Couch, On the Art of Writing (1916), lectures delivered at Cambridge 1913-14.</sub>

King received this on a rejection slip in the spring of 1966 at Lisbon High: 'Not bad, but PUFFY.
You need to revise for length. Formula: 2nd Draft = 1st Draft – 10%. Good luck.' He copied it onto
shirt cardboard and taped it beside the typewriter. His gloss: if you cannot get ten percent out
while retaining the basic story and flavor, you are not trying very hard.

*Mechanism:* An arbitrary, content-blind quota changes the *type* of decision you are making. Asked sentence by
sentence 'is this necessary?', a writer defends every sentence locally and cuts nothing, because
every sentence has a reason. Asked to remove ten percent, the writer is forced into a global
allocation problem — comparing this scene against that one — and humans are far better at ranked
comparison than at absolute judgment. The categories that reliably go are also predictable: throat-
clearing at the top of scenes, beats that restate the previous beat, and subtext that has been
explained. Related and better-attributed than usually thought: 'Whenever you feel an impulse to
perpetrate a piece of exceptionally fine writing, obey it — whole-heartedly — and delete it before
sending your manuscript to press. Murder your darlings' is Arthur Quiller-Couch, not Faulkner or
Hemingway — and note that he says to *write* it first, then delete. Writing the darling discharges
the impulse; the common paraphrase deletes the generative half of the instruction.

**Crowding and leaping** · <sub>strong · Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft (1998; revised 2015), chapter on Crowding and Leaping.</sub>

Le Guin's chapter in Steering the Craft. Crowding: keep the story full of what is happening in it,
dense with exact sensory and connotative detail — she invokes Keats's 'load every rift with ore.'
Leaping: 'What you leap over is what you leave out, and what you leave out is infinitely more
important than what you leave in.' Operationally, the draft-level move is to alternate — write the
two paragraphs that matter at full density, then cut across days, weeks or a room without
transition.

*Mechanism:* Reader immersion is built from concrete perceptual detail because concrete language recruits
perceptual simulation in a way abstraction does not. But attention is a fixed budget, and uniform
density flattens salience: if every moment is rendered at the same resolution, the reader has no
signal about what matters and disengages. Alternating high density with elision produces what
readers experience as pace — pace is not speed, it is a variance structure. The leap also recruits
the reader as a collaborator: crossing a gap requires inference, and inference produces engagement,
which is why the leapt-over material does more work than the rendered material.

**The drawer: mandatory forgetting before revision** · <sub>load-bearing · Stephen King, On Writing (2000); Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008), section 8, 'Step Away from the Vehicle.'</sub>

King's minimum is six weeks in a drawer before the second draft; Smith's counsel is longer — 'When
you finish your novel... put it in a drawer. For as long as you can manage. A year or more is ideal'
— and her diagnosis is exact: 'The secret to editing your work is simple: you need to become its
reader instead of its writer.' She names the perfect state as 'two years after it's published, ten
minutes before you go on stage at a literary festival,' when 'every redundant phrase, each show-off,
pointless metaphor, all the pieces of dead wood, stupidity, vanity, and tedium are distressingly
obvious to you.' She reports asking Alan Hollinghurst how The Line of Beauty got so good; he said:
'Oh, I left it for a long while. And then I tinkered with it. Five years, actually.'

*Mechanism:* While the draft is recent you do not read your text — you retrieve your intention, and the memory of
what you meant silently fills every gap on the page. This is not a failure of attention; it is
source confusion, and no amount of care defeats it, which is why professional editors also stop
being able to see a manuscript after enough passes ('after they've read a manuscript multiple times,
they stop being able to see it'). Forgetting is the instrument: the discrepancy between text and
intention only becomes perceptible once the intention has decayed enough that you must reconstruct
it from the words actually present. This is also why 'I'll just be really careful' is not a
substitute — the error is invisible from inside by construction.

**Manufacture strangeness when you cannot afford the drawer** · <sub>strong · Ursula K. Le Guin, Steering the Craft (1998/2015); Anthony Trollope, An Autobiography (1883); standard professional practice.</sub>

Change the perceptual channel to defeat your own predictive reading: read the manuscript aloud (Le
Guin's standing insistence; Trollope's half-hour), print it, change the typeface and line length,
read it on a device you never draft on, have a text-to-speech voice read it to you, read scenes in
reverse order.

*Mechanism:* Reading your own draft is overwhelmingly top-down: you predict the next words and your prediction is
correct, so you never process the actual surface. Every change to the physical presentation degrades
the prediction and forces bottom-up processing, which is the only mode in which you can see what is
on the page rather than what you meant. Reading aloud is the strongest version because it adds an
independent error channel — the mouth stumbles on clumsy rhythm, the ear catches repetition, and
neither can be talked out of it by your knowledge of your own intentions. Reverse order defeats
narrative momentum, which is the specific illusion that makes a weak scene feel adequate because the
previous one carried it.

**Cap the draft's calendar duration, not just its word count** · <sub>strong · Stephen King, On Writing (2000); corroborating observation from Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008), section 2.</sub>

King's rule: the first draft of a book, even a long one, should take no more than three months —
roughly a season — which at his 2,000 words a day yields a very long novel. The constraint is on
elapsed time, not on daily output; a writer producing 800 words a day should cap the *book*, not
raise the quota.

*Mechanism:* A writer's aesthetic drifts faster than a long draft can be completed. A first draft that takes two
years is stratigraphy — layers deposited by different writers with different sentence preferences,
different obsessions, and different beliefs about what the book is — and the seams cannot be sanded
out in revision because there is no single intention to revise toward. Zadie Smith's remark that
'the excitement of a new novel lies almost entirely in the repudiation of the one I wrote before'
and the Portuguese novelist who begged her not to read his first book are the same phenomenon on a
longer timescale: writers repudiate their past selves reliably and fast. The season cap is a device
for keeping one self in charge of one book.

**Draft when foggy, edit when sharp** · <sub>contested · Dorothea Brande, Becoming a Writer (1934); Julia Cameron, The Artist's Way (1992); Wieth, M. B. and Zacks, R. T., 'Time of day effects on problem solving: When the non-optimal is optimal,' Thinking & Reasoning 17(4):387-401 (2011).</sub>

The tradition says write first thing in the morning before the censor wakes — Dorothea Brande
(Becoming a Writer, 1934) told writers to write immediately on waking, before speaking to anyone;
Julia Cameron's morning pages (The Artist's Way, 1992) are three longhand pages first thing, never
reread. The defensible operational version is broader: schedule generative drafting at your low-
alertness hours and line editing at your peak hours, whichever those are.

*Mechanism:* The folk mechanism (purity, proximity to dreams) is weak. The measured one is better and stranger:
Wieth and Zacks found that insight problems are solved *more* successfully at a person's non-optimal
time of day, while analytic problems are solved better at the optimal time. The account is reduced
inhibitory control — when you are slightly foggy, weakly-activated and apparently irrelevant
associations are not filtered out, which is precisely what insight requires and precisely what
careful analytic work does not. So a night owl should draft in the morning and edit at night; most
writers, following the received advice, arrange the exact inverse of what suits them. Brande's
second and less famous exercise — write at an arbitrary appointed hour purely to prove you can
produce on demand — has the stronger mechanism of the two: it builds a conditioned cue so writing
becomes stimulus-controlled rather than mood-controlled, which is exactly the difference Boice
measured.

**Curate what you read during the draft — or read nothing** · <sub>useful · Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008), section 3, 'Other People's Words, Part Two.'</sub>

Two defensible opposite policies. Smith reads constantly and instrumentally: 'My writing desk is
covered in open novels. I read lines to swim in a certain sensibility, to strike a particular note,
to encourage rigor when I'm too sentimental, to bring verbal ease when I'm syntactically uptight...
if my sentences are baggy, too baroque, I cut back on fatty Foster Wallace, say, and pick up Kafka,
as roughage.' Her single absolute rule: 'When I'm writing the first hundred pages of a book, I can
eat crap, watch crap, listen to crap, talk crap, and be crap, but I can't read crap.' The other camp
reads no fiction at all while drafting.

*Mechanism:* Syntactic and prosodic priming is real, fast, and largely involuntary: whatever you have read
recently measurably shifts your sentence-length distribution, subordination depth, and register
within hours. Smith's policy exploits the mechanism as a corrective steering input — read against
your current drift. The abstainers are defending against the identical mechanism from the other
side, plus a second real cost (the demoralization of measuring an unfinished draft against a
finished masterpiece). Both are coherent; what is incoherent is reading indiscriminately, which is
why Smith's one hard rule is about quality rather than quantity. Her deeper argument is E. M.
Forster's literary echo chamber and Keats as the model of the writer who 'never feared influence —
he devoured influences.'

**Start the next book before you finish celebrating the last** · <sub>useful · Anthony Trollope, An Autobiography (1883), ch. XVII; contrast in Zadie Smith, 'That Crafty Feeling' (2008), section 7, 'The Last Day.'</sub>

Trollope: 'A shoemaker when he has finished one pair of shoes does not sit down and contemplate his
work in idle satisfaction. "There is my pair of shoes finished at last! What a pair of shoes it is!"
The shoemaker who so indulged himself would be without wages half his time... I had now quite
accustomed myself to begin a second pair as soon as the first was out of my hands.' The
counterweight is Smith's account of the last day — 'a feeling of happiness that knocks me clean out
of adjectives' — which is real and should be taken; the rule is about the week after, not the day
of.

*Mechanism:* The post-completion trough is the point at which identity attaches to the finished object rather
than to the practice, and identity attached to an object is fragile — it is now hostage to reviews,
sales, and rereading. Starting the next work relocates identity to the process. Second and more
concretely: the largest cost in the productivity data is re-entry after a lapse, because every lapse
forces the reconstruction of habit, cue, and confidence from scratch. Trollope's discipline keeps
the conditioned writing cue continuously alive; the writer who takes six months off after a book
pays the full unblocking cost again.

### Non-obvious findings — Drafting practice and the writer's working day: ho

- The empirical foundation of 'write every day' is far thinner than universally assumed. Boice's
  famous 1983 study had nine subjects per cell, all self-identified procrastinators from campuses
  around Albany, New York, 15 of the original 42 dropped out (nearly all citing 'the nuisance of
  charting'), and it has never been replicated. Helen Sword's survey of 1,223 academics plus 100
  peer-nominated 'exemplary writers' across 45 universities found that only 11.5% and 12.8%
  respectively write, or aspire to write, every weekday — roughly seven out of eight successful
  academic writers do not write daily. Daily writing is neither a reliable marker nor a predictor of
  productivity. What Boice actually demonstrated is that scheduled sessions beat mood-contingent
  ones, which is a different and defensible claim.

- Krashen's re-analysis of Boice's own data overturns the usual reading. The coerced group (donate $15
  to a hated organization for any miss) produced 3.2 pages/day and 1.39 creative ideas/day, but
  their ideas *per page* fell to 0.43, while the spontaneous and merely-regular groups both sat at
  about 0.8 ideas per page. Coercion nearly halves inventiveness per unit of output. The regular-
  schedule group — triple the spontaneous output at undiminished idea density — is the actual
  optimum in the data, not the punished group that everyone cites.

- The standard justification for Hemingway's stop-mid-sentence rule is wrong. The Zeigarnik effect
  (better recall for interrupted tasks) largely fails to replicate; a 2025 meta-analysis concluded
  the Zeigarnik effect 'lacks universal validity' while the Ovsiankina effect — the urge to *resume*
  an interrupted task — is the general tendency. The trick therefore works motivationally, not
  mnemonically: it makes tomorrow's start attractive rather than helping you remember. That
  distinction is actionable — writing yourself a note about the next scene does not substitute for
  the unfinished sentence, because the note closes the loop the interruption was meant to keep open.

- Zadie Smith's Macro Planner / Micro Manager split is not temperament dressed up. It corresponds to a
  measured psychological variable: Galbraith's research on self-monitoring found that low self-
  monitors generate more genuinely new ideas by producing continuous prose than by making planning
  notes, and that only their new content is associated with growth in the writer's own
  understanding, while high self-monitors benefit from explicit rhetorical planning. Telling a
  discovery writer to outline is not neutral advice — it removes the machinery that generates their
  ideas. The near-universal 'always outline' prescription is an untargeted intervention with a real
  cost for a large fraction of writers.

- Insight problems are solved better at your *non-optimal* time of day; analytic problems better at
  your optimal time (Wieth and Zacks, 2011). The mechanism is reduced inhibitory control letting
  weakly-activated associations through. The morning-pages tradition is right about the fog and
  wrong about the reason, and the practical implication inverts common practice: draft when slightly
  foggy, line-edit when sharp. A night owl should draft in the morning and edit at night, not the
  reverse — yet almost everyone schedules their hardest generative work at peak alertness and their
  editing when tired.

- There is measured support for the folk taboo on describing your unwritten novel. Gollwitzer,
  Sheeran, Michalski and Seifert (Psychological Science, 2009) found that when an identity-relevant
  intention is noticed by others, the social recognition partially substitutes for the act and
  reduces subsequent effort. Describing your novel well at dinner delivers a portion of the reward
  of having written it. King's 'write with the door closed' has a mechanism, not just a mood.

- Zadie Smith reworked the first twenty pages of On Beauty for almost two years, then wrote the rest
  of the book in five months. The ratio is the finding. Her explanation is that for a tone-first
  writer the opening *is* the plan: 'I think worrying over the first twenty pages is my way of
  working on the whole novel, my way of finding its structure, its plot, its characters — all of
  which, for a Micro Manager, are contained in the sensibility of a sentence.' A stalled opening in
  such a writer may be planning in progress, not procrastination — but only if the acceleration
  actually arrives.

- Trollope's actual system is more sophisticated than the legend. The famous 250-words-per-quarter-
  hour is real, but the operative instrument was a per-book diary of *weekly* page quotas (average
  40 pages of 250 words, ranging 20 to 112), so a bad day was recoverable within the week without
  moral collapse. And he opened every session by reading the previous day's work aloud for half an
  hour — one sixth of his working time spent rereading, which directly contradicts the modern 'never
  look back' advice. The reconcilable distinction is bounded re-entry (yesterday, aloud, for tone)
  versus unbounded rereading (page one, silently, for reassurance).

- 'Kill your darlings' is Arthur Quiller-Couch (On the Art of Writing, 1916), not Faulkner, Hemingway,
  Wilde, King or Fitzgerald — and the original instruction is materially different from the
  paraphrase: 'Whenever you feel an impulse to perpetrate a piece of exceptionally fine writing,
  obey it — whole-heartedly — and delete it before sending your manuscript to press.' Obey it first.
  The writing of the darling is not waste; it discharges an impulse that would otherwise keep
  interrupting. The popular shorthand deletes the generative half of the rule and turns a two-stage
  process into pure self-denial.

- Nabokov's claim that the novel is complete in his mind before he writes it is the least trustworthy
  practitioner self-report in the canon, and the same interview subject supplies the refutation: 'I
  have rewritten — often several times — every word I have ever published. My pencils outlast their
  erasers.' The Lolita card archive is heavily revised. The cards are a genuinely powerful tool; the
  omniscience is a rhetorical posture. This is the general warning: writers' accounts of their own
  process systematically overstate control and understate flailing, because the account is itself a
  performance of authorial mastery.

- Hemingway's 'stop while going good' is almost always reported as a productivity technique, but his
  stated reason was anti-productivity: 'never to empty the well of my writing, but always to stop
  when there was still something there in the deep part of the well, and let it refill at night from
  the springs that fed it.' It is deliberate daily under-production to protect capacity. Any writer
  who combines a daily quota with 'write until you are empty' is running the exact inverse of the
  method and will pay for it in cold starts.

- The 'sagging middle' conflates two unrelated problems that happen to share a word. The middle of the
  *manuscript* often sags for the reader — a structural problem. The middle of the *drafting
  process* is where the rate multiplies, not collapses: Smith reports going from three months of
  struggle to four thousand words between blinking, because the situation model is finally fully
  built and retrieval has become cheap. Advice aimed at one is routinely applied to the other. A
  writer whose drafting middle is slow does not have a sagging-middle problem; they have not yet
  built the model.

- Incubation has hard moderators that make 'take a break' either effective or useless. Sio and
  Ormerod's meta-analysis of 117 studies found that low-cognitive-demand interpolated activity
  outperforms both rest and demanding activity for divergent problems, and that longer preparation
  before the break produces a larger effect. That means: a walk beats a nap and beats email, and you
  must first be genuinely, specifically stuck for the break to pay anything at all. 'Step away when
  it isn't working' is not the same instruction as 'step away after two hours of concentrated
  failure on a well-specified problem,' and only the second one is supported.

- Even professional editors go blind to a manuscript, and Smith says so plainly: 'after they've read a
  manuscript multiple times, they stop being able to see it.' The drawer is not a writer's weakness
  being compensated for — it is a general property of repeated exposure. This is why 'I'll just read
  it more carefully' is structurally incapable of substituting for time or for a genuinely fresh
  reader; the error is invisible from inside by construction, not by inattention.
